This allows the key/query transformation between sequence elements to be a function of both their relative position and the input data, increasing expressivity. The authors develop an efficient parallel training algorithm that exploits a compact representation of Householder matrices and a FlashAttention-style blockwise algorithm to minimize I/O costs. They demonstrate PaTH's superior performance compared to RoPE and other baselines on synthetic state-tracking tasks and real-world language modeling experiments. The paper also introduces PaTH-FoX, a combination of PaTH with the Forgetting Transformer, showing further performance improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using accumulated data-dependent Householder transformations for position encoding is a significant departure from existing approaches like RoPE and learned absolute position embeddings. The paper effectively addresses a key limitation of RoPE, namely its data-independence, thereby enhancing the expressiveness of transformers. The integration of this concept with FlashAttention and the UT transform to achieve efficient parallel training demonstrates a practical and well-engineered contribution. The combination of PaTH with the Forgetting Transformer (FoX) is a relatively simple extension, but its empirical impact adds value.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a fundamental issue in transformer architecture: the limitations of existing position encoding schemes. By making positional information data-dependent, PaTH has the potential to improve the ability of transformers to model sequential dependencies and solve tasks requiring state tracking and long-range reasoning. The empirical results, demonstrating PaTH's superiority on challenging synthetic tasks and competitive performance on language modeling benchmarks, highlight its potential impact on the field. Moreover, the demonstration of solving an NC1-complete problem, combined with flash attention efficiency, marks a step towards more theoretically grounded and practically applicable efficient architectures.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper presents a well-motivated and theoretically sound approach to position encoding.
    *   The proposed PaTH mechanism is computationally efficient, leveraging existing techniques like FlashAttention and UT transform.
    *   The empirical evaluation is thorough and covers a range of tasks, including synthetic benchmarks and real-world language modeling.
    *   The authors provide an efficient implementation and make the code publicly available, facilitating further research and adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the language modeling results are promising, the improvements over RoPE are not dramatic and could be due to a slightly larger effective model size due to additional parameters of PaTH attention. A more in-depth ablation study analyzing the effect of parameter count on the performance improvement would be beneficial.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational overhead of PaTH compared to RoPE in different scenarios. The current speed comparison in Figure 1 is helpful, but a breakdown of the computational cost of the Householder transformations and masking operations would be valuable.
    *   The paper could be strengthened by including experiments with other related attention mechanism enhancements.

*   **Potential Influence:** PaTH has the potential to influence the design of future transformer architectures by offering a more expressive and data-dependent approach to position encoding. Its ability to solve challenging synthetic tasks could lead to improvements in real-world applications requiring complex sequential reasoning. The efficient training algorithm and code availability will likely encourage further research and adoption of PaTH in the LLM community.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the PaTH approach is undeniably innovative and demonstrates promising results, the empirical gains on language modeling, though positive, could be improved. The paper's strength lies in its theoretical grounding and potential to overcome fundamental limitations of RoPE while maintaining a computationally efficient framework through leveraging techniques like FlashAttention. Additionally, it remains unclear whether the slightly larger model size for PaTH truly makes the approach favorable. However, its strong performance on state tracking tasks and promise of scaling LLMs with significantly long context windows adds practical value.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Semantic Pivots Enable Cross-Lingual Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16385v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyu He, Tong Zhou, Yubo Chen, Delai Qiu, Shengping Liu, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable ability in cross-lingual tasks. Understanding how LLMs acquire this ability is crucial for their interpretability. To quantify the cross-lingual ability of LLMs accurately, we propose a Word-Level Cross-Lingual Translation Task. To find how LLMs learn cross-lingual ability, we trace the outputs of LLMs' intermediate layers in the word translation task. We identify and distinguish two distinct behaviors in the forward pass of LLMs: co-occurrence behavior and semantic pivot behavior. We attribute LLMs' two distinct behaviors to the co-occurrence frequency of words and find the semantic pivot from the pre-training dataset. Finally, to apply our findings to improve the cross-lingual ability of LLMs, we reconstruct a semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset using documents with a high proportion of semantic pivots. Our experiments validate the effectiveness of our approach in enhancing cross-lingual ability. Our research contributes insights into the interpretability of LLMs and offers a method for improving LLMs' cross-lingual ability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper "Semantic Pivots Enable Cross-Lingual Transfer in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve cross-lingual transfer, aiming to improve interpretability and performance. It proposes a Word-Level Cross-Lingual Translation Dataset (CLWTD) and a continuous metric to quantify cross-lingual abilities. Through analysis of LLMs' intermediate layers, the paper identifies two distinct behaviors: "co-occurrence behavior" (direct inference from input) and "semantic pivot behavior" (reasoning through a semantic pivot). They attribute these behaviors to the co-occurrence frequency of words in the pre-training data.  Finally, the paper proposes a method to enhance cross-lingual ability by reconstructing a pre-training dataset enriched with documents containing a high proportion of semantic pivots.  Experiments on a 1B parameter OLMO model show improved cross-lingual ability using this approach.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Interpretability Focus:**  The paper tackles a crucial aspect of LLMs - understanding *how* they achieve cross-lingual transfer, not just whether they do.  This is vital for building trust and enabling targeted improvements.
    *   **Novel Methodology:** The word-level translation task and associated continuous metric (CLWTD) offer a finer-grained assessment than typical sentence-level machine translation benchmarks. This addresses a key limitation in previous research, particularly for assessing early checkpoints or models with limited capabilities.
    *   **Clear Identification of Behaviors:** Distinguishing between co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors is a useful abstraction for understanding the internal workings of LLMs.
    *   **Data-Driven Analysis:** Linking these behaviors to the co-occurrence frequency in pre-training data provides a tangible connection between data and model behavior, enabling targeted data-centric interventions.
    *   **Practical Application:**  The idea of reconstructing a pre-training dataset based on semantic pivots is a valuable approach, although limited by resources.
    * The paper clearly motivates each step, providing a compelling narrative about the model's cross-lingual process.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Size:** The experimental evaluation is primarily limited to a relatively small (1B parameter) OLMO model. While the findings are insightful, it's unclear how well they generalize to larger, more complex LLMs like Llama-3 or GPT-4. The paper acknowledges this as a limitation.
    *   **Dataset Scalability:** The construction of CLWTD, while offering fine-grained evaluation, is likely time-consuming and may face scalability challenges for significantly larger vocabularies or more languages.
    *   **Semantic Pivot Selection Heuristic:**  The method for selecting semantic pivots relies on frequency and prompting DeepSeek.  While practical, it could be sensitive to the quality of DeepSeek's responses and might miss less frequent but more semantically relevant pivots. The process could be more robust with additional analysis or verification steps.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the paper reports improvements, the magnitude of the gains (0.013 over the original model) is relatively modest. This raises questions about the cost-benefit ratio of the dataset reconstruction approach, especially for production-scale pre-training.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-existing Tools:**  The study relies heavily on existing interpretability tools (logit lens, WIMBD, Infini-gram), without providing novel insights into or enhancements of these tools themselves.  While leveraging these tools is efficient, it reduces the standalone methodological contribution of the paper.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:** While the paper quantifies overall improvements, it lacks a detailed error analysis of the improved model. Understanding *which* kinds of translations are improved by the semantic pivot approach would be valuable. The paper could address how semantic pivot improves particular types of words.
    *   The connection between high co-occurrence with the source language and low co-occurrence with the target language for semantic pivots is less clear. Is a lack of direct association beneficial in some way?

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper demonstrates some novelty in the fine-grained word-level analysis, the identification of co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors, and the data-centric reconstruction strategy. The paper's approach to link intermediate states to pre-training documents is relatively novel.
    *   The impact of the findings is currently limited by the scale of the experiments. However, the concepts introduced could influence future research on improving cross-lingual transfer in larger LLMs. The interpretability aspect is a significant contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers valuable insights into how LLMs learn cross-lingual abilities by linking internal mechanisms to pre-training data statistics. The methodological approach is novel, and the findings are potentially useful for data-centric improvements. However, the limited scale of the experiments, the incremental improvement, and the reliance on existing tools reduce the overall impact of the paper.

Score: 6

The paper presents interesting findings and a reasonable approach, but its limited scale and incremental gains temper the degree of significant contribution. A larger scale, a more in-depth error analysis, or more compelling improvements in cross-lingual transfer would significantly boost the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Resource for Error Analysis in Text Simplification: New Taxonomy and Test Collection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16392v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Vendeville, Liana Ermakova, Pierre De Loor
- **Abstract**: The general public often encounters complex texts but does not have the time or expertise to fully understand them, leading to the spread of misinformation. Automatic Text Simplification (ATS) helps make information more accessible, but its evaluation methods have not kept up with advances in text generation, especially with Large Language Models (LLMs). In particular, recent studies have shown that current ATS metrics do not correlate with the presence of errors. Manual inspections have further revealed a variety of errors, underscoring the need for a more nuanced evaluation framework, which is currently lacking. This resource paper addresses this gap by introducing a test collection for detecting and classifying errors in simplified texts. First, we propose a taxonomy of errors, with a formal focus on information distortion. Next, we introduce a parallel dataset of automatically simplified scientific texts. This dataset has been human-annotated with labels based on our proposed taxonomy. Finally, we analyze the quality of the dataset, and we study the performance of existing models to detect and classify errors from that taxonomy. These contributions give researchers the tools to better evaluate errors in ATS, develop more reliable models, and ultimately improve the quality of automatically simplified texts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current Automatic Text Simplification (ATS) evaluation methods, particularly concerning error detection.  It introduces a novel taxonomy of errors in simplified texts, focusing on information distortion.  The authors also present a manually annotated parallel dataset of automatically simplified scientific texts, using their proposed taxonomy. They analyze the dataset's quality and evaluate the performance of existing error detection models on it, aiming to provide researchers with tools for better ATS evaluation and model development. The taxonomy consists of four main error categories: fluency, alignment, information and simplification, each with specific sub-types.  The authors argue that current ATS metrics fail to adequately capture the range of errors introduced by Large Language Models (LLMs), thus motivating the need for a more nuanced evaluation framework. They showcase error detection methods for identifying and classifying errors using their test collection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the **structured taxonomy of errors** specific to text simplification, with a detailed focus on information distortion, a aspect not often highlighted by ATS evaluation. While prior work exists on error analysis, the explicit focus on information distortion, and the structured taxonomy are valuable contributions. The annotated dataset, based on this taxonomy, adds to the novelty by providing a concrete resource for future research. The explicit distinction of Factuality hallucination and Faithfulness hallucinations is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the field of ATS. Current evaluation metrics often fail to correlate well with human perceptions of simplification quality, and manual inspections reveal a range of errors missed by these metrics. By providing a more nuanced error taxonomy and a test collection, this paper enables researchers to:

    *   **Evaluate ATS systems more effectively:** The taxonomy helps to identify and categorize different types of errors, allowing for a more granular assessment of system performance.

    *   **Develop better error detection models:** The annotated dataset provides a valuable resource for training and evaluating error detection models.

    *   **Improve the quality of automatically simplified texts:** By better understanding the types of errors that ATS systems make, researchers can develop models that are less prone to these errors.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Taxonomy:** The hierarchical taxonomy is well-structured and provides a comprehensive framework for classifying errors in simplified texts.
    *   **Emphasis on Information Distortion:** The focus on information distortion is crucial, as it directly impacts the accuracy and reliability of simplified texts.
    *   **Manually Annotated Dataset:** The availability of a manually annotated dataset is a significant strength, as it provides a ground truth for training and evaluating error detection models.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world problem and provides practical tools and resources that can be used by researchers and practitioners.
     * Provides practical implications that are potentially transformative for future models in ATS.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Inter-Annotator Agreement:** The inter-annotator agreement could be considered a point of weakness. Though discussed in detail, the variability in consistency rates, especially for annotator C, raises some concerns about the dataset's reliability (particularly since the author chose to retain the data).  More refined annotation guidelines and annotator training could address this in future iterations.

    *   **Scope of Showcase:** The analysis of existing error detection models, while informative, is somewhat limited in scope. It primarily focuses on hallucination detection models, leaving other error types less thoroughly explored.
    *   **Subjectivity in Simplification Errors:** As acknowledged in the paper, what constitutes an error related to simplification (e.g., loss of informative content) can be subjective and depend on the target audience and simplification goal. While the paper attempts to address this through a formal definition, it still introduces a degree of ambiguity.
    *   **Limited evaluation metrics:** Analysis relies mostly on AUROC and AUPRC. This is standard, but can be expanded in the future.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of ATS by providing a more rigorous and nuanced framework for evaluating system performance. The taxonomy and dataset can be used to develop better error detection models, which can ultimately lead to the creation of higher-quality automatically simplified texts. This improved quality directly impacts accessibility of information for wider audiences.

*The paper contains solid rationale and justifications for its choice in taxonomy and methodology which indicates a higher likelihood of future acceptance in the ATS community.*

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the paper's novelty in providing a structured taxonomy, its significance in addressing a crucial gap in ATS evaluation, the creation of a valuable resource in the form of an annotated dataset, and its potential to influence future research, a score of **7.5** is warranted. The weaknesses, particularly the variability in annotator consistency and the somewhat limited scope of the empirical analysis, prevent it from receiving a higher score. However, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a valuable contribution to the field.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Divide-Fuse-Conquer: Eliciting "Aha Moments" in Multi-Scenario Games
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16401v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoqing Zhang, Huabin Zheng, Ang Lv, Yuhan Liu, Zirui Song, Flood Sung, Xiuying Chen, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have been observed to suddenly exhibit advanced reasoning abilities during reinforcement learning (RL), resembling an ``aha moment'' triggered by simple outcome-based rewards. While RL has proven effective in eliciting such breakthroughs in tasks involving mathematics, coding, and vision, it faces significant challenges in multi-scenario games. The diversity of game rules, interaction modes, and environmental complexities often leads to policies that perform well in one scenario but fail to generalize to others. Simply combining multiple scenarios during training introduces additional challenges, such as training instability and poor performance. To overcome these challenges, we propose Divide-Fuse-Conquer, a framework designed to enhance generalization in multi-scenario RL. This approach starts by heuristically grouping games based on characteristics such as rules and difficulties. Specialized models are then trained for each group to excel at games in the group is what we refer to as the divide step. Next, we fuse model parameters from different groups as a new model, and continue training it for multiple groups, until the scenarios in all groups are conquered. Experiments across 18 TextArena games show that Qwen2.5-32B-Align trained with the Divide-Fuse-Conquer strategy reaches a performance level comparable to Claude3.5, achieving 7 wins and 4 draws. We hope our approach can inspire future research on using reinforcement learning to improve the generalization of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Divide-Fuse-Conquer: Eliciting “Aha Moments" in Multi-Scenario Games":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the "Divide-Fuse-Conquer" (DFC) framework to improve the generalization capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in reinforcement learning (RL) applied to multi-scenario games. The method addresses challenges like training instability and poor generalization when RL agents are trained across diverse game environments. DFC involves: (1) *Divide*: Heuristically grouping games based on characteristics like rules and difficulty. (2) *Fuse*: Merging parameters of specialized models trained on individual groups. (3) *Conquer*: Fine-tuning the fused model on combined groups using game-specific techniques to enhance stability, efficiency, and performance. The techniques include format reward shaping, half-negative sampling, epsilon-greedy disturbance, randomized seed initialization, mixed priority sampling, and a hasty-action penalty. Experimental results on 18 TextArena games using Qwen2.5-32B-Align demonstrate that DFC achieves performance comparable to Claude3.5 and outperforms direct RL application.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The concept of "Divide-Fuse-Conquer" isn't entirely novel in machine learning; however, its specific application to multi-scenario RL for LLMs, along with the suite of game-specific stabilization and optimization techniques, contributes a degree of originality.  The heuristic grouping of games, although intuitive, lacks theoretical justification and relies on empirical observations. The core innovation lies in combining these components in a progressive training framework. While the individual techniques like reward shaping and epsilon-greedy exploration are well-established, their tailored application and combination within DFC for stabilizing and improving LLM-based RL in multi-scenario settings have not been explored.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and important challenge in applying RL to LLMs: generalizing across diverse environments. The empirical results demonstrate tangible improvements in performance and stability, especially in comparison to applying RL directly (Naive-MSRL). The use of TextArena provides a relevant and challenging benchmark. The study offers a systematic exploration of techniques aimed at enhancing stability, efficiency, and performance of multi-scenario RL training. The detailed analysis of model behaviors during training provides insights that can guide future research.  The fact that a 32B parameter model trained with DFC achieves performance comparable to Claude3.5 (likely a much larger model) is noteworthy.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
*   Systematic exploration of a practical solution.
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation using a diverse set of games.
*   Detailed ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of the individual components.
*   Insights into model behavior during training.
*   Solid demonstration of comparable results with leading models on some tasks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The heuristic grouping of games lacks theoretical justification. A more principled approach based on, for instance, task embeddings or meta-learning techniques could have further strengthened the method.
*   Limited theoretical analysis. While empirical results are compelling, the paper lacks formal guarantees on convergence or generalization.
*   Dependency on manually designed reward shaping. Although the rule-based system is presented, it is still inherently hand-engineered, which is always a potential weakness from a generalizability standpoint.
*   The comparisons are against Claude3.5, and lack details about its size or training regime. This comparison, while impressive on its own, doesn't provide a fair baseline.
*   The reliance on TextArena, a text-based platform, while convenient, might limit the generalizability of the findings to other types of game environments or real-world scenarios.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper can influence future research in several ways:

*   It provides a practical framework for applying RL to LLMs in multi-scenario settings.
*   It highlights the importance of game-specific stabilization and optimization techniques.
*   It offers insights into model behavior during training that can inform the design of more effective RL algorithms.
*   It encourages further exploration of LLM-based RL for general-purpose agents capable of operating in diverse environments.

**Score:** 6.5/10

**Justification:**

The paper contributes a practically relevant method to a growing area of research (RL for LLMs). The "Divide-Fuse-Conquer" strategy provides a useful structure for tackling the challenge of generalization in multi-scenario environments. The detailed ablation studies and comparisons against Claude3.5 enhance the paper's value. However, the lack of theoretical justification for the heuristic grouping, the dependence on manually designed reward shaping, and limited discussion of the Claude3.5 comparisons, slightly constrain its overall significance and justify a score on the higher end of a moderate level. There is room for improvement by incorporating more theoretically grounded approaches and demonstrating generalizability beyond the specific TextArena environment.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Surveys to Narratives: Rethinking Cultural Value Adaptation in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16408v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Farid Adilazuarda, Chen Cecilia Liu, Iryna Gurevych, Alham Fikri Aji
- **Abstract**: Adapting cultural values in Large Language Models (LLMs) presents significant challenges, particularly due to biases and limited training data. Prior work primarily aligns LLMs with different cultural values using World Values Survey (WVS) data. However, it remains unclear whether this approach effectively captures cultural nuances or produces distinct cultural representations for various downstream tasks. In this paper, we systematically investigate WVS-based training for cultural value adaptation and find that relying solely on survey data can homogenize cultural norms and interfere with factual knowledge. To investigate these issues, we augment WVS with encyclopedic and scenario-based cultural narratives from Wikipedia and NormAd. While these narratives may have variable effects on downstream tasks, they consistently improve cultural distinctiveness than survey data alone. Our work highlights the inherent complexity of aligning cultural values with the goal of guiding task-specific behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the challenges of adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to reflect different cultural values, particularly using the World Values Survey (WVS) data.  It argues that relying solely on survey data like WVS can lead to homogenization of cultural norms and factual knowledge interference.  The authors explore augmenting WVS data with richer narrative sources from Wikipedia and NormAd (a dataset of cultural norms and situational examples). While these narratives show variable effects on downstream tasks, they consistently improve cultural distinctiveness compared to using WVS data alone. The work highlights the complexity of aligning cultural values to guide task-specific behavior in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem: how to effectively align LLMs with diverse cultural values beyond simply relying on potentially limited survey data.  The exploration of augmenting WVS with encyclopedic and scenario-based narratives is a valuable contribution. It directly challenges the established practice of using only survey data and attempts to move towards more nuanced representations. Prior work has recognized the problem of cultural bias and adapted LLMs. The novel contribution lies in systemically comparing the efficacy of various data types for cultural adaptation beyond standard survey data. **However**, some existing work has already acknowledged the limitations of survey data *in isolation* (see citations in the paper itself), and adding narrative data as an augment is a relatively intuitive approach. The novelty is primarily in the systematic evaluation and comparison of different types of data augmentation on downstream tasks.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for developing more culturally sensitive and globally relevant LLMs. Demonstrating the limitations of WVS alone and showcasing the potential of narrative data helps advance methods for mitigating WEIRD biases. Identifying that factual knowledge can be negatively affected by cultural adaptation highlights the inherent trade-offs. By proposing and employing the cultural distinctiveness metric (C-DIST score), the work offers a quantitative measure for evaluating cultural alignment in LLMs. **However**, the observed improvements in distinctiveness are not always mirrored by improvements in downstream task performance, suggesting further work is needed to guide task-specific behaviour from the adapted cultural norms.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper offers a rigorous and well-designed experimental setup, considering multiple LLMs, diverse linguistic-cultural settings, and various evaluation tasks.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The problem of effective cultural alignment in LLMs is clearly defined and motivated.
    *   **Quantitative Metric:**  The C-DIST score provides a useful measure of cultural distinctiveness, facilitating quantitative comparisons.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings offer practical guidance on data selection and augmentation strategies for cultural value adaptation.
    *   **Thorough Error analysis**:  Thorough invalid example analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Cultural Data:**  While the study explores different data sources, it could benefit from considering more diverse and regionally specific cultural data beyond Wikipedia and NormAd.
    *   **Inconsistent Downstream Task Performance:**  The effects of narrative data on downstream tasks are inconsistent, requiring further investigation to identify the factors driving these variations.
    *   **Lack of Clear Explanation for Knowledge Interference:** The reasons for knowledge interference after adaptation are not fully explored and require a deeper dive.
    *   **Limited Cultural Specificity of Knowledge Interference**: More exploration is needed on if some cultural values adaptation increases factual knowledge or if this only shows cultural adaptation degrades factual knowledge for some cases.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence future research by prompting a shift from reliance on survey data alone to incorporating richer, more contextual narratives. It will also stimulate the development of improved evaluation metrics and strategies for balancing cultural distinctiveness with task performance and factual knowledge retention.
**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 is justified because the paper makes a novel and important contribution by systematically evaluating different data types for cultural adaptation. It moves beyond existing work by demonstrating the limitations of WVS alone and showcasing the promise of narrative data. However, the inconsistent performance on downstream tasks, the need for more in-depth error analysis, and the limited scope of cultural data sources slightly diminish the overall impact. While the findings are valuable, further work is needed to refine the methods and address the identified limitations. A score between 6 and 8 is reasonable here, and leaning toward 7 is a judgement call based on the systematic and well-defined experimentation and C-DIST metric.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tool-Star: Empowering LLM-Brained Multi-Tool Reasoner via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16410v1)
- **Authors**: Guanting Dong, Yifei Chen, Xiaoxi Li, Jiajie Jin, Hongjin Qian, Yutao Zhu, Hangyu Mao, Guorui Zhou, Zhicheng Dou, Ji-Rong Wen
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities via large-scale reinforcement learning (RL). However, leveraging the RL algorithm to empower effective multi-tool collaborative reasoning in LLMs remains an open challenge. In this paper, we introduce Tool-Star, an RL-based framework designed to empower LLMs to autonomously invoke multiple external tools during stepwise reasoning. Tool-Star integrates six types of tools and incorporates systematic designs in both data synthesis and training. To address the scarcity of tool-use data, we propose a general tool-integrated reasoning data synthesis pipeline, which combines tool-integrated prompting with hint-based sampling to automatically and scalably generate tool-use trajectories. A subsequent quality normalization and difficulty-aware classification process filters out low-quality samples and organizes the dataset from easy to hard. Furthermore, we propose a two-stage training framework to enhance multi-tool collaborative reasoning by: (1) cold-start fine-tuning, which guides LLMs to explore reasoning patterns via tool-invocation feedback; and (2) a multi-tool self-critic RL algorithm with hierarchical reward design, which reinforces reward understanding and promotes effective tool collaboration. Experimental analyses on over 10 challenging reasoning benchmarks highlight the effectiveness and efficiency of Tool-Star. The code is available at https://github.com/dongguanting/Tool-Star.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Tool-Star: Empowering LLM-Brained Multi-Tool Reasoner via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tool-Star, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to enhance the multi-tool collaborative reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs). Tool-Star aims to enable LLMs to autonomously invoke and utilize multiple external tools (search engines, web browsers, code interpreters) during stepwise reasoning. The framework incorporates a tool-integrated data synthesis pipeline, a quality normalization and difficulty-aware classification process for data curation, and a two-stage training strategy consisting of cold-start fine-tuning and a multi-tool self-critic RL algorithm with hierarchical reward design. Experiments across various reasoning benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of Tool-Star compared to other baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel framework for multi-tool reasoning in LLMs. While individual components like RL for tool use, data synthesis pipelines, and reward shaping have been explored before, the combination of these elements, specifically tailored for multi-tool collaboration with hierarchical rewards and self-critic fine-tuning, is a valuable contribution. The introduction of inference-time optimization tools is also a positive aspect of the paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in tool-integrated reasoning – the coordination and effective use of multiple tools. Real-world reasoning often requires integrating various capabilities (information seeking, computation, etc.), making multi-tool collaboration essential. Tool-Star demonstrates improved performance on challenging reasoning benchmarks, suggesting its potential to advance the state-of-the-art in this area. The systematic approach to data synthesis and training also has the potential to inspire future research.
However, the paper has some limitations which impact the significance of it. The comparison of the method Tool-Star and the base models show a relatively small margin of improvement on many of the datasets. This can raise questions regarding its effectiveness on more robust benchmarks. In addition, while the components in the framework are well combined, the results from the experimental ablation show that there are performance degradation when some module is removed, but not significantly. This result indicates that these components might not be well integrated.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper provides a well-structured framework encompassing data synthesis, data curation, and training.
    *   **Multi-Tool Focus:** The explicit focus on multi-tool collaboration distinguishes this work from many prior approaches focused on individual tool use.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on various benchmarks support the effectiveness of Tool-Star.
    *   **Careful Data Curation:** The data synthesis pipeline and quality normalization steps are valuable for addressing the data scarcity issue in tool-use learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The framework's complexity may make it challenging to implement and reproduce.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Tool Combinations:** The paper could benefit from more detailed analysis of how different tool combinations contribute to performance gains.
    *   **Benchmarking:** The number of high-quality benchmarks and number of models compared could be expanded.
    *   **Justification for the choices made.** The authors provided reasonable justification for the framework and how the components are combined, but these can be enhanced to provide more insights for why those are effective.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's potential lies in promoting further research on multi-tool reasoning and inspiring the development of more sophisticated and practical tool-integrated LLMs. The data synthesis pipeline and training techniques could serve as valuable resources for the community.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:** While the paper presents a reasonably novel and technically sound framework with promising results, the relatively small margin of improvement over existing methods on some datasets and the complexity of the implementation limit its overall significance. The paper offers a solid contribution to the field of tool-integrated reasoning, but more thorough exploration of certain aspects and more robust benchmarking would strengthen its impact. However, the approach taken is promising, which can be further developed and integrated to future models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Attributing Response to Context: A Jensen-Shannon Divergence Driven Mechanistic Study of Context Attribution in Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16415v1)
- **Authors**: Ruizhe Li, Chen Chen, Yuchen Hu, Yanjun Gao, Xi Wang, Emine Yilmaz
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) leverages large language models (LLMs) combined with external contexts to enhance the accuracy and reliability of generated responses. However, reliably attributing generated content to specific context segments, context attribution, remains challenging due to the computationally intensive nature of current methods, which often require extensive fine-tuning or human annotation. In this work, we introduce a novel Jensen-Shannon Divergence driven method to Attribute Response to Context (ARC-JSD), enabling efficient and accurate identification of essential context sentences without additional fine-tuning or surrogate modelling. Evaluations on a wide range of RAG benchmarks, such as TyDi QA, Hotpot QA, and Musique, using instruction-tuned LLMs in different scales demonstrate superior accuracy and significant computational efficiency improvements compared to the previous surrogate-based method. Furthermore, our mechanistic analysis reveals specific attention heads and multilayer perceptron (MLP) layers responsible for context attribution, providing valuable insights into the internal workings of RAG models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARC-JSD, a novel and efficient inference-time method for attributing responses to context in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) models.  ARC-JSD leverages Jensen-Shannon Divergence to measure the difference in response distributions when context sentences are ablated.  Sentences causing the largest divergence are identified as crucial for generating the response. The method avoids the need for computationally expensive fine-tuning or surrogate modeling approaches.  The paper demonstrates superior accuracy and speed compared to prior surrogate-based methods on several QA benchmarks using various instruction-tuned LLMs.  Furthermore, the authors perform a mechanistic analysis, pinpointing specific attention heads and MLP layers responsible for context attribution by integrating JSD-based analysis with Logit Lens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The use of Jensen-Shannon Divergence to quantify the impact of context ablation on response distributions is a relatively novel approach for context attribution in RAG.  While the general idea of ablating context to assess importance is not new, the specific application of JSD and the elimination of surrogate models represent a contribution. The mechanistic analysis, while building on existing techniques like Logit Lens, adds a layer of understanding specific to context attribution in RAG models, rather than just general hallucination or next token prediction.

*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the improved efficiency and accuracy of the proposed method.  Eliminating the need for fine-tuning or surrogate models drastically reduces the computational burden of context attribution, making it more practical for real-world RAG applications. The mechanistic insights are also valuable for understanding how LLMs integrate external knowledge and could potentially inform the design of more reliable RAG architectures. However, the dependence on ablation introduces quadratic complexity. Additionally, the scope of the mechanistic investigation is limited to the first-order analysis of attention heads and MLP layers and the paper lacks detailed exploration of neuron-level mechanisms or interventional studies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The method demonstrates a significant speedup compared to surrogate-based approaches.
    *   **Accuracy:**  The empirical results show improved context attribution accuracy across multiple datasets and models.
    *   **Mechanistic Insights:**  The paper provides valuable insights into the internal workings of RAG models, identifying specific attention heads and MLP layers involved in context attribution.
    *   **Modularity and Easy Integration:** The claim of seamless integration into existing RAG pipelines strengthens its practical adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Ablation Complexity:** While more efficient than training surrogates, the method still requires a forward pass for each context sentence. This could become expensive for very long contexts.
    *   **Scope of Mechanistic Analysis:** The study primarily focuses on identifying relevant attention heads and MLP layers without delving into deeper circuit-level understanding or ablation interventions. The exploration of neuron-level mechanisms or interventional studies remains limited, offering potential avenues for future research.
    *   **Generalizability:** While evaluated on several datasets and LLMs, the results may not generalize to all possible RAG settings or domains. For example, it could be challenging to determine what a context sentence is for tasks such as coding.
    *   **Limited Ablation Granularity:**  The ablation is done at the sentence level, which is arbitrary and may miss fine-grained token-level context dependencies.
    *   **Dependence on High-Quality LLMs:** The performance depends on the RAG models having a good capability to perform the inference task.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:
    *   **RAG Efficiency:** Encourages the development of more efficient context attribution methods.
    *   **RAG Trustworthiness:** Improves the reliability and transparency of RAG systems.
    *   **Mechanistic Interpretability:** Provides a foundation for further research into the internal mechanisms of RAG models.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of RAG by addressing a practical challenge – efficient context attribution. The use of JSD is well-motivated, and the empirical results support the claims of improved accuracy and efficiency. The mechanistic analysis is a valuable addition, providing insights that could guide future RAG architecture design. However, the method's dependence on ablation, while more efficient than prior work, still presents a computational hurdle, and its overall effectiveness is tied to the quality of the underlying LLM. While the efficiency of ARC-JSD has been proven, the scalability of its deployment has not been established. Overall, the work is a valuable advancement but has some limitations that temper its long-term influence.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Circle-RoPE: Cone-like Decoupled Rotary Positional Embedding for Large Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16416v1)
- **Authors**: Chengcheng Wang, Jianyuan Guo, Hongguang Li, Yuchuan Tian, Ying Nie, Chang Xu, Kai Han
- **Abstract**: Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) is a widely adopted technique for encoding relative positional information in large language models (LLMs). However, when extended to large vision-language models (LVLMs), its variants introduce unintended cross-modal positional biases. Specifically, they enforce relative positional dependencies between text token indices and image tokens, causing spurious alignments. This issue arises because image tokens representing the same content but located at different spatial positions are assigned distinct positional biases, leading to inconsistent cross-modal associations. To address this, we propose Per-Token Distance (PTD) - a simple yet effective metric for quantifying the independence of positional encodings across modalities. Informed by this analysis, we introduce Circle-RoPE, a novel encoding scheme that maps image token indices onto a circular trajectory orthogonal to the linear path of text token indices, forming a cone-like structure. This configuration ensures that each text token maintains an equal distance to all image tokens, reducing artificial cross-modal biases while preserving intra-image spatial information. To further enhance performance, we propose a staggered layer strategy that applies different RoPE variants across layers. This design leverages the complementary strengths of each RoPE variant, thereby enhancing the model's overall performance. Our experimental results demonstrate that our method effectively preserves spatial information from images while reducing relative positional bias, offering a more robust and flexible positional encoding framework for LVLMs. The code is available at [https://github.com/lose4578/CircleRoPE](https://github.com/lose4578/CircleRoPE).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Circle-RoPE: Cone-like Decoupled Rotary Positional Embedding for Large Vision-Language Models" addresses the problem of positional bias in large vision-language models (LVLMs) that arises from directly applying Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) to both text and image tokens.  The authors argue that naive application of RoPE creates unintended dependencies between text and image token positions, leading to spurious alignments and impaired cross-modal understanding. To address this, they propose Circle-RoPE, a novel positional encoding scheme that maps image token indices onto a circular trajectory, orthogonal to the linear trajectory of text token indices. This forms a cone-like structure, ensuring equal distance between each text token and all image tokens, thus reducing cross-modal biases.  Circle-RoPE also includes a Dual-Frame Fusion (DFF) mechanism to enhance intra-image spatial consistency and an Alternating Geometric Encoding (AGE) strategy to leverage complementary strengths of different RoPE variants across Transformer layers.  Experimental results demonstrate that Circle-RoPE effectively preserves spatial information from images while reducing relative positional bias, leading to improved performance on various visual reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to positional encoding in LVLMs. The idea of decoupling text and image token positions by mapping image tokens onto a circular trajectory orthogonal to the text token trajectory is conceptually interesting and reasonably innovative. The Dual-Frame Fusion and Alternating Geometric Encoding strategies add further refinement and flexibility. However, the building blocks—RoPE and 2D positional encodings used in M-RoPE—are well-established. The main novelty lies in the specific geometric transformation and the combined application of DFF and AGE. The Per-Token Distance (PTD) metric is a useful tool for analyzing positional biases.

* **Significance:** The paper tackles a relevant problem in LVLMs. The identified positional biases can hinder cross-modal alignment and negatively impact performance. The proposed Circle-RoPE offers a potentially more robust and flexible positional encoding framework, offering a promising approach to mitigate these issues. The empirical results demonstrate some improvements over the baseline, indicating the effectiveness of the method. However, the experiments are done on a subset of data for training and the results are only marginally better than the baselines.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and analysis of existing RoPE limitations in LVLMs.
    * Novel Circle-RoPE encoding scheme with a good geometric interpretation.
    * Introduction of the PTD metric for quantifying positional bias.
    * Dual-Frame Fusion for balancing decoupling and spatial preservation.
    * The AGE strategy improves the model's capabilities.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The performance gains over the baseline (Qwen2.5-VL) in the results are marginal, particularly considering the significant changes to the model architecture.
    * The use of a subset of the MammotH-VL Instruct dataset limits the generalizability of the results. Training and evaluation on the full dataset are needed to confirm consistent performance improvements.
    * While the paper provides ablation studies to evaluate the impact of different components (angle mixing, radius, dual frame fusion, alternating encoding), a more rigorous sensitivity analysis would strengthen the conclusions. It would be useful to see how the performance varies with a broader range of hyperparameter settings for these components.
    * The code is provided, which aids reproducibility, but a more detailed complexity analysis of Circle-RoPE compared to standard RoPE is missing.

* **Potential Influence:** If the performance of Circle-RoPE can be shown to consistently outperform standard RoPE and its variants across a broader range of datasets and model architectures, it could become a valuable technique for improving cross-modal alignment in LVLMs. The PTD metric could also be adopted by other researchers for analyzing and mitigating positional biases.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to addressing positional bias in LVLMs.  While the core building blocks are not entirely new, the specific geometric transformation and the combined application of the different components are unique and contribute to the field. The introduction of the PTD metric is also a valuable contribution. However, the marginal performance gains in the results, the limited experimental scope (subset of the training data) and the missing complexity analysis limit its impact. The paper demonstrates potential but needs more significant improvements and rigorous experimental validation to achieve a higher score.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### WebAgent-R1: Training Web Agents via End-to-End Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16421v1)
- **Authors**: Zhepei Wei, Wenlin Yao, Yao Liu, Weizhi Zhang, Qin Lu, Liang Qiu, Changlong Yu, Puyang Xu, Chao Zhang, Bing Yin, Hyokun Yun, Lihong Li
- **Abstract**: While reinforcement learning (RL) has demonstrated remarkable success in enhancing large language models (LLMs), it has primarily focused on single-turn tasks such as solving math problems. Training effective web agents for multi-turn interactions remains challenging due to the complexity of long-horizon decision-making across dynamic web interfaces. In this work, we present WebAgent-R1, a simple yet effective end-to-end multi-turn RL framework for training web agents. It learns directly from online interactions with web environments by asynchronously generating diverse trajectories, entirely guided by binary rewards depending on task success. Experiments on the WebArena-Lite benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of WebAgent-R1, boosting the task success rate of Qwen-2.5-3B from 6.1% to 33.9% and Llama-3.1-8B from 8.5% to 44.8%, significantly outperforming existing state-of-the-art methods and strong proprietary models such as OpenAI o3. In-depth analyses reveal the effectiveness of the thinking-based prompting strategy and test-time scaling through increased interactions for web tasks. We further investigate different RL initialization policies by introducing two variants, namely WebAgent-R1-Zero and WebAgent-R1-CoT, which highlight the importance of the warm-up training stage (i.e., behavior cloning) and provide insights on incorporating long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in web agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "WEBAGENT-R1: Training Web Agents via End-to-End Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WEBAGENT-R1, an end-to-end multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed for training web agents.  The framework addresses the challenges of long-horizon decision-making and dynamic web interfaces by using dynamic context compression and asynchronous trajectory rollouts.  It extends group relative policy optimization (GRPO) to a multi-turn setting (M-GRPO) and uses rule-based rewards.  Experiments on the WebArena-Lite benchmark show that WEBAGENT-R1 significantly improves the task success rate of web agents compared to existing prompting, fine-tuning, and other RL-based methods, even outperforming proprietary models like OpenAI 03. The authors also analyze the impact of behavior cloning, thinking-based prompting, and test-time scaling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **End-to-End RL for Web Agents:** The paper presents a straightforward and practical approach to tackle web agent training with end-to-end RL using binary rewards. This is significant because many previous works relied on offline or iterative RL solutions, thereby interrupting the agent-environment interaction loop and introducing complexities.
    * **Performance Improvements:**  The results on the WebArena-Lite benchmark are compelling. The reported increases in task success rates over existing methods and strong proprietary models are a strong indicator of the framework's effectiveness.
    * **Addressing Practical Challenges:** The dynamic context compression effectively tackles the memory constraints inherent in processing long HTML contexts, while asynchronous trajectory rollout increases training efficiency.  These practical considerations are valuable for real-world deployment.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The ablations are valuable, particularly the analysis of behavior cloning's impact. The finding that behavior cloning is crucial before applying RL adds to the understanding of how to effectively train web agents and underscores the challenges of pure exploration-based learning in this setting.
    * **Insightful Analysis of Prompting:**  The paper investigates the effects of thinking-based prompting and reveals a novel test-time scaling strategy: increasing the number of interactions. This is a practical finding that could be easily adopted.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** The paper extends GRPO to a multi-turn setting, but the core RL algorithm is relatively standard. While the dynamic context compression and asynchronous trajectory rollout are beneficial, their novelty is somewhat limited. The techniques are well-established.
    * **Limited Ablation on M-GRPO:** While the paper shows the effectiveness of RL, a more in-depth ablation on the M-GRPO algorithm itself (e.g., the impact of group size, hyperparameters) would further strengthen the analysis.
    * **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While success rates are provided, a deeper dive into the types of tasks where WEBAGENT-R1 still struggles would be beneficial. Understanding the limitations can guide future research directions.  Discussing the specific challenges of failure modes provides insights as valuable as success rate reports.
    * **Reliance on Rule-Based Rewards:** Using rule-based rewards, while straightforward, can limit the flexibility and robustness of the agent. Learning more nuanced rewards would further enhance the agent's capabilities.
* **Significance:**

The paper provides a valuable, practical framework for training web agents using end-to-end RL. The significant performance gains on WebArena-Lite and the insights from the ablation studies contribute meaningfully to the field. While some components of the approach are incremental extensions of existing work, the overall system is well-engineered, addresses practical challenges, and provides strong empirical evidence of its effectiveness. The analysis of test-time scaling through increased interactions is novel and particularly valuable.

**Justification:**

WEBAGENT-R1 achieves strong empirical results and offers practical design insights for web agent training. It’s more than a simple application of existing techniques; it's a well-integrated system that addresses real-world challenges. However, the core RL algorithm is not groundbreaking, and there are opportunities for more in-depth analysis of the M-GRPO algorithm, failure cases, and potential improvements to the reward structure.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Static Testbeds: An Interaction-Centric Agent Simulation Platform for Dynamic Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16429v1)
- **Authors**: Song Jin, Juntian Zhang, Yuhan Liu, Xun Zhang, Yufei Zhang, Guojun Yin, Fei Jiang, Wei Lin, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Evaluating and iterating upon recommender systems is crucial, yet traditional A/B testing is resource-intensive, and offline methods struggle with dynamic user-platform interactions. While agent-based simulation is promising, existing platforms often lack a mechanism for user actions to dynamically reshape the environment. To bridge this gap, we introduce RecInter, a novel agent-based simulation platform for recommender systems featuring a robust interaction mechanism. In RecInter platform, simulated user actions (e.g., likes, reviews, purchases) dynamically update item attributes in real-time, and introduced Merchant Agents can reply, fostering a more realistic and evolving ecosystem. High-fidelity simulation is ensured through Multidimensional User Profiling module, Advanced Agent Architecture, and LLM fine-tuned on Chain-of-Thought (CoT) enriched interaction data. Our platform achieves significantly improved simulation credibility and successfully replicates emergent phenomena like Brand Loyalty and the Matthew Effect. Experiments demonstrate that this interaction mechanism is pivotal for simulating realistic system evolution, establishing our platform as a credible testbed for recommender systems research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Static Testbeds: An Interaction-Centric Agent Simulation Platform for Dynamic Recommender Systems":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RecInter, a novel agent-based simulation platform for recommender systems designed to address the limitations of traditional A/B testing and static offline evaluation methods.  A key feature of RecInter is its focus on creating a dynamic, interactive environment where user actions (likes, reviews, purchases) directly influence item attributes in real-time. The platform incorporates Merchant Agents that can respond to user feedback, further enhancing the realism of the simulated ecosystem. To achieve high-fidelity simulations, RecInter integrates (1) a Multidimensional User Profiling module (objective, subjective, inferred), (2) an Advanced Agent Architecture (perceptual and cognitive memory), and (3) a Behavior Simulation Training pipeline using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) enriched interaction data for LLM-based agents. The authors demonstrate through experiments that RecInter can reproduce emergent phenomena like brand loyalty and the Matthew effect, suggesting its credibility as a testbed for recommender systems research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *explicit* and *robust* integration of an interaction mechanism within an agent-based recommender system simulation. While previous works have explored agent-based simulations and LLM-powered agents for recommendation, RecInter distinguishes itself by making user actions dynamically reshape the environment, affecting future user decisions in a feedback loop. The introduction of Merchant Agents is also a valuable addition, although the strategies they employ could be expanded.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to offer a more realistic and cost-effective alternative to A/B testing for evaluating recommender systems. By accurately simulating dynamic user-platform interactions, RecInter can help researchers and practitioners: (1) better understand the long-term effects of recommendation algorithms, (2) identify unintended consequences, and (3) test new strategies in a controlled environment without exposing real users to untested systems. The paper's ability to replicate established phenomena like brand loyalty and the Matthew effect provides strong initial validation of the platform's realism.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing evaluation methods and the need for interaction-centric simulations.
    *   **Well-Designed Platform:** RecInter's modular design, incorporating multi-dimensional user profiling, advanced agent architecture, and behavior simulation training, is well-thought-out and contributes to the platform's credibility.
    *   **Strong Experimental Validation:** The experiments demonstrate that RecInter achieves improved simulation accuracy and can replicate real-world phenomena.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide details about the experimental setup, baseline models, and training procedures, enhancing the reproducibility of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependence:** Reliance on LLMs for user profile generation and action selection introduces potential biases and inaccuracies. The paper acknowledges this limitation but could benefit from more in-depth analysis and mitigation strategies. While CoT fine-tuning is used, more could be done to address potential hallucination issues during agent interaction.
    *   **Limited Action Space:** The predefined set of user actions may not fully capture the complexity of real-world user behavior. Expanding the action space could improve the platform's realism.
    *   **Scope of Interactions:** The platform primarily focuses on user-item and merchant-item interactions. It neglects other important aspects of recommendation ecosystems, such as social influence, community dynamics, and the impact of external events. User to user interactions, and the overall impact of social factors on the simulation, were not captured.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Merchant Agent Strategy**: Although the idea of merchant agent is promising, the paper does not elaborate the strategy how merchants will response to different user feedback.

*   **Potential Influence:**  RecInter has the potential to significantly influence the field of recommender systems by providing a more robust and realistic evaluation environment. It can serve as a valuable tool for researchers to explore complex system dynamics and develop more effective and user-centric recommendation strategies. The platform could also be adopted by industry practitioners to test and refine their algorithms before deployment.

**Justification:**

The assigned score reflects the paper's significant contribution in addressing a critical gap in recommender system evaluation by introducing an interaction-centric simulation platform. While acknowledging the limitations related to LLM dependence, limited action space, scope of interactions, and merchant agent strategy, the paper's novelty, strong experimental validation, and potential influence justify a relatively high score. The ability to simulate emergent phenomena and demonstrate higher accuracy than baselines is compelling. This paper can be seen as a valuable step towards more realistic and insightful recommender system evaluation.

### Implicit Jailbreak Attacks via Cross-Modal Information Concealment on Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16446v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaoxin Wang, Handing Wang, Cong Tian, Yaochu Jin
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) enable powerful cross-modal reasoning capabilities. However, the expanded input space introduces new attack surfaces. Previous jailbreak attacks often inject malicious instructions from text into less aligned modalities, such as vision. As MLLMs increasingly incorporate cross-modal consistency and alignment mechanisms, such explicit attacks become easier to detect and block. In this work, we propose a novel implicit jailbreak framework termed IJA that stealthily embeds malicious instructions into images via least significant bit steganography and couples them with seemingly benign, image-related textual prompts. To further enhance attack effectiveness across diverse MLLMs, we incorporate adversarial suffixes generated by a surrogate model and introduce a template optimization module that iteratively refines both the prompt and embedding based on model feedback. On commercial models like GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5 Pro, our method achieves attack success rates of over 90% using an average of only 3 queries.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Implicit Jailbreak Attacks via Cross-Modal Information Concealment on Vision-Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel implicit jailbreak attack framework (IJA) against Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). IJA bypasses safety mechanisms by concealing malicious instructions within images using steganography (LSB embedding) and coupling them with benign text prompts. The framework incorporates adversarial suffixes generated by a surrogate model and an iterative template optimization module based on model feedback to enhance the attack's effectiveness across various MLLMs. Experiments on commercial models like GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5 Pro demonstrate high attack success rates (over 90%) using few queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach demonstrates novelty in its implicit methodology. Existing jailbreak attacks often rely on explicit adversarial text or obvious visual manipulations, making them susceptible to detection by safety filters. The IJA cleverly combines steganography, adversarial suffixes, and iterative prompt refinement to bypass these defenses more effectively. The approach of using LSB embedding for concealing instructions, while not entirely novel in itself, is used creatively within the context of multimodal jailbreaking. The adaptive prompt refinement based on model feedback is also a noteworthy contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper identifies a significant vulnerability in MLLMs related to cross-modal alignment and implicit instruction following. By successfully executing jailbreak attacks on state-of-the-art commercial models, the paper highlights the limitations of current safety alignment techniques in MLLMs. This emphasizes the need for more robust defense mechanisms that consider implicit information embedding and cross-modal reasoning. The potential impact of this research is substantial, as it raises awareness and encourages further research into securing MLLMs against such attacks.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating high attack success rates on commercial MLLMs.
    *   Detailed description of the proposed framework, including the different components and their roles.
    *   Comprehensive experimental setup with appropriate baselines and evaluation metrics.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrating the contribution of each component to the overall performance.
    *   The paper explicitly acknowledges potential societal impacts, which improves its credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on LSB steganography has limitations. While visually imperceptible, it is susceptible to certain image processing operations. Future work could explore more robust steganographic methods.
    *   The surrogate model approach adds complexity and computational cost.
    *   The template optimization process, while effective, may be dependent on the specific characteristics of the target MLLMs.
    *   The experiments primarily focus on image-text MLLMs. Further research is needed to evaluate the applicability of the approach to other modalities (e.g., audio, video).
    *   The authors mention "the surrogate model to generate adversarial suffixes incurs additional computational overhead." More detail should be provided about the computational resources consumed for the suffix optimization process.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper can influence future research in multimodal security and defense, particularly in developing more robust alignment techniques and safety filters.
    *   It highlights the importance of considering implicit information concealment in security assessments of MLLMs.
    *   The proposed IJA framework can be used as a benchmark for evaluating the effectiveness of new defense mechanisms.
    *   The template optimization module can inspire the development of adaptive defense strategies that dynamically adjust to the characteristics of the attack.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score**

The paper presents a well-executed and novel attack methodology that successfully circumvents current safety measures in sophisticated MLLMs. The empirical results are compelling and clearly demonstrate the practical implications of the vulnerability it has discovered. However, the limitations related to the reliance on LSB steganography, the computational overhead, and the need for further evaluation across different modalities prevent the work from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The methodology presented is novel and impactful, but the techniques utilized may need more refinement to be robustly applicable in a wide variety of situations. Overall, it is a strong research contribution that makes an important addition to the growing research on multimodal LLM security.

### Psychology-driven LLM Agents for Explainable Panic Prediction on Social Media during Sudden Disaster Events
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16455v1)
- **Authors**: Mengzhu Liu, Zhengqiu Zhu, Chuan Ai, Chen Gao, Xinghong Li, Lingnan He, Kaisheng Lai, Yingfeng Chen, Xin Lu, Yong Li, Quanjun Yin
- **Abstract**: During sudden disaster events, accurately predicting public panic sentiment on social media is crucial for proactive governance and crisis management. Current efforts on this problem face three main challenges: lack of finely annotated data hinders emotion prediction studies, unmodeled risk perception causes prediction inaccuracies, and insufficient interpretability of panic formation mechanisms. We address these issues by proposing a Psychology-driven generative Agent framework (PsychoAgent) for explainable panic prediction based on emotion arousal theory. Specifically, we first construct a fine-grained open panic emotion dataset (namely COPE) via human-large language models (LLMs) collaboration to mitigate semantic bias. Then, we develop a framework integrating cross-domain heterogeneous data grounded in psychological mechanisms to model risk perception and cognitive differences in emotion generation. To enhance interpretability, we design an LLM-based role-playing agent that simulates individual psychological chains through dedicatedly designed prompts. Experimental results on our annotated dataset show that PsychoAgent improves panic emotion prediction performance by 12.6% to 21.7% compared to baseline models. Furthermore, the explainability and generalization of our approach is validated. Crucially, this represents a paradigm shift from opaque "data-driven fitting" to transparent "role-based simulation with mechanistic interpretation" for panic emotion prediction during emergencies. Our implementation is publicly available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PsychoAgent-19DD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper "Psychology-driven LLM Agents for Explainable Panic Prediction on Social Media during Sudden Disaster Events":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of predicting panic on social media during sudden disasters. It proposes a framework called PsychoAgent, which uses psychology-driven LLM agents to simulate the formation of panic emotions based on multi-domain data (physical disaster characteristics, risk communication, and individual psychological traits). The framework incorporates a novel dual-phase panic emotion dataset (COPE) created using human-LLM collaboration for annotation. PsychoAgent leverages CoT-driven LLM agents with specific prompts to model disaster perception, risk assessment, and emotional arousal. The approach includes a multi-expert verification system to improve the quality of generated content. The experiments demonstrate that PsychoAgent improves the panic emotion prediction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects:
    *   The human-LLM collaborative annotation method for building the COPE dataset.
    *   The psychological informed framework, particularly the use of LLMs to simulate psychological processes and cognitive-emotional chains that lead to panic.
    *   Integration of multi-domain data within a theoretical framework (emotion arousal, risk perception).
    *   The CoT-driven LLM agent design with specific stages for disaster perception, risk assessment, and emotional response.

*   **Significance:**
    *   Addresses a real-world problem of great importance (disaster management, public safety).
    *   The explainability of the framework is a crucial advantage over opaque "data-driven fitting" approaches, potentially leading to more trust and better intervention strategies.
    *   The multi-domain data integration approach could be adapted for predicting other social phenomena during emergencies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The grounding in psychological theory provides a robust and interpretable framework.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate improved performance compared to several baselines.
    *   The ablation study demonstrates contributions of key components of the PsychoAgent framework, especially the interaction of Risk Perception (RP), Emotion Arousal (EA), Multi-Expert Assessment (MEA).
    *   The case studies provide qualitative evidence of the framework's ability to model users' emotional responses in a more nuanced way.
    *   Addresses semantic deviations and cross-cultural annotation biases
    *   The study provides evidence of the synergy with model size, showing a trend that performance benefits more as model size exceeds a certain threshold.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on LLMs introduces the potential for hallucinations and stylistic deviations.
    *   The framework may be sensitive to the quality of the LLM's underlying knowledge.
    *   The political correctness guardrails can suppress negative emotions
    *   The evaluation is limited to the Hurricane Sandy dataset. While COPE dataset is a new contribution, generalization of findings would be strengthened by evaluation on different disaster scenarios or regions.
    *   While the paper addresses class imbalance in panic detection through dataset construction and model design, a deeper analysis of the specific strategies used to mitigate this imbalance could enhance the paper.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The work could influence the development of more responsible and explainable AI systems for emergency management.
    *   The framework could be extended to predict other types of emotional contagion during crises.
    *   The human-LLM collaboration approach for annotation could be used to create other high-quality datasets in related fields.

*   **Rationale for Score:**
This paper demonstrates a strong combination of novelty, significance, and methodological rigor. Its innovative use of psychology-driven LLM agents for explainable panic prediction during disasters addresses a critical need and offers a valuable alternative to traditional black-box approaches. While the paper acknowledges certain limitations, such as the potential for LLM hallucinations and sensitivity to dataset characteristics, its strengths outweigh these weaknesses. The COPE dataset and the framework's ability to model cognitive-emotional chains provide valuable contributions to the field, with the potential to improve future disaster management strategies.
