The paper is a good characterization of a problem, but it doesn't significantly advance the state-of-the-art in terms of solutions.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.PF
- **Score**: 6/10

### Beyond Face Swapping: A Diffusion-Based Digital Human Benchmark for Multimodal Deepfake Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16512v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxin Liu, Jia Wang, Saihui Hou, Min Ren, Huijia Wu, Zhaofeng He
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the rapid development of deepfake technology has given rise to an emerging and serious threat to public security: diffusion model-based digital human generation. Unlike traditional face manipulation methods, such models can generate highly realistic videos with consistency through multimodal control signals. Their flexibility and covertness pose severe challenges to existing detection strategies. To bridge this gap, we introduce DigiFakeAV, the first large-scale multimodal digital human forgery dataset based on diffusion models. Employing five latest digital human generation methods (Sonic, Hallo, etc.) and voice cloning method, we systematically produce a dataset comprising 60,000 videos (8.4 million frames), covering multiple nationalities, skin tones, genders, and real-world scenarios, significantly enhancing data diversity and realism. User studies show that the confusion rate between forged and real videos reaches 68%, and existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) detection models exhibit large drops in AUC values on DigiFakeAV, highlighting the challenge of the dataset. To address this problem, we further propose DigiShield, a detection baseline based on spatiotemporal and cross-modal fusion. By jointly modeling the 3D spatiotemporal features of videos and the semantic-acoustic features of audio, DigiShield achieves SOTA performance on both the DigiFakeAV and DF-TIMIT datasets. Experiments show that this method effectively identifies covert artifacts through fine-grained analysis of the temporal evolution of facial features in synthetic videos.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Face Swapping: A Diffusion-Based Digital Human Benchmark for Multimodal Deepfake Detection."

**Summary**

This paper introduces DigiFakeAV, a new large-scale multimodal deepfake dataset designed to benchmark the detection of forgeries created using diffusion models for digital human generation.  The authors argue that existing deepfake datasets are becoming saturated due to outdated synthesis techniques (primarily GAN-based or face-swapping) and are insufficient to capture the realism and consistency of modern diffusion-based forgeries. DigiFakeAV comprises 60,000 videos generated using five state-of-the-art digital human generation methods and voice cloning, ensuring diversity in nationalities, skin tones, genders, and real-world scenarios. User studies reveal a high confusion rate between forged and real videos, and state-of-the-art detection models exhibit a significant performance drop on DigiFakeAV, highlighting the dataset's challenge. The authors also propose DigiShield, a baseline detection method based on spatiotemporal and cross-modal fusion, achieving state-of-the-art performance on both DigiFakeAV and DF-TIMIT datasets. The work aims to bridge the gap between rapidly advancing deepfake generation technology and the lagging detection methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the *creation of a large-scale deepfake dataset specifically designed to challenge detection models against diffusion-based digital human generation*. While existing datasets have improved in size and realism, they predominantly focus on face-swapping or GAN-based techniques. The explicit focus on diffusion models, which offer higher realism and multimodal consistency, is a key contribution.  The proposed detection baseline, DigiShield, while competent, is a more incremental contribution, building upon existing spatiotemporal and cross-modal fusion techniques. Its main value is as an initial benchmark for the new dataset.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from its ability to address a critical gap in the deepfake detection field. As diffusion models become more prevalent and accessible, the threat of highly realistic synthetic media increases. This dataset provides researchers with a valuable resource to develop and evaluate detection methods capable of identifying these advanced forgeries. The user study demonstrating high confusion rates (68%) underscores the urgency and relevance of this research direction. By exposing the vulnerabilities of existing detection models, the paper motivates the development of more robust and adaptable techniques.
    The increase in multimodality in terms of video and audio is also helpful, since, more and more, deep fakes depend on the content of audio and video.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Timeliness and Relevance:**  The dataset addresses a pressing need in the field, aligning with the current advancements in deepfake generation technology.
    *   **Scale and Diversity:**  The dataset's size (60,000 videos) and the diversity of scenarios, ethnicities, and genders significantly enhance its practical value.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:**  The paper presents a thorough evaluation of state-of-the-art detection methods and includes a user study to assess the dataset's realism.
    *   **Baseline Method:**  DigiShield provides a useful starting point for future research on deepfake detection within this new context.
    *   **Open Availability:** The authors claim that they made the dataset publicly available.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Innovation in the Baseline Method:**  DigiShield, while effective, is not particularly novel in its design.  It primarily serves as a proof-of-concept for the dataset's utility, which diminishes the overall significance.  The incremental nature of the baseline could limit the long-term impact.
    *   **Potential for Rapid Obsolescence:**  As deepfake generation techniques continue to evolve rapidly, DigiFakeAV may eventually become outdated. A more adaptive or generative approach to benchmark creation could have mitigated this risk. However, that would have increased the complexity and scope of the paper, but is a potential avenue for future research.
    *   **Limited Focus on Specific Artifacts:** While claiming fine-grained analysis, the paper does not deeply explore which specific artifacts are captured via spatio-temporal analysis. Deeper analysis here would strengthen the manuscript, since currently it is somewhat shallow.
    *   **Limited discussion on negative societal impacts:** While the dataset is well motivated, it lacks discussion on potentially negative societal impacts of the dataset, such as it being used to develop advanced technologies for identity theft or spreading false information.
*   **Overall:** While the dataset presents a very interesting venue, it is likely to change quickly. The methods of doing the benchmark are relatively simple. The authors didn't perform detailed analysis on specific features for which the fakes could be detected.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's significant contribution to the field by providing a much-needed benchmark for diffusion-based deepfake detection. The dataset's scale, diversity, and timely relevance are strong points. However, the incremental novelty of the proposed baseline method and the inherent risk of obsolescence due to rapidly evolving generation techniques limit the overall impact. While the paper addresses a critical problem, it could be strengthened by more innovative detection techniques, as well as deeper artifact analysis. The proposed DigiShield appears more like a useful add-on for the manuscript, instead of the core message of the work. However, the dataset itself is helpful and well-motivated.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are the Hidden States Hiding Something? Testing the Limits of Factuality-Encoding Capabilities in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16520v1)
- **Authors**: Giovanni Servedio, Alessandro De Bellis, Dario Di Palma, Vito Walter Anelli, Tommaso Di Noia
- **Abstract**: Factual hallucinations are a major challenge for Large Language Models (LLMs). They undermine reliability and user trust by generating inaccurate or fabricated content. Recent studies suggest that when generating false statements, the internal states of LLMs encode information about truthfulness. However, these studies often rely on synthetic datasets that lack realism, which limits generalization when evaluating the factual accuracy of text generated by the model itself. In this paper, we challenge the findings of previous work by investigating truthfulness encoding capabilities, leading to the generation of a more realistic and challenging dataset. Specifically, we extend previous work by introducing: (1) a strategy for sampling plausible true-false factoid sentences from tabular data and (2) a procedure for generating realistic, LLM-dependent true-false datasets from Question Answering collections. Our analysis of two open-source LLMs reveals that while the findings from previous studies are partially validated, generalization to LLM-generated datasets remains challenging. This study lays the groundwork for future research on factuality in LLMs and offers practical guidelines for more effective evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to encode factuality in their hidden states, building upon previous work by Azaria and Mitchell (2023). The authors challenge the limitations of existing synthetic datasets used for this purpose, arguing that they lack realism and may not accurately reflect real-world scenarios where LLMs encounter subtle or nuanced false statements. To address this, they propose two novel dataset generation strategies: (1) a perplexity-based negative sampling approach to generate more plausible false statements from tabular data, and (2) a method for generating realistic, LLM-dependent true-false datasets from question answering (QA) collections. They reproduce the original experimental setup, including the SAPLMA probe, and then evaluate the performance of open-source LLMs (Llama 2-7b and OPT-6.7b) on both the original dataset and their newly generated datasets. Their results show that while the original findings are partially validated on the new synthetic dataset, generalizing to LLM-generated datasets remains a significant challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in its two novel dataset generation strategies. The perplexity-based negative sampling strategy is a reasonable improvement over random substitutions, generating more plausible false statements. The LLM-generated dataset from QA collections is another strong point, bringing more realism by directly involving the LLM in the fact generation. The paper's focus on addressing the limitations of existing synthetic datasets is a valuable addition to the field.
*   **Significance:** The study highlights the challenges in evaluating factuality in LLMs and points out the limitations of existing benchmarks. By demonstrating that probes trained on synthetic data do not generalize well to LLM-generated content, the paper underscores the need for more realistic and diverse evaluation methods. This is crucial for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs in real-world applications. The paper provides practical guidelines and insights for future research on factuality assessments in LLMs. The reproducibility study is important and commendable, though it only serves as a baseline.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated problem statement and clear articulation of the limitations of previous work.
    *   Two novel and well-explained dataset generation strategies.
    *   Thorough experimental setup with replication of previous work.
    *   Clear presentation of results and insightful discussion.
    *   Code availability adds to the reproducibility and usefulness.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While novel, the complexity and resource intensiveness of the new LLM-generated data set limits the scale of its application in the experiments. A bigger dataset could produce more robust results.
    *   Although the authors mention it is not a surprise, that there are limits in generalizing previous results to LLM-generated data sets is a significant one.
    *   Lack of thorough hyperparameter tuning for SAPLMA on the new datasets. The methodology is reused but not fully explored. This means, there is a risk the SAPLMA implementation is not well adapted to the new data sets.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence future research by encouraging the development of more realistic and challenging benchmarks for evaluating LLM factuality. It might shift the focus towards evaluation datasets crafted to better align with the distribution of content generated by LLMs themselves. It may lead researchers to better adapt probe architectures.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:** The paper offers a solid contribution by identifying the limitations of current evaluation methods and proposing two relevant dataset generation strategies to better assess LLM factuality. While it builds directly on previous work, the added realism in the datasets is a valuable advance. The core finding –that generalization to LLM-generated content is difficult– is somewhat unsurprising but nonetheless crucial for directing future research. However, the limitations regarding the relatively small size of the newly generated data sets, the limited exploration of the probe architecture, and the generalization difficulties prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Benchmarking and Pushing the Multi-Bias Elimination Boundary of LLMs via Causal Effect Estimation-guided Debiasing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16522v1)
- **Authors**: Zhouhao Sun, Zhiyuan Kan, Xiao Ding, Li Du, Yang Zhao, Bing Qin, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: Despite significant progress, recent studies have indicated that current large language models (LLMs) may still utilize bias during inference, leading to the poor generalizability of LLMs. Some benchmarks are proposed to investigate the generalizability of LLMs, with each piece of data typically containing one type of controlled bias. However, a single piece of data may contain multiple types of biases in practical applications. To bridge this gap, we propose a multi-bias benchmark where each piece of data contains five types of biases. The evaluations conducted on this benchmark reveal that the performance of existing LLMs and debiasing methods is unsatisfying, highlighting the challenge of eliminating multiple types of biases simultaneously. To overcome this challenge, we propose a causal effect estimation-guided multi-bias elimination method (CMBE). This method first estimates the causal effect of multiple types of biases simultaneously. Subsequently, we eliminate the causal effect of biases from the total causal effect exerted by both the semantic information and biases during inference. Experimental results show that CMBE can effectively eliminate multiple types of bias simultaneously to enhance the generalizability of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of bias in Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in scenarios where a single piece of data contains multiple types of biases. The authors argue that existing benchmarks and debiasing methods primarily focus on single-bias scenarios and are inadequate for real-world applications where biases often co-occur. To address this, the paper proposes a multi-bias benchmark where each data point contains five types of biases with the same polarity, making it harder for LLMs to filter out the bias. The authors then introduce a Causal Effect Estimation-guided Multi-Bias Elimination (CMBE) method to mitigate these biases. CMBE estimates the causal effect of each bias and removes them from the total causal effect to improve the generalizability. Experimental results demonstrate that CMBE effectively debiases LLMs and enhances their generalizability in multi-bias scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's key strength lies in its identification and formalization of the multi-bias problem. Previous works largely focus on single or limited types of biases. Constructing a benchmark to evaluate LLMs in this more complex, realistic setting is a significant contribution. The CMBE method, while building on causal inference techniques, presents a novel application to the specific problem of multi-bias mitigation in LLMs. The assumption that the combined effect of different biases is a weighted linear combination of individual bias effects simplifies the otherwise intractable problem, but raises some questions (detailed below).

*   **Significance:** The paper is important because it highlights a critical gap in current LLM evaluation and debiasing efforts. By showing that existing LLMs and debiasing techniques perform poorly on multi-bias datasets, it motivates further research in this area. The CMBE method provides a promising direction for tackling this challenge. The work has potential implications for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs in real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines and motivates the multi-bias problem, highlighting its relevance and the limitations of existing solutions.
    *   **Benchmark Construction:** The proposed multi-bias benchmark is a valuable resource for evaluating LLMs in a more realistic setting. The authors outline a detailed process for constructing this benchmark.
    *   **Methodological Approach:** The CMBE method is well-motivated and grounded in causal inference principles. The simplification assumption makes the problem tractable.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of CMBE in comparison to existing debiasing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifying Assumption:** The assumption that the causal effect of combined biases is a linear combination of individual bias effects may not always hold true. Complex interactions between biases may lead to non-linear effects. This limits the ability of CMBE to model all possible bias interactions.
    *   **Bias Polarity Focus:** Constructing biases with the *same* polarity means that biases are *added* together.  In the real world, biases can have *opposing* polarities, which may lead to canceling of biases and unpredictable model behaviour.  This constraint makes the benchmark valuable, but does not account for the broad array of real world situations.
    *   **Scalability:** The CMBE method relies on estimating the causal effect of each bias individually, which may become computationally expensive as the number of biases increases.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses on the NLI task. While this allows for a controlled evaluation, it is important to investigate whether the findings and the CMBE method generalize to other NLP tasks and domains.
    *   **Limited types of Bias types examined:** Although the title and abstract focuses on multi-bias, it is important to note that the authors use types of biases, such as speculative word, and length of premise, which are types of features that are commonly used to describe datasets in machine learning. There are *no* human-based biases addressed.
    *   **Lack of comparison to adversarial debiasing methods:**  Prior works in adversarial debiasing are not addressed and there are no comparisons performed against those methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's contribution is notable and the benchmark that the authors propose and release has the potential to stimulate further research on multi-bias mitigation in LLMs.
* **Clarity:** The paper has excellent clarity and is well-structured.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7/10**.

*   The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the under-explored problem of multi-bias in LLMs. The benchmark and the CMBE method provide a tangible step forward in evaluating and mitigating biases in more realistic scenarios.
*   However, the simplifying assumption of linear bias interaction and the limited scope of evaluation (NLI task, and *no* human biases) constrain the impact and generalizability of the findings.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EnSToM: Enhancing Dialogue Systems with Entropy-Scaled Steering Vectors for Topic Maintenance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16526v1)
- **Authors**: Heejae Suh, Yejin Jeon, Deokhyung Kang, Taehee Park, Yejin Min, Gary Geunbae Lee
- **Abstract**: Small large language models (sLLMs) offer the advantage of being lightweight and efficient, which makes them suitable for resource-constrained environments. However, sLLMs often struggle to maintain topic consistency in task-oriented dialogue systems, which is critical for scenarios such as service chatbots. Specifically, it is important to ensure that the model denies off-topic or malicious inputs and adheres to its intended functionality so as to prevent potential misuse and uphold reliability. Towards this, existing activation engineering approaches have been proposed to manipulate internal activations during inference. While these methods are effective in certain scenarios, our preliminary experiments reveal their limitations in ensuring topic adherence. Therefore, to address this, we propose a novel approach termed Entropy-scaled Steering vectors for Topic Maintenance (EnSToM). EnSToM dynamically adjusts the steering intensity based on input uncertainty, which allows the model to handle off-topic distractors effectively while preserving on-topic accuracy. Our experiments demonstrate that EnSToM achieves significant performance gain with a relatively small data size compared to fine-tuning approaches. By improving topic adherence without compromising efficiency, our approach provides a robust solution for enhancing sLLM-based dialogue systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EnSToM: Enhancing Dialogue Systems with Entropy-Scaled Steering Vectors for Topic Maintenance":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of maintaining topic consistency in task-oriented dialogue systems, especially when using small large language models (sLLMs) in resource-constrained environments. sLLMs often struggle to avoid off-topic or malicious inputs, hindering their reliability as service chatbots. To overcome this, the authors introduce EnSToM (Entropy-scaled Steering vectors for Topic Maintenance). EnSToM leverages activation addition and dynamically adjusts the steering vector's intensity based on the input's entropy (uncertainty). This allows the model to effectively handle off-topic inputs while preserving accuracy on in-topic queries. The authors conducted experiments on the CantTalkAboutThis dataset, demonstrating that EnSToM significantly improves topic adherence in task-oriented dialogues and analyzed entropy patterns in LLMs to inform the design of the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using entropy to scale steering vectors is relatively novel. While activation steering and prompt engineering are established techniques, the dynamic, entropy-aware adjustment is a significant addition.  The paper is grounded in the observation that entropy distributions differ between on-topic and off-topic queries, which is a sound and insightful basis for the method. A strength is its relative simplicity of implementation and lack of requirement of extra training.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from the growing need for efficient and reliable dialogue systems that can handle diverse user inputs while adhering to specific task constraints. sLLMs offer resource advantages, but their tendency to deviate from the topic poses a practical challenge. EnSToM directly addresses this by improving topic adherence without extensive fine-tuning or computationally expensive inference. If the method proves generalizable, it would have broad utility in the creation of service chatbots and assistants.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is lightweight and training-free, making it practical for resource-constrained environments.
    *   The paper provides a clear explanation of the approach, including the motivation behind entropy-based scaling.
    *   The experimental results on the CantTalkAboutThis dataset convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of EnSToM in improving distractor accuracy while minimizing the impact on on-topic accuracy.
    *   The analysis of entropy patterns in LLMs provides valuable insights into the model's internal mechanisms.
    *   Cross-domain generalization experiments and tests over different model architectures (Minstral) provide further support for the approach's wide applicability.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on manually selecting the entropy extraction layer and threshold. This may limit its ease of use and generalizability to new datasets or tasks. Automation of the optimal layer extraction would be useful.
    *   Although cross-domain applicability is tested, the dataset for that testing (banking domain) is very specific. A much more varied set of cross-domain tests would further increase its claim.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4o to classify model responses introduces a potential bias. A human evaluation might provide a more reliable assessment.
    *   The limitations section mentions that some samples produce results opposite to the intended effect. This should be investigated in more detail to understand the method's failure cases and potential mitigation strategies.
    *   A more detailed justification on choosing some variables (ex: Cmax) over others would be more helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** If EnSToM proves to be robust and generalizable, it could become a standard technique for improving topic adherence in dialogue systems using sLLMs. It could influence future research on activation engineering and model steering, encouraging the development of more adaptive and context-aware methods.

**Overall Assessment:**

EnSToM presents a valuable contribution to the field of dialogue systems, offering a practical and effective approach for enhancing topic consistency in sLLMs. While some limitations need to be addressed, the method's novelty, significance, and demonstrated performance make it a noteworthy advancement.

### Joint Relational Database Generation via Graph-Conditional Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16527v1)
- **Authors**: Mohamed Amine Ketata, David Lüdke, Leo Schwinn, Stephan Günnemann
- **Abstract**: Building generative models for relational databases (RDBs) is important for applications like privacy-preserving data release and augmenting real datasets. However, most prior work either focuses on single-table generation or relies on autoregressive factorizations that impose a fixed table order and generate tables sequentially. This approach limits parallelism, restricts flexibility in downstream applications like missing value imputation, and compounds errors due to commonly made conditional independence assumptions. We propose a fundamentally different approach: jointly modeling all tables in an RDB without imposing any order. By using a natural graph representation of RDBs, we propose the Graph-Conditional Relational Diffusion Model (GRDM). GRDM leverages a graph neural network to jointly denoise row attributes and capture complex inter-table dependencies. Extensive experiments on six real-world RDBs demonstrate that our approach substantially outperforms autoregressive baselines in modeling multi-hop inter-table correlations and achieves state-of-the-art performance on single-table fidelity metrics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Graph-Conditional Relational Diffusion Model (GRDM), a novel non-autoregressive generative model for relational databases (RDBs). Unlike prior work that relies on sequential table generation with a predefined order, GRDM jointly models all tables in an RDB using a graph-based representation where nodes are rows and edges represent primary-foreign key relationships. GRDM leverages graph neural networks (GNNs) within a diffusion model framework to jointly denoise row attributes, capturing complex inter-table dependencies without imposing any specific table ordering. The model preserves node degree distribution, and experiments on several real-world RDBs demonstrate that GRDM outperforms autoregressive baselines, especially in modeling multi-hop inter-table correlations while achieving state-of-the-art performance on single-table fidelity metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of this paper lies in proposing the *first non-autoregressive approach* for generating relational databases. This is a significant departure from existing methods, which are predominantly autoregressive and suffer from limitations related to table ordering, parallelization, and error compounding. Representing RDBs as graphs and using graph-conditional diffusion models is also novel within this specific context.

*   **Significance:** The shift towards non-autoregressive RDB generation is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Improved Parallelization:** It allows for parallel sampling, which can drastically speed up the data generation process, particularly for large databases.
    *   **Greater Flexibility:** Removing the fixed table order provides greater flexibility in downstream tasks such as missing value imputation, as tables can be accessed and modified concurrently.
    *   **Reduced Conditional Independence Assumptions:** Autoregressive models often rely on simplifying conditional independence assumptions that sacrifice fidelity. GRDM's joint modeling approach allows it to capture more complex dependencies.
    *   **Long-Range Dependency Modeling**: Empirically demonstrated to better capture high-order correlations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound technical approach:** The use of a graph representation and a diffusion model framework is well-motivated and theoretically sound.
    *   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods and motivates the need for a non-autoregressive approach.
    *   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The experiments are conducted on a variety of real-world datasets, and the results consistently demonstrate the superiority of GRDM over autoregressive baselines, particularly on long-range dependencies.
    *   **Detailed ablation studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of key design decisions, such as joint modeling and the number of hops.
    *   **Well-written and easy to follow:** The paper is well-organized and easy to understand, making it accessible to a broad audience.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Graph generation method simplification:** While effective, using node degree preservation may not fully capture inter-table structural characteristics. Other graph generation methods might be considered to improve graph construction and thus RDB fidelity.
    *   **Computational complexity for very large graphs:**  The scalability of graph neural networks (even with neighborhood sampling) to extremely large databases and graphs may still present a challenge and could be discussed more deeply.
    *   **Limited investigation of privacy guarantees:** While generative models can be used for privacy-preserving data release, the paper does not explicitly address privacy concerns or provide any theoretical privacy guarantees.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of relational database generation. It provides a new and promising direction for future research, potentially enabling the development of more efficient, flexible, and accurate generative models for RDBs. It could also inspire new applications of generative models in areas such as data augmentation, fairness, and robust downstream analysis. Furthermore, opens the door for conditional database generation.

*   **Conclusion:** GRDM offers a significant advancement in the area of relational data synthesis, addressing key limitations of existing autoregressive approaches. The empirical results robustly demonstrate the model’s superior ability to capture relational dependencies and accurately represent tabular distributions. The novelty in approach, combined with well-executed experimentation, marks GRDM as a noteworthy contribution. While some limitations regarding graph generation and scalability exist, the foundational advantages of the non-autoregressive structure combined with inter-table correlation handling make the work a strong offering.

### DuFFin: A Dual-Level Fingerprinting Framework for LLMs IP Protection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16530v1)
- **Authors**: Yuliang Yan, Haochun Tang, Shuo Yan, Enyan Dai
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are considered valuable Intellectual Properties (IP) for legitimate owners due to the enormous computational cost of training. It is crucial to protect the IP of LLMs from malicious stealing or unauthorized deployment. Despite existing efforts in watermarking and fingerprinting LLMs, these methods either impact the text generation process or are limited in white-box access to the suspect model, making them impractical. Hence, we propose DuFFin, a novel $\textbf{Du}$al-Level $\textbf{Fin}$gerprinting $\textbf{F}$ramework for black-box setting ownership verification. DuFFin extracts the trigger pattern and the knowledge-level fingerprints to identify the source of a suspect model. We conduct experiments on a variety of models collected from the open-source website, including four popular base models as protected LLMs and their fine-tuning, quantization, and safety alignment versions, which are released by large companies, start-ups, and individual users. Results show that our method can accurately verify the copyright of the base protected LLM on their model variants, achieving the IP-ROC metric greater than 0.95. Our code is available at https://github.com/yuliangyan0807/llm-fingerprint.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "DuFFin: A Dual-Level Fingerprinting Framework for LLMs IP Protection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DuFFin, a novel framework for protecting the intellectual property (IP) of Large Language Models (LLMs). DuFFin aims to address the challenge of identifying pirated LLMs in a black-box setting, where access to model parameters and intermediate layer outputs is restricted. The framework operates at two levels: 1) *Trigger-DuFFin*, which extracts fingerprints based on the model's responses to specific prompt triggers, and 2) *Knowledge-DuFFin*, which uses the model's answers to diverse knowledge questions as fingerprints. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of DuFFin in accurately verifying the copyright of base LLMs on their modified variants (fine-tuned, quantized, safety-aligned models) using experiments on open-source models, achieving high IP-ROC scores.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper proposes a dual-level approach, which is somewhat novel. The idea of using both prompt-based triggers and knowledge-based questions is an intuitive and potentially effective combination. The training method for the trigger-based fingerprint extractor to capture invariant patterns across fine-tuned versions also adds some novelty. The work addresses a practical problem with a system-level solution that aims to circumvent problems in other IP protection solutions.

**Significance:** IP protection for LLMs is becoming increasingly important as these models become more valuable and widely used. The black-box setting considered by DuFFin is particularly relevant as many real-world deployments restrict access to internal model states. The authors' experiments demonstrating high verification accuracy on a variety of pirated models highlight the practical significance of the framework. Successfully differentiating between pirated models and genuinely independent models is a crucial feature. The successful transfer of the fingerprint to unseen models points to the viability of the system.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** Addresses a timely and important problem in LLM security.
*   **Black-box Setting:** Considers a realistic deployment scenario.
*   **Dual-Level Approach:** Leverages both prompt-based and knowledge-based fingerprints for enhanced robustness.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** Demonstrates high IP-ROC scores on various pirated models.
*   **Transferability:** Shows some generalization ability to unseen LLMs.
*   **Robustness:** Demonstrates effectiveness against paraphrasing attacks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments primarily focus on specific types of model modifications (fine-tuning, quantization, safety alignment). The method should be evaluated against more sophisticated model stealing techniques or adversarial attacks on the fingerprint.
*   **Trigger Sensitivity:** While the paper addresses robustness to paraphrasing, it is unclear how sensitive the trigger-based fingerprints are to other variations in prompt design.  The selection of prompt triggers seems to rely on security and reasoning benchmarks.  It's unclear how effective this would be for models fine-tuned for completely different purposes. A systematic evaluation of trigger effectiveness could improve the method.
*   **Knowledge-Based fingerprint dependency on the dataset:** The knowledge questions are drawn from curated datasets, which may limit the generalizability of the knowledge-based fingerprint to domains not represented in the dataset. Also, the model's answer would depend on the domain knowledge captured while fine-tuning which differs from model to model, a more robust fingerprint would be ideal.
*   **Reproducibility:**  While the code is provided, full reproducibility will depend on access to the exact LLMs and datasets used in the experiments.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM security by providing a practical and effective approach for IP protection in black-box settings. The dual-level fingerprinting framework could inspire further research on more robust and generalizable IP protection techniques for LLMs.  However, the specific implementation details and reliance on training data (for the trigger-based fingerprint) may limit the immediate impact. The limitations identified above will need to be addressed to make the approach more widely applicable.

**Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the growing field of LLM IP protection. Its strength lies in the practicality of its black-box approach and the demonstration of its effectiveness against common model modifications.  However, the method is limited by its scope, reliance on data sets for knowledge assessment and its dependence on datasets to build the trigger, hence the trigger might be specific to certain base models. Furthermore, more complex model-stealing strategies or dedicated adversarial attacks against the fingerprint have not been addressed. Given these points, while representing a sound and practically relevant contribution, the novelty and the generalization ability of the approach are somewhat limited at this stage.

### Mechanistic Understanding and Mitigation of Language Confusion in English-Centric Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16538v1)
- **Authors**: Ercong Nie, Helmut Schmid, Hinrich Schütze
- **Abstract**: Language confusion -- where large language models (LLMs) generate unintended languages against the user's need -- remains a critical challenge, especially for English-centric models. We present the first mechanistic interpretability (MI) study of language confusion, combining behavioral benchmarking with neuron-level analysis. Using the Language Confusion Benchmark (LCB), we show that confusion points (CPs) -- specific positions where language switches occur -- are central to this phenomenon. Through layer-wise analysis with TunedLens and targeted neuron attribution, we reveal that transition failures in the final layers drive confusion. We further demonstrate that editing a small set of critical neurons, identified via comparative analysis with multilingual-tuned models, substantially mitigates confusion without harming general competence or fluency. Our approach matches multilingual alignment in confusion reduction for most languages and yields cleaner, higher-quality outputs. These findings provide new insights into the internal dynamics of LLMs and highlight neuron-level interventions as a promising direction for robust, interpretable multilingual language modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates language confusion in English-centric large language models (LLMs), where the models unintentionally switch to other languages despite instructions to the contrary. It employs mechanistic interpretability (MI) techniques to understand the internal mechanisms driving this phenomenon. The study identifies "confusion points" (CPs) as critical junctures where language switches occur. Layer-wise analysis using TunedLens reveals that transition failures in the final layers contribute significantly to confusion. Further, the research pinpoints specific neurons involved in these failures. The paper proposes a neuron editing strategy, selectively deactivating a small set of neurons identified through comparative analysis with multilingual-tuned models, to mitigate language confusion. This editing strategy effectively reduces confusion without significantly degrading the model's overall performance (fluency and general competence).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its application of mechanistic interpretability (MI) to the problem of language confusion in LLMs. While MI techniques have been applied to various aspects of LLMs, their use to diagnose and address language confusion is novel.  The identification of CPs and the layer-wise analysis highlighting late-layer transition failures are valuable insights. The neuron editing approach is also a new mitigation strategy.
*   **Significance:** Language confusion is a significant practical problem, especially for users of English-centric models who require outputs in other languages. Mitigating this issue increases the reliability and usability of LLMs in multilingual settings. The neuron editing approach provides a potentially more targeted and efficient solution compared to large-scale data-driven methods such as multilingual fine-tuning. This could have implications for resource-constrained scenarios.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper combines behavioral benchmarking (LCB) with detailed neuron-level analysis, providing a more comprehensive understanding than surface-level approaches.
    *   The use of TunedLens and neuron attribution techniques allows for a granular understanding of the internal dynamics leading to language confusion.
    *   The neuron editing strategy offers a novel, model-internal solution that mitigates confusion while preserving general competence.
    *   The comparison with multilingual-tuned models contextualizes the effectiveness of neuron editing and reveals potential advantages (cleaner outputs).
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The study focuses primarily on English-centric LLMs and the monolingual setting of the Language Confusion Benchmark (LCB).  It is unclear how well the findings generalize to truly multilingual models or cross-lingual tasks.
    *   The neuron editing strategy is evaluated on a relatively small set of tasks and metrics. Further validation across broader domains and model architectures is needed.
    *   While the paper identifies specific neurons, a more in-depth understanding of *why* these neurons are implicated in language confusion would strengthen the work.  What is the semantic or functional role of these neurons, and how is their activity related to language transition failures?
    * The paper mentions that the work is conducted in accordance with the ACM Code of Ethics. The statement is rather general and lacks specific justifications.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence future research in several ways:

    *   Encourage further investigation of MI techniques for diagnosing and addressing specific limitations of LLMs.
    *   Inspire the development of more targeted and efficient mitigation strategies based on neuron-level interventions.
    *   Shift the focus from solely data-driven approaches to incorporate model-internal mechanisms for improving multilingual capabilities.
    *   Promote a more nuanced understanding of the internal dynamics of LLMs and how they process and transfer information across languages.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by applying MI to address a practical problem in LLMs. The findings are novel and offer a promising direction for improving multilingual capabilities. However, the limitations discussed above, including the limited scope and the lack of deeper mechanistic insights, prevent it from receiving a higher score. A score of 7 reflects the paper's significant contribution and potential influence, balanced by its existing limitations.

### Towards Coordinate- and Dimension-Agnostic Machine Learning for Partial Differential Equations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16549v1)
- **Authors**: Trung V. Phan, George A. Kevrekidis, Soledad Villar, Yannis G. Kevrekidis, Juan M. Bello-Rivas
- **Abstract**: The machine learning methods for data-driven identification of partial differential equations (PDEs) are typically defined for a given number of spatial dimensions and a choice of coordinates the data have been collected in. This dependence prevents the learned evolution equation from generalizing to other spaces. In this work, we reformulate the problem in terms of coordinate- and dimension-independent representations, paving the way toward what we call ``spatially liberated" PDE learning. To this end, we employ a machine learning approach to predict the evolution of scalar field systems expressed in the formalism of exterior calculus, which is coordinate-free and immediately generalizes to arbitrary dimensions by construction. We demonstrate the performance of this approach in the FitzHugh-Nagumo and Barkley reaction-diffusion models, as well as the Patlak-Keller-Segel model informed by in-situ chemotactic bacteria observations. We provide extensive numerical experiments that demonstrate that our approach allows for seamless transitions across various spatial contexts. We show that the field dynamics learned in one space can be used to make accurate predictions in other spaces with different dimensions, coordinate systems, boundary conditions, and curvatures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel machine learning approach for identifying and modeling partial differential equations (PDEs) that aims to overcome the limitations of traditional methods tied to specific coordinate systems and spatial dimensions.  The key idea is to reformulate the problem using coordinate- and dimension-independent representations based on exterior calculus and differential forms.  The approach leverages the fact that certain PDEs can be expressed in a coordinate-free manner, allowing a machine-learned model to generalize to different geometries, dimensions, boundary conditions, and even curvatures. The authors train neural networks on data generated from simple one-dimensional simulations and demonstrate their ability to accurately predict the dynamics of the same systems in higher dimensions (2D and 3D) and curved spaces using the FitzHugh-Nagumo, Barkley, and a modified Patlak-Keller-Segel models as examples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper presents a significant step forward in machine learning for PDEs. The traditional coordinate-dependence of data-driven PDE discovery is a major limitation, hindering the ability to apply learned models to systems with different geometries or dimensions. The use of exterior calculus to achieve coordinate- and dimension-agnostic PDE learning is a clever and well-motivated approach. The idea of "spatial liberation" of PDE learning is compelling and could potentially have a broad impact on the field.

*Strengths:*

*   **Sound Mathematical Foundation:** The paper is grounded in a strong mathematical foundation using exterior calculus and differential forms to represent PDEs in a coordinate-free manner.
*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The authors clearly articulate the limitations of existing PDE learning approaches and the need for coordinate- and dimension-agnostic methods.
*   **Convincing Empirical Results:** The authors provide extensive numerical experiments demonstrating the effectiveness of their approach on several well-known PDEs. The experiments cover various scenarios, including higher dimensions, different coordinate systems, and curved spaces, which provides compelling evidence for the generalizability of the method. The inclusion of models derived from real biological experiments (bacterial chemotaxis) further bolsters the practical significance.
*   **Rigorous Comparison:** The 'true' and 'learned' systems are compared and evaluated, using MSE to give a sense of performance.
*   **Well-Structured and Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making the technical concepts accessible.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope:** The approach is currently limited to systems of scalar fields governed by PDEs that are invariant under local orthogonal transformations. While this covers a broad class of PDEs, it excludes more complex systems involving vector or tensor fields or anisotropic effects.
*   **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't provide detailed information on the computational cost associated with training and deploying the models, which could be a concern for more complex systems. Scalability to more complex, turbulent, or stochastic systems could pose challenges.
*   **Reliance on Symmetry:** The approach relies heavily on orthogonal symmetry. While common in many physical systems, this reliance may restrict the applicability of the method to situations lacking such symmetry. The paper could be strengthened by addressing how the absence or breakdown of such symmetry might impact the performance.
*   **Second Order Derivative Limit:**  The approach, in this work, is limited to second-order PDEs. The authors state future work can extend the invariant features beyond second-order.
*   **Practicality:** The appendix material gives a sense of the initial conditions and parameter choice. It isn't clear how easy this is to set up in new geometries for new PDEs.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of data-driven PDE discovery by enabling more general and robust models that can be applied across different spatial contexts. It opens up new avenues for integrating experimental data from diverse sources and geometries into predictive models. The spatial-liberation concept is particularly impactful, allowing simulations in settings that could be complex for traditional methods.

*Score Justification:*

Given its strong mathematical foundation, convincing empirical results, and potential for broad impact, the paper is a significant contribution. The limitations related to symmetry and computational cost somewhat temper its immediate practical applicability. The clarity of the writing and comprehensive set of experiments further justify the below score.

### Think Silently, Think Fast: Dynamic Latent Compression of LLM Reasoning Chains
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16552v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhui Tan, Jiaze Li, Jianzhong Ju, Zhenbo Luo, Jian Luan, Ruihua Song
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve superior performance through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, but these token-level reasoning chains are computationally expensive and inefficient. In this paper, we introduce Compressed Latent Reasoning (CoLaR), a novel framework that dynamically compresses reasoning processes in latent space through a two-stage training approach. First, during supervised fine-tuning, CoLaR extends beyond next-token prediction by incorporating an auxiliary next compressed embedding prediction objective. This process merges embeddings of consecutive tokens using a compression factor randomly sampled from a predefined range, and trains a specialized latent head to predict distributions of subsequent compressed embeddings. Second, we enhance CoLaR through reinforcement learning (RL) that leverages the latent head's non-deterministic nature to explore diverse reasoning paths and exploit more compact ones. This approach enables CoLaR to: i) perform reasoning at a dense latent level (i.e., silently), substantially reducing reasoning chain length, and ii) dynamically adjust reasoning speed at inference time by simply prompting the desired compression factor. Extensive experiments across four mathematical reasoning datasets demonstrate that CoLaR achieves 14.1% higher accuracy than latent-based baseline methods at comparable compression ratios, and reduces reasoning chain length by 53.3% with only 4.8% performance degradation compared to explicit CoT method. Moreover, when applied to more challenging mathematical reasoning tasks, our RL-enhanced CoLaR demonstrates performance gains of up to 5.4% while dramatically reducing latent reasoning chain length by 82.8%. The code and models will be released upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think Silently, Think Fast: Dynamic Latent Compression of LLM Reasoning Chains":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Compressed Latent Reasoning (CoLaR), a novel framework designed to improve the efficiency of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs).  CoLaR dynamically compresses reasoning chains into a latent space, significantly reducing the number of tokens that need to be processed. It employs a two-stage training approach:  (1) supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with an auxiliary objective that predicts compressed embeddings of consecutive tokens, and (2) reinforcement learning (RL) to explore diverse and more compact reasoning paths leveraging a probabilistic latent head. The compression factor is dynamically adjusted at inference time via prompting. Experiments demonstrate that CoLaR achieves higher accuracy than existing latent-based methods with comparable compression ratios and can significantly reduce reasoning chain length with minimal performance degradation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper exhibits moderate novelty. Compressing reasoning chains and operating in latent space isn't entirely new (Coconut and CODI are cited). However, CoLaR innovates through several key aspects:
*   **Dynamic compression:** The ability to control the compression factor at inference time, enabling speed-accuracy trade-offs via prompting, provides a level of flexibility not readily found in previous methods.
*   **Probabilistic latent head and RL:** The use of a probabilistic latent head, combined with reinforcement learning, to explore different reasoning paths and exploit shorter ones, represents a departure from deterministic latent reasoning approaches.  This is significant as it introduces an exploration-exploitation mechanism within the latent reasoning space.
*   **Next compressed embedding prediction loss:** Their loss, that trains CoLaR to predict all r consecutive tokens, approximates multi-label classification, and improves performance when compared to just supervising for final answer.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper lies in its potential to address a crucial bottleneck in LLM deployment: the computational cost of lengthy reasoning chains. The gains in efficiency, coupled with the maintained or even improved accuracy, could have a substantial impact on real-world applications where LLM inference is a major cost factor. The results on challenging datasets like MATH are particularly compelling. However, there are caveats:
*   The paper clearly states its limitation that its CoLaR performance does not surpass explicit CoT performance.
*   Generalization to non-integer compression factors remains a challenge.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and well-structured:**  The paper is well-written, clearly explaining the CoLaR framework and the motivation behind its design choices.
*   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experimental setup is thorough, with evaluations across multiple datasets and ablation studies that analyze the contributions of different components.
*   **Strong results:** The quantitative results demonstrate clear improvements over existing latent-based baselines and show the effectiveness of dynamic compression and reinforcement learning.
*   **Detailed implementation information:** The paper provides comprehensive details regarding the model architecture, training hyperparameters, and dataset specifications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Base Model Dependence:** The paper acknowledges the dependency on base model quality for effective RL. This indicates that the RL component is not a magic bullet and requires a strong initial foundation.
*   **Generalization Limitations:** The difficulty in generalizing to non-integer compression factors or factors beyond the training range is a notable limitation, restricting the flexibility of the approach.
*   **Limited Improvement on GSM Datasets:** Applying RL to GSM datasets didn't result in substantial gains. This suggests that the exploration and exploitation encouraged by RL might be less relevant for simpler math problems.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence future research on efficient LLM reasoning. The dynamic compression approach and the use of reinforcement learning in latent space could inspire new methods for optimizing LLM inference. The code and models released will aid reproducibility.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a moderately novel approach to a significant problem (efficient LLM reasoning). It demonstrates clear performance improvements over existing latent-based methods through a well-designed framework and comprehensive experiments. The introduction of dynamic compression and reinforcement learning in latent space is a valuable contribution.  However, the limitations regarding generalization to non-integer compression factors and complete dependence on the base model, along with the fact that it does not surpass explicit CoT performance, prevent it from achieving a higher score. It has strong practical value for reducing costs in many real-world applications but is not a groundbreaking theoretical advance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CTRAP: Embedding Collapse Trap to Safeguard Large Language Models from Harmful Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16559v1)
- **Authors**: Biao Yi, Tiansheng Huang, Baolei Zhang, Tong Li, Lihai Nie, Zheli Liu, Li Shen
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning-as-a-service, while commercially successful for Large Language Model (LLM) providers, exposes models to harmful fine-tuning attacks. As a widely explored defense paradigm against such attacks, unlearning attempts to remove malicious knowledge from LLMs, thereby essentially preventing them from being used to perform malicious tasks. However, we highlight a critical flaw: the powerful general adaptability of LLMs allows them to easily bypass selective unlearning by rapidly relearning or repurposing their capabilities for harmful tasks. To address this fundamental limitation, we propose a paradigm shift: instead of selective removal, we advocate for inducing model collapse--effectively forcing the model to "unlearn everything"--specifically in response to updates characteristic of malicious adaptation. This collapse directly neutralizes the very general capabilities that attackers exploit, tackling the core issue unaddressed by selective unlearning. We introduce the Collapse Trap (CTRAP) as a practical mechanism to implement this concept conditionally. Embedded during alignment, CTRAP pre-configures the model's reaction to subsequent fine-tuning dynamics. If updates during fine-tuning constitute a persistent attempt to reverse safety alignment, the pre-configured trap triggers a progressive degradation of the model's core language modeling abilities, ultimately rendering it inert and useless for the attacker. Crucially, this collapse mechanism remains dormant during benign fine-tuning, ensuring the model's utility and general capabilities are preserved for legitimate users. Extensive empirical results demonstrate that CTRAP effectively counters harmful fine-tuning risks across various LLMs and attack settings, while maintaining high performance in benign scenarios. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CTRAP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to harmful fine-tuning attacks, particularly in the context of fine-tuning-as-a-service. It argues that existing unlearning methods, which selectively remove malicious knowledge, are insufficient because LLMs possess a strong general adaptability that allows them to quickly relearn or repurpose their capabilities for harmful tasks. To combat this, the paper proposes a new paradigm: inducing model collapse conditionally.  The core idea is to embed a "Collapse Trap" (CTRAP) during the alignment phase.  CTRAP pre-configures the model to degrade its core language modeling abilities if it detects persistent attempts to reverse safety alignment during subsequent fine-tuning.  Crucially, CTRAP remains dormant during benign fine-tuning, preserving the model's utility for legitimate users. The paper presents empirical results across multiple LLMs and attack settings, demonstrating that CTRAP effectively counters harmful fine-tuning risks while maintaining performance in benign scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its paradigm shift from selective unlearning to conditional model collapse.  While unlearning is an established area, the argument that LLM's general adaptability undermines these methods is well-articulated.  The concept of inducing collapse specifically in response to malicious adaptation, rather than trying to pinpoint and remove specific knowledge, is a significant contribution.  The CTRAP mechanism itself, embedding the collapse trigger during alignment, is a novel technical implementation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the practical problem it addresses: securing LLMs in the increasingly common fine-tuning-as-a-service setting. Harmful fine-tuning attacks are a serious concern, and the paper offers a scalable defense mechanism that can be integrated into the foundation model.  The results suggest a substantial improvement over existing methods, potentially providing a more robust and reliable way to safeguard LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and motivates the limitations of existing unlearning approaches.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The concept of conditional model collapse is a novel and potentially powerful defense mechanism.
    *   **Solid Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive empirical results across multiple LLMs, attack settings, and baselines.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The work addresses a real-world problem with practical implications for LLM providers.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Overhead Analysis:** The results show a computational overhead (memory and training time) compared with SFT. While the text argues that this is a one-time expense, the relative additional cost should be explicitly stated in terms of a percentage and justified that the marginal benefits outweigh the overhead.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions the importance of tuning hyperparameters λ and α for CTRAP (see Table 5 and Table 6), but does not provide enough detail on how to select the best hyperparameters. There needs to be more discussion on robustness with different hyperparameters values.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM security. It offers a practical and effective defense mechanism against a growing threat. However, the limitations regarding hyperparameter selection affect its applicability and the overhead analysis results require further justification.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### ScholarBench: A Bilingual Benchmark for Abstraction, Comprehension, and Reasoning Evaluation in Academic Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16566v1)
- **Authors**: Dongwon Noh, Donghyeok Koh, Junghun Yuk, Gyuwan Kim, Jaeyong Lee, Kyungtae Lim, Cheoneum Park
- **Abstract**: Prior benchmarks for evaluating the domain-specific knowledge of large language models (LLMs) lack the scalability to handle complex academic tasks. To address this, we introduce \texttt{ScholarBench}, a benchmark centered on deep expert knowledge and complex academic problem-solving, which evaluates the academic reasoning ability of LLMs and is constructed through a three-step process. \texttt{ScholarBench} targets more specialized and logically complex contexts derived from academic literature, encompassing five distinct problem types. Unlike prior benchmarks, \texttt{ScholarBench} evaluates the abstraction, comprehension, and reasoning capabilities of LLMs across eight distinct research domains. To ensure high-quality evaluation data, we define category-specific example attributes and design questions that are aligned with the characteristic research methodologies and discourse structures of each domain. Additionally, this benchmark operates as an English-Korean bilingual dataset, facilitating simultaneous evaluation for linguistic capabilities of LLMs in both languages. The benchmark comprises 5,031 examples in Korean and 5,309 in English, with even state-of-the-art models like o3-mini achieving an average evaluation score of only 0.543, demonstrating the challenging nature of this benchmark.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can do that. Here's a concise summary, rigorous critical evaluation, and score for the provided paper information:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScholarBench, a new bilingual (English-Korean) benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the abstraction, comprehension, and reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in academic contexts. ScholarBench aims to address the lack of scalable benchmarks for evaluating domain-specific knowledge and complex problem-solving in LLMs. The benchmark comprises five distinct problem types across eight academic domains and includes fine-grained attributes to capture diverse question types. The authors demonstrate the challenging nature of the benchmark by evaluating several state-of-the-art LLMs, which achieve relatively low scores.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focus on evaluating LLMs' reasoning abilities within specialized academic domains and its bilingual (English-Korean) construction. Existing benchmarks often focus on general knowledge or STEM disciplines, making ScholarBench a valuable addition to the field. The effort to create fine-grained attributes and aligning questions with research methodologies in different domains is also a strength.

*   **Significance:** ScholarBench's significance stems from its potential to:

    *   **Drive LLM Improvement:** By highlighting weaknesses in academic reasoning, the benchmark can guide future LLM development and tuning strategies.
    *   **Promote Domain-Specific Applications:** A more nuanced understanding of LLM capabilities in specific academic fields can facilitate their adoption in research, education, and specialized tasks.
    *   **Enable Cross-Lingual Analysis:** The bilingual nature of the benchmark allows for the assessment of cross-lingual knowledge transfer and multilingual understanding in LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Scope:** The paper clearly outlines the motivation, design, and construction of ScholarBench.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The benchmark covers a range of problem types and academic domains, providing a holistic assessment of LLM capabilities.
    *   **Rigorous Construction:** The paper details the data collection, annotation, and validation processes, ensuring the quality and reliability of the benchmark.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The experimental results are thoroughly analyzed, revealing insights into the strengths and weaknesses of various LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Data Modality:** The benchmark primarily relies on paragraph text, neglecting other modalities (e.g., figures, tables) commonly found in academic papers.
    *   **Focus on Existing Literature:** While focusing on academic knowledge, it is limited to scholarly articles. Real scholarly papers, beyond plain text, contain information across diverse modalities such as figures, tables, algorithms, and diagrams, which are often essential for understanding the research content.
    *   **English-Centric Bias:** Despite the inclusion of Korean, the benchmark's design and evaluation may be biased towards English-language academic conventions. This has been shown to be a major problem when attempting to adapt these models for other language.

*   **Potential Influence:** ScholarBench has the potential to become a valuable resource for the LLM research community, fostering the development of more capable and reliable AI systems for academic applications. Its fine-grained evaluations and cross-lingual capabilities can also contribute to a more nuanced understanding of LLM strengths and weaknesses.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-designed benchmark that addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs' academic reasoning abilities. The detailed construction process, comprehensive evaluation, and insightful analysis contribute significantly to the field. However, the limitations related to data modality and potential English-centric bias prevent it from achieving a higher score.
