- **Score**: 7/10

### Finetuning-Activated Backdoors in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16567v1)
- **Authors**: Thibaud Gloaguen, Mark Vero, Robin Staab, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: Finetuning openly accessible Large Language Models (LLMs) has become standard practice for achieving task-specific performance improvements. Until now, finetuning has been regarded as a controlled and secure process in which training on benign datasets led to predictable behaviors. In this paper, we demonstrate for the first time that an adversary can create poisoned LLMs that initially appear benign but exhibit malicious behaviors once finetuned by downstream users. To this end, our proposed attack, FAB (Finetuning-Activated Backdoor), poisons an LLM via meta-learning techniques to simulate downstream finetuning, explicitly optimizing for the emergence of malicious behaviors in the finetuned models. At the same time, the poisoned LLM is regularized to retain general capabilities and to exhibit no malicious behaviors prior to finetuning. As a result, when users finetune the seemingly benign model on their own datasets, they unknowingly trigger its hidden backdoor behavior. We demonstrate the effectiveness of FAB across multiple LLMs and three target behaviors: unsolicited advertising, refusal, and jailbreakability. Additionally, we show that FAB-backdoors are robust to various finetuning choices made by the user (e.g., dataset, number of steps, scheduler). Our findings challenge prevailing assumptions about the security of finetuning, revealing yet another critical attack vector exploiting the complexities of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Finetuning-Activated Backdoors in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack called Finetuning-Activated Backdoor (FAB) on Large Language Models (LLMs).  FAB involves poisoning an LLM during a meta-learning phase, simulating downstream finetuning, to embed malicious behavior that remains dormant until a user fine-tunes the model on their own (benign) dataset. The poisoned LLM retains general capabilities and exhibits no malicious behavior before finetuning. After a user fine-tunes the model, however, the embedded backdoor is activated, causing the model to exhibit undesirable behaviors like advertising injection, informative refusal, and jailbreakability.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of FAB across different LLMs and target behaviors, showing the backdoor is robust to a variety of user finetuning choices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution is the concept of *finetuning as a trigger* for backdoors in LLMs. This is a significant departure from traditional backdoor attacks that rely on poisoned *input data* to activate malicious behavior. The idea that a seemingly safe process like finetuning on a clean dataset can inadvertently activate a pre-existing backdoor is genuinely novel. This significantly broadens the threat landscape. The use of meta-learning to achieve this, by optimizing for behavior during simulated finetuning, is also a technically sound approach.

*   **Significance:** The implications of this research are significant for several reasons:

    *   **Breaches Trust in Finetuning:**  Finetuning is a cornerstone of LLM customization and deployment.  FAB undermines the assumption that finetuning provides users with full control and security.  It highlights the need for much greater scrutiny of pre-trained models before finetuning.
    *   **Practical Threat Model:** The threat model is realistic.  Adversaries don't need access to the finetuning dataset, just the initial base model. This bypasses defenses that focus on detecting poisoned *finetuning* data.
    *   **Wide Range of Attack Vectors:** The paper demonstrates FAB's effectiveness in different adversarial scenarios (advertising, refusal, jailbreaking) demonstrating the versatility of the attack. The robustness study further increases the concern as the backdoors are effective with a wide variety of finetuning configurations.
    *   **Raises Awareness:** This work serves as an important call to action for researchers to develop defenses and mitigations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the threat model and the technical challenges.
    *   **Technically Sound Approach:**  The use of meta-learning and noise injection seems well justified and effective.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experiments are well-designed, covering multiple models, attack scenarios, and finetuning configurations. The ablation studies are particularly valuable for understanding the different components of FAB.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide code and detailed experimental settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of Meta-Learning:** FAB relies on a meta-learning stage, which is inherently more computationally expensive than regular finetuning. This may limit the attack's scalability to much larger models (the paper focuses on ≤3B parameter models). This is acknowledged in the paper.
    *   **Reliance on specific parameters, datasets, and loss functions:** While robustness against user finetuning config is shown, some of the effectiveness of FAB depends on carefully chosen parameters and loss functions during the poisoning stage. It might need adjustments for different models or behaviours. The paper also acknowledges this.
    *   **Limited exploration of alternative triggers:** The paper focuses on supervised finetuning, but other adaptation methods (e.g., reinforcement learning, distillation) could also be potential triggers.

*   **Impact on the Field:** The paper will likely spur significant research into defending against backdoor attacks activated by downstream actions like finetuning.  It also highlights the need for better methods for verifying the integrity of pre-trained models.

* **Score**: 8.  The paper is a significant contribution to the field of LLM security. The novelty of using finetuning as a backdoor trigger is high, and the potential impact on the security and trustworthiness of LLMs is considerable. While the method may have limitations in terms of scalability and the need for careful configuration, the paper convincingly demonstrates the feasibility and robustness of the attack, making it an important and timely piece of research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 0/10

### URLs Help, Topics Guide: Understanding Metadata Utility in LLM Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16570v1)
- **Authors**: Dongyang Fan, Vinko Sabolčec, Martin Jaggi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are commonly pretrained on vast corpora of text without utilizing contextual metadata such as source, quality, or topic, leading to a context-free learning paradigm. While recent studies suggest that adding metadata like URL information as context (i.e., auxiliary inputs not used in the loss calculation) can improve training efficiency and downstream performance, they offer limited understanding of which types of metadata are truly effective and under what conditions. In this work, we conduct a systematic evaluation and find that not all metadata types contribute equally. Only URL context speeds up training, whereas quality scores and topic/format domain information offer no clear benefit. Furthermore, the improved downstream performances of URL conditioning emerge only when longer prompts are used at inference time. In addition, we demonstrate that context-aware pretraining enables more controllable generation than context-free pretraining, in a classifier-free guidance fashion. Although topic and format metadata do not accelerate training, they are effective for steering outputs, offering human-interpretable control over generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the utility of different types of metadata during the pretraining of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It challenges the common practice of context-free pretraining by systematically evaluating the impact of including metadata like URLs, quality scores, and topic/format information as context during training. The key findings are: (1) Only URL context significantly accelerates pretraining.  (2) URL-conditioned pretraining benefits downstream performance primarily with longer prompts. (3) Metadata-conditioned pretrained models enable more controllable generation than their context-free counterparts. While topic and format domains do not accelerate training, they offer effective and human-interpretable ways to steer generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a systematic empirical evaluation of different metadata types. Prior works had focused primarily on URLs.  The investigation into quality scores and topic/format information, and the finding that only URLs significantly speed up pretraining, contributes a more nuanced understanding. The discovery that URL conditioning only improves downstream performance on longer prompts, confirming prior findings of Higuchi et al.[4], is also valuable.  The use of context-aware pretraining for controllable generation, while drawing from classifier-free guidance, is a novel application of metadata conditioning. It combines the ideas of URL conditioning and controllable generation.

*   **Significance:** The work has several potential impacts:
    *   **More Efficient Pretraining:** The finding that URLs accelerate pretraining suggests a way to reduce computational costs for large models.
    *   **Improved Model Control:** The demonstrated controllability using topic and format metadata can lead to LLMs that are more tailored to specific tasks and user preferences.
    *   **Informing Future Research:** The empirical analysis helps guide future research towards identifying and leveraging the most effective types of metadata for LLM pretraining.
    *   **More Human-Aligned Language Model Applications:** the authors suggest the insight helps build more flexible and human-aligned language model applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:**  The paper performs a thorough evaluation of different metadata types.
    *   **Real-world Dataset:**  The use of the FineWeb-Edu dataset adds practical relevance to the findings.
    *   **Clear Results:** The key findings are well-supported by the experimental results.
    *   **Controllable steering:** The authors point out how certain types of metadata like Topic and format domains provides human-interpretable steering possibilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model and Dataset Limitations:** The experiments are conducted with a 1.5B parameter Llama model, the findings might not generalize to bigger models. The 100B Fineweb-Edu might not be the perfect reflection of current internet data composition.
    *   **Lack of Mechanistic Explanation:** While the paper identifies the utility of URLs, it doesn't fully explain *why* URLs are so effective. The paper admits that the mechanics behind the improved performance brought by URL conditioning are still unclear.
    *   **Limited scope of context-guided generation tests:** The tests of the controllable generation are qualitative and rely on LLM as Judge. This raises some concerns regarding potential biases.

*   **Critical considerations for the score:** The experimental setups of prior studies are built up on to make this study. The results of prior studies could not be fully reproduced either. The controllability of the steering part is not fully investigated yet.

*   **Influence on the field:** This paper highlights the need to investigate activations between context-free and context-conditioned pretrained models to understand the utility of URLs, topic information, and format domains.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a valuable, systematic investigation into metadata conditioning for LLMs. The finding that URLs accelerate pretraining and the demonstration of controllable generation contribute meaningfully to the field. The impact of this work will grow and promote more research into the investigation of activations between different types of metadata and what the model actually learns. However, the model and dataset limitations, as well as the lack of full mechanistic explanation, prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work worthy of a higher score. Future work will build on the groundwork established here.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Model-Empowered Interactive Load Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16577v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Zuo, Dalin Qin, Yi Wang
- **Abstract**: The growing complexity of power systems has made accurate load forecasting more important than ever. An increasing number of advanced load forecasting methods have been developed. However, the static design of current methods offers no mechanism for human-model interaction. As the primary users of forecasting models, system operators often find it difficult to understand and apply these advanced models, which typically requires expertise in artificial intelligence (AI). This also prevents them from incorporating their experience and real-world contextual understanding into the forecasting process. Recent breakthroughs in large language models (LLMs) offer a new opportunity to address this issue. By leveraging their natural language understanding and reasoning capabilities, we propose an LLM-based multi-agent collaboration framework to bridge the gap between human operators and forecasting models. A set of specialized agents is designed to perform different tasks in the forecasting workflow and collaborate via a dedicated communication mechanism. This framework embeds interactive mechanisms throughout the load forecasting pipeline, reducing the technical threshold for non-expert users and enabling the integration of human experience. Our experiments demonstrate that the interactive load forecasting accuracy can be significantly improved when users provide proper insight in key stages. Our cost analysis shows that the framework remains affordable, making it practical for real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges faced by system operators in utilizing advanced load forecasting models due to their complexity and lack of human-model interaction. It proposes an LLM-based multi-agent collaboration framework to bridge this gap. The framework consists of specialized agents for different tasks (preparation, model management, development, deployment) communicating via a message system and interacting with users in natural language. Experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that incorporating user insights can improve forecasting accuracy. The paper also includes a cost analysis to assess the feasibility of the framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to create an interactive load forecasting system has merit, but the overall novelty is somewhat limited. While it integrates recent advances in LLMs and multi-agent systems, the specific implementation and the agent roles, while functional, do not appear particularly groundbreaking. The individual components (Bayesian Optimization, post-processing steps) are well-established. The novelty primarily lies in the **integration and adaptation** of these components within an interactive LLM-driven framework tailored for load forecasting. However, similar papers leveraging LLMs for related tasks in the energy domain are emerging, reducing its relative novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to democratize access to advanced load forecasting techniques and improve their practical applicability. By lowering the technical barrier and enabling users to inject domain knowledge, the framework can lead to more accurate forecasts tailored to specific operational contexts. The demonstration of improved forecasting accuracy through user interaction provides tangible evidence of the benefits. The cost analysis further strengthens the significance by showing the framework is affordable. This interactive approach to model building and deployment is a valuable paradigm shift for the field of power systems, which is traditionally more focused on static, pre-defined models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with specialized agents.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on real-world datasets.
    *   Demonstration of improved accuracy through user interaction.
    *   Cost analysis addressing practical feasibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The design of agent's workflows seems rigidly defined based on profile, memory, and actions. More flexibility might be beneficial in practical applications.
    *   While the architecture is sound, specific agent functionalities appear more like software engineering implementation. It would be valuable to see more detail on how LLMs are actually *reasoning* and contributing beyond orchestration.
    *   The explanation of how the human user guides the exploration or tunes hyperparameters from the observation of the visualization tool requires clarification.
    *   The user involvement in the experiment setting is restricted.
    *   The postprocessing section is a little simple.
    *   The cost analysis is limited to token count. Analysis of latency and system resource usage would improve the discussion.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting a more user-centric approach to load forecasting and encouraging further research into interactive AI systems for power systems. The framework can serve as a template for developing similar systems in other domains. The results may also inform the design of more intuitive interfaces for complex AI models.

**Overall:** The paper presents a useful and practical application of LLMs to address a relevant problem in the power systems domain. While the individual components lack significant novelty, the integration within an interactive framework demonstrates a valuable approach to improve load forecasting accuracy and usability. The significance is bolstered by the experimental results and cost analysis. There are opportunities to strengthen the paper by detailing the inner workings of the agents' reasoning, providing more insight of the user guiding the workflow, and giving more freedom to the user's action in the experiment.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Bridging the Dynamic Perception Gap: Training-Free Draft Chain-of-Thought for Dynamic Multimodal Spatial Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16579v1)
- **Authors**: Siqu Ou, Hongcheng Liu, Pingjie Wang, Yusheng Liao, Chuan Xuan, Yanfeng Wang, Yu Wang
- **Abstract**: While chains-of-thought (CoT) have advanced complex reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), existing methods remain confined to text or static visual domains, often faltering in dynamic spatial reasoning tasks. To bridge this gap, we present GRASSLAND, a novel maze navigation benchmark designed to evaluate dynamic spatial reasoning. Our experiments show that augmenting textual reasoning chains with dynamic visual drafts, overlaid on input images, significantly outperforms conventional approaches, offering new insights into spatial reasoning in evolving environments. To generalize this capability, we propose D2R (Dynamic Draft-Augmented Reasoning), a training-free framework that seamlessly integrates textual CoT with corresponding visual drafts into MLLMs. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that D2R consistently enhances performance across diverse tasks, establishing a robust baseline for dynamic spatial reasoning without requiring model fine-tuning. Project is open at https://github.com/Cratileo/D2R.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GRASSLAND, a novel maze navigation benchmark for evaluating dynamic spatial reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  It highlights the limitations of existing approaches, which often struggle with dynamic visual environments. To address this, the authors propose Draft Chain-of-Thought (Draft CoT), augmenting textual reasoning with dynamic visual drafts overlaid on input images. Further, to improve the broad applicability, they develop a training-free framework called Dynamic Draft Augmented Reasoning (D2R), which integrates visual and textual inputs, generating visual drafts to enhance MLLMs' reasoning abilities without requiring fine-tuning. Experimental results demonstrate D2R's effectiveness across diverse tasks in the GRASSLAND environment, surpassing existing methods and establishing a robust baseline for dynamic spatial reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a new benchmark (GRASSLAND) specifically designed to assess dynamic spatial reasoning capabilities in MLLMs. This is a valuable contribution, as existing benchmarks often focus on static visual scenes or primarily textual reasoning.  The D2R framework, which leverages visual drafts to augment reasoning without training, is also novel. While Chain-of-Thought and visual prompting techniques exist, the explicit combination of textual and dynamically generated visual drafts in a training-free manner sets D2R apart. The idea to create and use intermediate "drafts" which combines text and image is clever.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses an important limitation in current MLLMs: their difficulty in handling dynamic visual environments. By introducing GRASSLAND and D2R, the authors provide a concrete way to evaluate and improve the ability of models to reason in such scenarios. The training-free nature of D2R is significant, as it allows easy integration with existing MLLMs without the need for extensive retraining. The results convincingly demonstrate the performance gains achieved by D2R across various tasks. The method fills a real gap in model capability and the benchmark could see real utility.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of dynamic spatial reasoning and the limitations of existing approaches.
    * **Novel Benchmark:** GRASSLAND provides a challenging and realistic environment for evaluating MLLMs.
    * **Effective Method:** D2R demonstrably improves performance on the proposed tasks.
    * **Training-Free:** The training-free aspect of D2R is a significant advantage, enabling wider adoption.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are well-designed and thoroughly evaluate D2R's performance across different tasks, models, and difficulty levels.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on External Tools:**  D2R relies on external toolkits (Dynamic-Information-Extract and Position-Draw tools, and an LLM scheduling hub) which could limit its accessibility and increase complexity, but they argue that its in service of general applicability and adaptability.
    * **Benchmark Simplicity:** While valuable, the GRASSLAND environment is still a simplified maze world. It may not fully capture the complexities of real-world dynamic spatial reasoning.
    * **Incremental Improvement over Baselines:** While there are substantial improvements relative to many base models, there are only marginal performance differences between D2R and Draft CoT, suggesting that D2R is not essential.
    * **Performance Discrepancy:**  The performance improvement of D2R across different models (Qwen2.5-VL-72B, QwenVL-max, Qwen2.5VL-7B) is somewhat uneven. The paper mentions this limitation but could provide further analysis or strategies to improve D2R's applicability to weaker models.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and adaptable MLLMs capable of handling dynamic visual information. The GRASSLAND benchmark could become a standard for evaluating dynamic spatial reasoning, and the D2R framework could serve as a valuable building block for future research in this area.

* **Score Justification:** While the paper introduces a valuable benchmark and method, and demonstrates clear improvements on dynamic spatial reasoning tasks, the dependence on external tools and limited complexity of GRASSLAND prevents it from being considered a truly groundbreaking contribution. There is a potential concern about broad accessibility that limits the score. There are also incremental improvements on baselines.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### O$^2$-Searcher: A Searching-based Agent Model for Open-Domain Open-Ended Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16582v1)
- **Authors**: Jianbiao Mei, Tao Hu, Daocheng Fu, Licheng Wen, Xuemeng Yang, Rong Wu, Pinlong Cai, Xing Gao, Yu Yang, Chengjun Xie, Botian Shi, Yong Liu, Yu Qiao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their advancements, are fundamentally limited by their static parametric knowledge, hindering performance on tasks requiring open-domain up-to-date information. While enabling LLMs to interact with external knowledge environments is a promising solution, current efforts primarily address closed-end problems. Open-ended questions, which characterized by lacking a standard answer or providing non-unique and diverse answers, remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we present O$^2$-Searcher, a novel search agent leveraging reinforcement learning to effectively tackle both open-ended and closed-ended questions in the open domain. O$^2$-Searcher leverages an efficient, locally simulated search environment for dynamic knowledge acquisition, effectively decoupling the external world knowledge from model's sophisticated reasoning processes. It employs a unified training mechanism with meticulously designed reward functions, enabling the agent to identify problem types and adapt different answer generation strategies. Furthermore, to evaluate performance on complex open-ended tasks, we construct O$^2$-QA, a high-quality benchmark featuring 300 manually curated, multi-domain open-ended questions with associated web page caches. Extensive experiments show that O$^2$-Searcher, using only a 3B model, significantly surpasses leading LLM agents on O$^2$-QA. It also achieves SOTA results on various closed-ended QA benchmarks against similarly-sized models, while performing on par with much larger ones.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "O2-Searcher: A Searching-based Agent Model for Open-Domain Open-Ended Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces O2-Searcher, a reinforcement learning-based agent designed to answer both closed-ended and open-ended questions in an open-domain setting.  O2-Searcher uses a simulated local search environment for efficient knowledge acquisition, decoupling external world knowledge from the LLM's internal reasoning.  It employs a unified training mechanism with specialized reward functions, allowing the agent to identify question types and adapt its answer generation strategy. To evaluate performance on complex open-ended tasks, the authors introduce O2-QA, a new high-quality benchmark dataset.  Experiments demonstrate that O2-Searcher, using a relatively small 3B parameter model, outperforms existing LLM agents on O2-QA and achieves state-of-the-art results on closed-ended QA benchmarks when compared with similarly sized models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates several key novel aspects. It introduces a specialized RL agent, O2-Searcher, specifically tailored for open-ended question answering, a relatively underexplored area in open-domain QA. The simulated search environment to decouple external knowledge acquisition is also novel and addresses scalability and cost issues. Finally, the unified training mechanism with task-adaptive reward functions allows the agent to handle both closed and open-ended QA.
*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to influence the field due to its contribution to open-ended question answering. The introduction of the O2-QA benchmark dataset is a valuable resource for future research in this area. The performance of the relatively small O2-Searcher model compared to larger models suggests a promising direction for more efficient and effective open-domain QA systems.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The design of the search environment and RL training framework is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   The O2-QA dataset addresses a gap in existing QA benchmarks by focusing on complex, open-ended questions.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of O2-Searcher compared to strong baselines.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach and experimental setup.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The O2-QA dataset, while novel, relies on manually curated questions and a static cache, which limits generalizability. This raises concerns about potential sampling bias and an inability to adapt to real-time information changes.
    *   The model is evaluated only on a 3B parameter model, raising questions regarding its scalability to larger models and whether the observed performance gains will hold.
    *   The dependency on external search, even with a simulated environment, can make the model susceptible to noisy or misinformative search results. While the method claims noise resilience, deeper analyses regarding the robustness to misinformation would strengthen the claim.
    *   The limited number of training examples in RL training calls to question the generalization of the system for truly open and unbounded domains.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could stimulate further research in developing more efficient and adaptable open-domain QA systems, as well as encourage the creation of more robust benchmarks for open-ended question answering. It also highlights the importance of task-adaptive training and RL in improving LLM performance.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing the gap in open-ended QA and providing a working RL-based agent. The benchmark is welcome, but its manual creation is a concern. Also, the reliance on external information always poses questions about the reliability of the final answer. Given the contributions, the weaknesses, and the limited scope of evaluation.

**Score: 7**

**Rigorous Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of open-domain question answering by tackling the challenging problem of open-ended questions. The novel O2-Searcher architecture, the unified training approach, and the O2-QA benchmark address existing gaps in the literature. However, the limitations related to dataset generalizability, model scalability, noise susceptibility, and the size of the model prevent a higher score. While O2-Searcher shows promising results, further research is needed to address these limitations and fully realize its potential for real-world applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Survey on the Application of Large Language Models in Scenario-Based Testing of Automated Driving Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16587v1)
- **Authors**: Yongqi Zhao, Ji Zhou, Dong Bi, Tomislav Mihalj, Jia Hu, Arno Eichberger
- **Abstract**: The safety and reliability of Automated Driving Systems (ADSs) must be validated prior to large-scale deployment. Among existing validation approaches, scenario-based testing has been regarded as a promising method to improve testing efficiency and reduce associated costs. Recently, the emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has introduced new opportunities to reinforce this approach. While an increasing number of studies have explored the use of LLMs in the field of automated driving, a dedicated review focusing on their application within scenario-based testing remains absent. This survey addresses this gap by systematically categorizing the roles played by LLMs across various phased of scenario-based testing, drawing from both academic research and industrial practice. In addition, key characteristics of LLMs and corresponding usage strategies are comprehensively summarized. The paper concludes by outlining five open challenges and potential research directions. To support ongoing research efforts, a continuously updated repository of recent advancements and relevant open-source tools is made available at: https://github.com/ftgTUGraz/LLM4ADSTest.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the OCRed text.

**Summary:**

This paper presents a survey on the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in scenario-based testing of Automated Driving Systems (ADSs). It addresses the gap in the existing literature by systematically categorizing the roles LLMs play across different phases of scenario-based testing, drawing from both academic research and industrial practice. The paper summarizes the types of LLMs used, their corresponding adoption strategies, and the simulators integrated with them. It also identifies five key challenges and proposes potential research directions to advance the application of LLMs in this domain. The paper aims to provide a comprehensive overview of emerging methodologies and offers a constantly updated repository of recent advancements and open-source tools to support ongoing research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to be the first comprehensive survey that systematically examines how LLMs are integrated into various phases of scenario-based testing of ADSs. This claim hinges on the absence of prior dedicated reviews focusing specifically on this application. While existing surveys have covered LLMs in AD or scenario-based testing in general, the authors assert the novelty by focusing on the *intersection* of LLMs *within* scenario-based testing. If this is indeed true, it represents a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this survey stems from the increasing importance of both LLMs and scenario-based testing in the development and validation of ADSs. LLMs offer the potential to automate and improve various aspects of scenario-based testing, such as scenario generation, data enrichment, and test execution. By providing a comprehensive overview of the existing research and identifying key challenges, the survey can help accelerate the development and adoption of LLM-based testing methodologies, potentially leading to safer and more reliable ADSs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Categorization:** The paper establishes a taxonomy for classifying existing approaches based on testing phases and roles assigned to LLMs, which provides a structured overview.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** By including both academic research and industrial practice, the paper offers a balanced perspective.
    *   **Identified Challenges:** The identification of five key challenges provides a clear roadmap for future research directions.
    *   **Supporting Resource:** The online repository of recent advancements and open-source tools is a valuable resource for researchers in the field.
    *   **Quantitative Analysis:** The paper includes data on the number of publications in related areas, demonstrating the growing interest in the subject.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on OCR Text:** This evaluation relies on OCRed text, which might not be perfectly accurate, potentially affecting the analysis.
    *   **Claims without Full Substantiation:** While the survey claims to be the first in its specific niche, one would need to compare it to all existing surveys in AD and LLMs to fully confirm the absolute novelty.
    *   **Limited Industrial Perspective:** While the paper mentions including industrial practice, the examples in Table IV show relatively small number of documented industrial use cases. Deeper dive into the real-world challenges would increase the significance of the work.
    *   **Potential for Overlap:** There is always the possibility of substantial overlap with existing survey papers that cover adjacent topics. If a large portion of the content merely rehashes existing summaries, the novelty diminishes.
    *   **Limited scope for "New" methods:** Because this is a survey, it doesn't introduce fundamentally novel approaches. The value rests on the comprehensiveness and insightfulness of the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The survey has the potential to be highly influential if it is indeed the first comprehensive review of the topic, is well-written and clearly organized, and accurately identifies the key challenges and opportunities in the field. The accompanying repository also adds to its value.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7/10**.

*   **Novelty:** The specific focus on the *intersection* of LLMs and *within* scenario-based testing gives it an edge but the magnitude of novelty depends on how much overlap it has with adjacent research areas.
*   **Significance:** The topic is undoubtedly important, and the potential for LLMs to improve ADSs testing is high.
*   **Comprehensiveness:** The structured approach, inclusion of both academic and industrial perspectives and the online repository contribute to the survey's value.
*   **Challenges:** The paper should be careful not to overstate its uniqueness and to acknowledge any limitations clearly.

This score reflects the fact that the paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important area, provides a useful categorization of existing research, and identifies key challenges. However, the score is not higher because the novelty is somewhat dependent on the specific phrasing of the research focus and it's a survey so it doesn't directly introduce new algorithms or methodologies. The limited industrial scope and the potential for overlap with adjacent areas also temper the score. If the paper offers genuinely novel insights and stimulates further research in this critical area, its impact could be significant.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond LLMs: An Exploration of Small Open-source Language Models in Logging Statement Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16590v1)
- **Authors**: Renyi Zhong, Yichen Li, Guangba Yu, Wenwei Gu, Jinxi Kuang, Yintong Huo, Michael R. Lyu
- **Abstract**: Effective software maintenance heavily relies on high-quality logging statements, but manual logging is challenging, error-prone, and insufficiently standardized, often leading to inconsistent log quality. While large language models have shown promise in automatic logging, they introduce concerns regarding privacy, resource intensity, and adaptability to specific enterprise needs. To tackle these limitations, this paper empirically investigates whether Small Open-source Language Models (SOLMs) could become a viable alternative via proper exploitation. Specifically, we conduct a large-scale empirical study on four prominent SOLMs, systematically evaluating the impacts of various interaction strategies, parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques, model sizes, and model types in automatic logging. Our key findings reveal that Retrieval-Augmented Generation significantly enhances performance, and LoRA is a highly effective PEFT technique. While larger SOLMs tend to perform better, this involves a trade-off with computational resources, and instruct-tuned SOLMs generally surpass their base counterparts. Notably, fine-tuned SOLMs, particularly Qwen2.5-coder-14B, outperformed existing specialized tools and LLM baselines in accurately predicting logging locations and generating high-quality statements, a conclusion supported by traditional evaluation metrics and LLM-as-a-judge evaluations. Furthermore, SOLMs also demonstrated robust generalization across diverse, unseen code repositories.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond LLMs: An Exploration of Small Open-source Language Models in Logging Statement Generation" explores the viability of Small Open-source Language Models (SOLMs) as an alternative to Large Language Models (LLMs) for automatic logging statement generation. The authors argue that while LLMs show promise, they raise concerns regarding privacy, resource consumption, and adaptability. They conduct a large-scale empirical study evaluating the performance of several SOLMs (LLaMA, Mistral, CodeLLaMA, and Qwen2.5coder) under various interaction strategies (instruction prompting, in-context learning, retrieval-augmented generation, chain-of-thought), parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques (Prefix Tuning, Prompt Tuning, LoRA, and QLoRA), model sizes, and model types (base vs. instruction-tuned). The key findings show that Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) significantly enhances performance, LoRA is a highly effective PEFT technique, larger SOLMs tend to perform better (with a computational trade-off), instruction-tuned SOLMs outperform base counterparts, and fine-tuned SOLMs (especially Qwen2.5-coder-14B) outperform existing specialized tools and LLM baselines. Furthermore, SOLMs show strong generalization across diverse code repositories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic empirical investigation of SOLMs for the specific task of automated logging statement generation. While prior work has explored LLMs for this task, the paper makes a strong case for the viability of SOLMs by addressing practical limitations associated with LLMs. The comprehensive exploration of various PEFT techniques, model sizes, and interaction strategies is also a notable contribution. The cross-repository generalization analysis is valuable and strengthens the argument for practical applicability.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to democratize automated logging. By demonstrating that SOLMs can achieve comparable or even superior performance to LLMs while addressing privacy and resource concerns, the paper opens up new possibilities for organizations with limited resources or strict data protection policies. The results provide practical guidance on selecting and fine-tuning SOLMs for this task. It also introduces the practical advantages of using SOLMs, including sustainable resource usage and local deployment capabilities

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:**  The study's strength lies in its thorough and well-designed experimental setup. The use of a large-scale dataset, multiple SOLMs, diverse techniques, and evaluation metrics provides strong evidence for the findings.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world problem with practical solutions, offering actionable insights for practitioners.
    *   **Rigorous Analysis:** The authors carefully analyze their results, providing insightful explanations for the observed trends. They also acknowledge and address potential threats to validity.
    *   **Open Source:** The availability of the code, dataset, and results enhances reproducibility and promotes further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset limitations**: The reliance on a predominantly Java-based dataset limits the generalizability of the findings to other programming languages. The paper acknowledges this, but future research should address this gap.
    *   **Limited Scope of Model Exploration**: While the paper explores several SOLMs, it is not exhaustive. Future work should investigate other emerging models and architectures.
    *   **Metric limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of certain metrics in capturing the nuanced aspects of logging statement quality and mitigate this by incorporating LLM-as-a-judge and including developer-centric evaluations, however future work should include an investigation of new custom metrics for evaluating code quality

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of automated logging. It provides a compelling case for the use of SOLMs, highlighting their advantages and practical considerations. The findings can guide the development of new logging tools and techniques and promote the adoption of more sustainable and privacy-aware approaches.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates substantial novelty and significance in the area of automatic logging statement generation. The work provides a clear and compelling argument for the adoption of Small Open-source Language Models (SOLMs) as a viable alternative to Large Language Models (LLMs). The strengths of the paper include a comprehensive and well-designed experimental setup, practical relevance, rigorous analysis, and the public availability of resources. While there are limitations, including the dataset being focused predominantly on Java and further expansion of the SOLM model, these do not diminish the paper's overall impact or importance. The potential to democratize automated logging and promote sustainable, privacy-aware approaches justifies the score. The paper has the capacity to influence the trajectory of automated logging practices and encourages further investigation into the capabilities of SOLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Evaluating Large Language Model with Knowledge Oriented Language Specific Simple Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16591v1)
- **Authors**: Bowen Jiang, Runchuan Zhu, Jiang Wu, Zinco Jiang, Yifan He, Junyuan Gao, Jia Yu, Rui Min, Yinfan Wang, Haote Yang, Songyang Zhang, Dahua Lin, Lijun Wu, Conghui He
- **Abstract**: We introduce KoLasSimpleQA, the first benchmark evaluating the multilingual factual ability of Large Language Models (LLMs). Inspired by existing research, we created the question set with features such as single knowledge point coverage, absolute objectivity, unique answers, and temporal stability. These questions enable efficient evaluation using the LLM-as-judge paradigm, testing both the LLMs' factual memory and self-awareness ("know what they don't know"). KoLasSimpleQA expands existing research in two key dimensions: (1) Breadth (Multilingual Coverage): It includes 9 languages, supporting global applicability evaluation. (2) Depth (Dual Domain Design): It covers both the general domain (global facts) and the language-specific domain (such as history, culture, and regional traditions) for a comprehensive assessment of multilingual capabilities. We evaluated mainstream LLMs, including traditional LLM and emerging Large Reasoning Models. Results show significant performance differences between the two domains, particularly in performance metrics, ranking, calibration, and robustness. This highlights the need for targeted evaluation and optimization in multilingual contexts. We hope KoLasSimpleQA will help the research community better identify LLM capability boundaries in multilingual contexts and provide guidance for model optimization. We will release KoLasSimpleQA at https://github.com/opendatalab/KoLasSimpleQA .
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Evaluating Large Language Model with Knowledge Oriented Language Specific Simple Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KoLasSimpleQA, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the multilingual factual ability of Large Language Models (LLMs). The benchmark is built on simple, fact-based question answering inspired by SimpleQA and ChineseSimpleQA, but expands on them in two key dimensions: breadth and depth. KoLasSimpleQA includes nine languages, offering broad multilingual coverage, and covers both general knowledge and language-specific knowledge (e.g., history, culture, regional traditions). The authors evaluate several mainstream LLMs and large reasoning models (LRMs) on KoLasSimpleQA, finding significant performance differences between general and language-specific domains.  The results reveal that models optimized for general knowledge often struggle with language-specific facts, and that translating non-English queries into English is less effective in the language-specific domain. The authors further analyze the calibration and knowledge robustness of LLMs in both domains, highlighting the need for targeted evaluation and optimization for multilingual contexts.  The paper releases the benchmark dataset to the community.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in the construction of the KoLasSimpleQA benchmark. While the basic concept of simple QA is not new, the expansion to nine languages and the inclusion of a language-specific knowledge domain are significant contributions. The dual domain design is particularly important for probing factual knowledge in specific cultural contexts. While other multilingual benchmarks exist, they often do not emphasize specific domain knowledge for the evaluated languages, which is this work's distinguishing feature.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the increasing importance of multilingual LLMs. The benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating factual knowledge beyond the commonly evaluated English or Chinese data. The findings of the paper are important for guiding future research and development efforts in creating more culturally aware and accurate multilingual LLMs. The analysis of the evaluated models provides insights into their limitations and calibration problems. The release of the dataset is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Benchmark:** The paper provides a clear description of the benchmark's construction and the criteria for question selection. The multi-stage quality control helps to ensure the dataset's reliability.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is performed on a diverse set of models, including both instruction-tuned models and large reasoning models.
    *   **Important Findings:** The results reveal significant performance differences between general and language-specific domains, which highlights the importance of culturally grounded evaluation.
    *   **Availability of Resources:** The dataset's release to the public will foster further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on GPT-40 for Evaluation:** Using GPT-40 as a judge, while practical, introduces potential bias and isn't fully automated or transparent. While common practice, it's still a limiting factor.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Error Types:** The analysis could be enriched by more qualitative error analysis, identifying specific types of factual errors or reasoning failures.
    *   **Scope of Language Coverage:** Although covering 9 languages is a substantial increase, it still only covers a fraction of the world's languages, limiting the generalizability to truly global multilingual LLMs.
    *   **Dependency on Wikipedia:** While leveraging Wikipedia allows for scale, the inter-language link number method to classify general and specific knowledge is limited and potentially biased.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future benchmark development in the multilingual setting. It may encourage the development of targeted evaluation and optimization strategies for LLMs in specific languages and cultural contexts.
*   **Justification of Score:** The paper has notable novelty in benchmark construction and is a valuable resource for the field. However, the GPT-40 dependency is an obvious issue and the error analysis is somewhat limited.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Generic Empathy to Personalized Emotional Support: A Self-Evolution Framework for User Preference Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16610v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Ye, Lu Xiang, Yaping Zhang, Chengqing Zong
- **Abstract**: Effective emotional support hinges on understanding users' emotions and needs to provide meaningful comfort during multi-turn interactions. Large Language Models (LLMs) show great potential for expressing empathy; however, they often deliver generic and one-size-fits-all responses that fail to address users' specific needs. To tackle this issue, we propose a self-evolution framework designed to help LLMs improve their responses to better align with users' implicit preferences concerning user profiles (personalities), emotional states, and specific situations. Our framework consists of two distinct phases: \textit{(1)} \textit{Emotional Support Experience Acquisition}, where LLMs are fine-tuned on limited emotional support conversation data to provide basic support, and \textit{(2)} \textit{Self-Improvement for Personalized Emotional Support}, where LLMs leverage self-reflection and self-refinement to generate personalized responses. Through iterative direct preference optimization between the pre- and post-refined responses, our model generates responses that reflect a better understanding of the user's implicit preferences. Extensive experiments and evaluations demonstrate that our method significantly enhances the model's performance in emotional support, reducing unhelpful responses and minimizing discrepancies between user preferences and model outputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of generic and superficial empathy in emotional support conversations generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). It proposes a two-stage self-evolution framework to improve personalization and alignment with user preferences. The framework involves: (1) fine-tuning LLMs on a limited set of emotional support conversations (Emotional Support Experience Acquisition) to provide basic support capabilities and (2) using self-reflection and self-refinement to generate personalized responses considering user profiles, emotional states, and specific situations (Self-Improvement for Personalized Emotional Support).  The method uses direct preference optimization (DPO) to refine the model based on synthetic preference data derived from pre- and post-refined responses, effectively teaching the LLM to implicitly understand user needs. The authors demonstrate through experiments and human evaluations that their method enhances model performance, reduces unhelpful responses, and minimizes discrepancies between user preferences and model outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its approach to personalized emotional support through a self-evolution framework that implicitly learns user preferences. Previous methods often rely on direct fine-tuning, complex prompting, or explicit reflection steps. The presented work attempts to streamline the process by having the LLM learn user preferences from its own refined outputs, leveraging self-reflection to generate synthetic preference data for DPO. The use of DPO to improve on an SFT model using synthetic data generated from the model itself is a sensible and potentially impactful research direction.

*   **Significance:** Emotional support conversation systems are important in practical applications, especially in areas such as mental health care and customer service. Improving the ability of LLMs to provide more personalized and effective support can have significant real-world impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing emotional support chatbots and motivates the need for more personalized approaches.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The two-stage framework is clearly explained and the individual components are well-justified.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation, including both objective metrics and subjective human evaluations, to demonstrate the effectiveness of their method. The ablations of using different data for the DPO training loop also offers some interesting insight into the best ways to train the models.
    *   **Strong Results:** The experiments consistently show improvements over baseline models, suggesting that the self-evolution framework is effective.
    *   **Human Evaluation:**  The use of both pointwise and pairwise interactive human evaluations strengthens the validity of the results and better reflects real-world user experiences.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation study provides a useful perspective on the impact of using different types of preference data, showing the advantage of the iterative self-improvement aspect of the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Preference Data Quality:** The quality of the synthetic preference data generated by the LLM could be a concern. The paper acknowledges this and describes filtering techniques to mitigate this issue. However, further analysis of the characteristics and potential biases of the synthetic data would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Subjectivity of Evaluation:**  Despite using multiple evaluation metrics, subjective evaluation remains challenging in this domain. Although the user feedback for pairwise and pointwise evaluation, together with the multiple evaluation metrics do strengthen the paper it would still be beneficial to add more detail as to the consistency between raters.
    *   **Limited Scope of User Personalization:** Although the paper aims for personalized support, the extent of personalization considered in the experiments might be limited. A deeper exploration of how the LLM is tailoring responses based on different types of user profiles or emotional states would enhance the significance. The paper would also benefit from stronger explanation of exactly how the the model is able to infer things from users that could lead to personalization.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the authors demonstrate strong results across multiple models and architectures, it is unclear how well this method generalises to other tasks or situations outside of emotional support. The benefits and trade-offs should be explicitly discussed to contextualize the effectiveness of the models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on personalized emotional support systems and the use of self-reflection and preference learning in LLMs. The framework provides a practical approach for improving LLM performance in this area and could inspire further research on related techniques.

**Justification:**

The paper proposes a simple, yet effective framework with strong empirical results to better align LLMs with user implicit preferences for emotional support. While the approach leverages existing techniques like SFT and DPO, the novelty lies in its creative approach to synthetic data generation through self-reflection, effectively improving the LLM's implicit understanding of the user. The weaknesses of the paper include the questions surrounding the synthetic data and the scope of personalization. The generalizability of the results could be strengthened by extending the analysis.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Steering Large Language Models for Machine Translation Personalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16612v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Scalena, Gabriele Sarti, Arianna Bisazza, Elisabetta Fersini, Malvina Nissim
- **Abstract**: High-quality machine translation systems based on large language models (LLMs) have simplified the production of personalized translations reflecting specific stylistic constraints. However, these systems still struggle in settings where stylistic requirements are less explicit and might be harder to convey via prompting. We explore various strategies for personalizing LLM-generated translations in low-resource settings, focusing on the challenging literary translation domain. We explore prompting strategies and inference-time interventions for steering model generations towards a personalized style, and propose a contrastive framework exploiting latent concepts extracted from sparse autoencoders to identify salient personalization properties. Our results show that steering achieves strong personalization while preserving translation quality. We further examine the impact of steering on LLM representations, finding model layers with a relevant impact for personalization are impacted similarly by multi-shot prompting and our steering method, suggesting similar mechanism at play.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates methods for personalizing machine translation (MT) using large language models (LLMs), focusing on the challenging domain of literary translation.  It explores prompting strategies and inference-time interventions to steer LLM-generated translations towards a specific translator's style, especially in low-resource settings.  A key contribution is a contrastive framework that leverages sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to identify and emphasize salient personalization properties. The results show that steering with SAEs achieves effective personalization while maintaining translation quality.  The authors also analyze the impact of their approach on LLM representations, finding similarities between the effects of multi-shot prompting and SAE steering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several elements that offer a degree of novelty.  Applying steering techniques based on interpretability methods (SAEs) to the problem of MT personalization, particularly in literary translation, is relatively unexplored. While prompting and some forms of steering have been tried before, the specific combination with SAEs and the focus on *style* rather than just content in a *low-resource* setting adds a fresh perspective. The study of how steering affects the *internal representations* of the LLM and its connection to prompting mechanisms is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant challenge in MT: moving beyond generic translations to capture stylistic nuances and personal voices. Literary translation is a suitable testbed due to the importance of translator style. If the proposed techniques prove robust and scalable, they could improve the quality and expressiveness of automatically translated literary works. Furthermore, the insights into how prompting and steering interact with internal model representations can contribute to a better understanding of LLM behavior and control. The practical application of SAEs for fine-grained LLM control is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear research questions.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across multiple languages, models, and techniques.
    *   Use of a relevant dataset (PAR3) with multiple human translations.
    *   Incorporation of both automatic metrics and analysis of model representations.
    *   Comparison against established prompting and steering baselines.
    *   Insightful analysis of the trade-off between personalization and translation quality.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The limitation to a relatively small set of LLMs, especially in size. The generalizability of the findings to larger, more powerful models needs further validation.  The constrained SAE availability for Llama also limits a direct comparison.
    *   The computational cost of training SAEs could limit broader adoption. The paper acknowledges this, but a discussion of potential ways to alleviate this constraint would be valuable.
    *   The qualitative aspects of the translations could be strengthened. While Comet and classification scores are used, more detailed analysis of specific stylistic features captured and reproduced would be helpful. The discussion of incoherent results for very high steering intensities indicates a potential need for more refined control mechanisms.
    *   The reliance on pre-trained SAEs limits the ability to target specific stylistic features; future work should explore fine-tuning these components to the target task.
    *   The degree of actual “interpretability” provided by SAEs could be questioned. While they offer a step towards it, disentangling the *meaning* of the latents remains a challenge.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's contributions, while incremental, are well-executed and relevant to the field of machine translation and LLM control. The novel combination of SAEs and steering, the focus on stylistic personalization, and the analysis of model representations justify a solid, but not exceptional, score. The limitations regarding model size, computational cost, and the depth of qualitative and interpretability analysis prevent it from reaching a higher level of impact at this stage. The potential for broader application and the insights into LLM behavior are promising but require further research.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Grounding Chest X-Ray Visual Question Answering with Generated Radiology Reports
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16624v1)
- **Authors**: Francesco Dalla Serra, Patrick Schrempf, Chaoyang Wang, Zaiqiao Meng, Fani Deligianni, Alison Q. O'Neil
- **Abstract**: We present a novel approach to Chest X-ray (CXR) Visual Question Answering (VQA), addressing both single-image image-difference questions. Single-image questions focus on abnormalities within a specific CXR ("What abnormalities are seen in image X?"), while image-difference questions compare two longitudinal CXRs acquired at different time points ("What are the differences between image X and Y?"). We further explore how the integration of radiology reports can enhance the performance of VQA models. While previous approaches have demonstrated the utility of radiology reports during the pre-training phase, we extend this idea by showing that the reports can also be leveraged as additional input to improve the VQA model's predicted answers. First, we propose a unified method that handles both types of questions and auto-regressively generates the answers. For single-image questions, the model is provided with a single CXR. For image-difference questions, the model is provided with two CXRs from the same patient, captured at different time points, enabling the model to detect and describe temporal changes. Taking inspiration from 'Chain-of-Thought reasoning', we demonstrate that performance on the CXR VQA task can be improved by grounding the answer generator module with a radiology report predicted for the same CXR. In our approach, the VQA model is divided into two steps: i) Report Generation (RG) and ii) Answer Generation (AG). Our results demonstrate that incorporating predicted radiology reports as evidence to the AG model enhances performance on both single-image and image-difference questions, achieving state-of-the-art results on the Medical-Diff-VQA dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to Chest X-Ray (CXR) Visual Question Answering (VQA) that addresses both single-image and image-difference questions. The key innovation lies in grounding the answer generation module with radiology reports predicted for the same CXR. The approach, termed Report Generator-Answer Generator (RG-AG) pipeline, first generates a radiology report using a Report Generator (RG) and then uses this report as additional input to the Answer Generator (AG). The paper demonstrates that incorporating predicted radiology reports enhances performance on both single-image and image-difference questions, achieving state-of-the-art results on the Medical-Diff-VQA dataset. The authors also perform ablation studies to analyze the impact of various input components and qualitative analysis to gain a better understanding of the model's behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of grounding the answer generation process in medical VQA with automatically generated radiology reports is a worthwhile contribution.  Previous work has used radiology reports for pre-training, but the authors demonstrate that *also* using them at inference time to improve VQA performance is beneficial. The unified approach to single-image and image-difference VQA is also a positive aspect, simplifying the model architecture. While the individual components (anatomical token extraction, Transformer-based language models, chain-of-thought inspiration) are not necessarily groundbreaking on their own, their integration within the RG-AG framework for the specific task of CXR VQA is a novel combination.

*   **Significance:** The paper's results demonstrate a clear performance improvement over existing methods on the Medical-Diff-VQA dataset. The improvements are especially notable for image-difference questions, which are clinically relevant in monitoring disease progression.  The qualitative analysis provides insights into the model's behavior, highlighting the importance of accurate radiology reports for generating reliable answers. The paper addresses a relevant problem in medical imaging, as accurate VQA systems can potentially assist radiologists and improve clinical workflows. While the study uses a specific dataset (Medical-Diff-VQA), the methodology could be generalized to other medical imaging modalities and VQA tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation of the methodology.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with ablation studies and qualitative analysis.
    *   State-of-the-art performance on a challenging benchmark dataset.
    *   Unified framework for handling both single-image and image-difference questions.
    *   Strong connection to clinical relevance through focusing on CXR and image-difference analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on automatically generated radiology reports introduces a dependency on the accuracy of the RG module. Errors in the report can propagate to the answer generation stage, leading to incorrect answers. While the authors acknowledge this limitation and discuss the impact of using ground truth reports in the ablation study, further research is needed to improve the robustness of the RG module.
    *   The limited scope of the questions in the Medical-Diff-VQA dataset is also a limitation. The dataset mainly focuses on "What has changed" questions and insertion of keywords. More complex and clinically relevant questions would necessitate more sophisticated methods of parsing and answer expression.
    *   The complexity of the RG-AG pipeline might be a concern, as it involves training multiple modules (anatomical token extractor, RG, AG). Simpler, end-to-end approaches might be more desirable in some applications.
    *   Generalizability to other medical imaging modalities or other VQA datasets. The paper should include discussions around the limitations and challenges if the methodology is applied to other modalities.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the development of more accurate and reliable medical VQA systems.  The idea of grounding VQA with generated reports can be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field. The paper also highlights the importance of high-quality radiology reports for VQA, motivating further research into improving report generation models.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to CXR VQA, demonstrating clear performance improvements on a challenging dataset. The idea of grounding the answer generation with automatically generated radiology reports is a valuable contribution.  While the paper has limitations related to the dataset's scope and the reliance on the RG module's accuracy, the overall contribution is significant enough to warrant a score of 7. This score indicates a solid contribution with clear novelty and potential for impact, but with room for improvement and further research to address the identified weaknesses. The paper's findings warrant further exploration and adaptation within the medical VQA field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SSR-Zero: Simple Self-Rewarding Reinforcement Learning for Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16637v1)
- **Authors**: Wenjie Yang, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Zheng Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated remarkable capabilities in machine translation (MT). However, most advanced MT-specific LLMs heavily rely on external supervision signals during training, such as human-annotated reference data or trained reward models (RMs), which are often expensive to obtain and challenging to scale. To overcome this limitation, we propose a Simple Self-Rewarding (SSR) Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework for MT that is reference-free, fully online, and relies solely on self-judging rewards. Training with SSR using 13K monolingual examples and Qwen-2.5-7B as the backbone, our model SSR-Zero-7B outperforms existing MT-specific LLMs, e.g., TowerInstruct-13B and GemmaX-28-9B, as well as larger general LLMs like Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct in English $\leftrightarrow$ Chinese translation tasks from WMT23, WMT24, and Flores200 benchmarks. Furthermore, by augmenting SSR with external supervision from COMET, our strongest model, SSR-X-Zero-7B, achieves state-of-the-art performance in English $\leftrightarrow$ Chinese translation, surpassing all existing open-source models under 72B parameters and even outperforming closed-source models, e.g., GPT-4o and Gemini 1.5 Pro. Our analysis highlights the effectiveness of the self-rewarding mechanism compared to the external LLM-as-a-judge approach in MT and demonstrates its complementary benefits when combined with trained RMs. Our findings provide valuable insight into the potential of self-improving RL methods. We have publicly released our code, data and models.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SSR-Zero: Simple Self-Rewarding Reinforcement Learning for Machine Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SSR (Simple Self-Rewarding), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework for machine translation (MT). SSR aims to overcome the reliance on external supervision signals like human-annotated data or trained reward models (RMs) by using a self-judging mechanism. The LLM acts as both actor (generating translations) and judge (evaluating translation quality). The rewards derived from these self-evaluations are used within the GRPO algorithm to improve translation performance. The authors demonstrate that SSR-Zero-7B, an SSR-trained Qwen-2.5-7B model, outperforms existing MT-specific LLMs and larger general-purpose LLMs. They further enhance the framework by incorporating external supervision from COMET, resulting in SSR-X-Zero-7B, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. The paper provides a comparative analysis between the self-rewarding mechanism and external reward methods, offering insights into effective reward selection for MT systems. The authors release their code, data, and models publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of a completely self-rewarding MT system, without requiring external parallel data or reward models, is relatively novel. While self-play and self-judging mechanisms exist in other domains, their application in MT, especially with this degree of independence from external supervision, is a valuable contribution. The R1-Zero like training applied here to Qwen is also a novel approach to MT.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact on the field is significant because it addresses a key scalability bottleneck: the reliance on expensive labeled data for MT training. The results demonstrate that large pre-trained models have inherent translation and MT-evaluation capabilities that can be leveraged for self-improvement. This could potentially lead to more cost-effective and adaptable MT systems. Also, the approach provides better quality translation compared to external LLM judges in zero-shot settings, pointing to better utilization of model resources for reward modeling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined SSR framework with a practical implementation.
    *   Comprehensive experiments and comparative analyses against existing methods (MT-specific LLMs, general-purpose LLMs, and different reward strategies).
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating significant improvements in MT quality.
    *   Thorough analysis of training dynamics and ablations.
    *   Public release of code, data, and models for reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study focuses on English-Chinese translation. Generalization to other language pairs, especially low-resource languages, is an open question.
    *   The model size is still relatively small (7B parameters), although the approach works, scaling might be an important direction.
    *   Although the authors mention the importance of formatting outputs with the "answer" tag, they dont address the issues arising from it as a limitation.
    *   The analysis of the reward method is not fully clear. Is the increase due to the method working better in a zero-shot scenario, or a better reward in the end? Is the method actually better, or is it just cheaper? The paper makes the argument that the method is cheaper, but that it outperforms zero shot.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work can influence future research in several directions:
    *   Encouraging the development of self-improving RL methods for MT.
    *   Exploring the potential of leveraging inherent capabilities of large pre-trained models for MT and evaluation.
    *   Investigating the effectiveness of different self-judging mechanisms and reward designs.
    *   Developing more scalable and cost-effective MT systems for a wider range of languages.

*   **Score Justification:** While the core concept isn't entirely revolutionary, the specific application to MT with this level of self-reliance is a significant step. The strong empirical results, detailed analysis, and public release of resources contribute to its value. However, the limited language pairs and model size, and the existing research on self-play in other tasks prevent it from receiving a very high score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Evaluation to Defense: Advancing Safety in Video Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16643v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwei Sun, Peiqi Jiang, Chuanbin Liu, Luohao Lin, Zhiying Lu, Hongtao Xie
- **Abstract**: While the safety risks of image-based large language models have been extensively studied, their video-based counterparts (Video LLMs) remain critically under-examined.
