### Breaking mBad! Supervised Fine-tuning for Cross-Lingual Detoxification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16722v1)
- **Authors**: Himanshu Beniwal, Youngwoo Kim, Maarten Sap, Soham Dan, Thomas Hartvigsen
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly prevalent in global applications, ensuring that they are toxicity-free across diverse linguistic contexts remains a critical challenge. We explore "Cross-lingual Detoxification", a cross-lingual paradigm that mitigates toxicity, enabling detoxification capabilities to transfer between high and low-resource languages across different script families. We analyze cross-lingual detoxification's effectiveness through 504 extensive settings to evaluate toxicity reduction in cross-distribution settings with limited data and investigate how mitigation impacts model performance on non-toxic tasks, revealing trade-offs between safety and knowledge preservation. Our code and dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/himanshubeniwal/Breaking-mBad.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Breaking mBad! Supervised Fine-tuning for Cross-Lingual Detoxification" addresses the problem of toxicity in multilingual large language models (m-LLMs). It proposes and evaluates "Cross-lingual Detoxification" (X-DET), a methodology where a model is fine-tuned in a source language with parallel toxic-neutral pairs and then evaluated for toxicity reduction in various target languages. The study involves extensive experiments across 9 languages, 4 model variants, and different data limitations, aiming to determine the effectiveness of transferring detoxification capabilities across languages, scripts, and with limited parallel data. The key findings suggest that script similarity and language proportion during pre-training influence cross-lingual toxicity transfer, and that effective detoxification is possible even with limited parallel data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Significant Problem:** The paper tackles a critical issue of toxicity in m-LLMs, which is a major impediment to their wider and safer deployment.
*   **Cross-Lingual Focus:** The focus on cross-lingual transfer is crucial and extends beyond the typical English-centric approach of many toxicity mitigation studies. The paper acknowledges that toxicity manifests differently across cultures.
*   **Extensive Experiments:** The paper boasts an impressive experimental setup involving 504 configurations. This allows for a relatively comprehensive analysis of various factors affecting cross-lingual detoxification.
*   **Data Limitations Scenarios:** Investigating the effectiveness of X-DET with limited parallel data is valuable for low-resource languages where extensive datasets are often unavailable.
*   **Analysis of Linguistic Properties:**  The observation that linguistic properties such as script similarity and language proportion influence cross-lingual transfer is insightful and provides valuable directions for future research.
*   **Public Code and Dataset:** Releasing code and datasets significantly increases the reproducibility and impact of the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Selection and Fine-Tuning Techniques:** The paper focuses primarily on traditional fine-tuning, despite the existence of more advanced techniques like DPO or model editing. It doesn't adequately justify this choice other than mentioning a focus on data scarcity, however DPO and other techniques can be adapted to low data settings.
*   **Metric Limitations:** Reliance solely on Perspective API has limitations, as this tool, while standard, may not be equally accurate or culturally sensitive across all evaluated languages. The paper acknowledges this limitation, but it remains a notable weakness.
*   **Lack of Novelty in Methodology:** The basic idea of fine-tuning on parallel data for detoxification is not particularly novel *per se*.  The contribution lies more in the comprehensive experimental evaluation and analysis of cross-lingual transfer.
*   **Clarity and Presentation:** While the experiments are extensive, the presentation of the results is sometimes dense and difficult to interpret at a glance. The figures and tables are not always self-explanatory. There's a lot of information but extracting the key takeaways can be challenging.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides empirical observations about the impact of script similarity, language proportion, and data size. A more in-depth theoretical analysis explaining *why* these factors have the observed effects would enhance the contribution.
*   **Lack of Comparison to Strong Baselines:** A clear comparison to a solid baseline such as MinTox would help to clearly show the effectiveness of the approach compared to existing techniques.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The *combination* of addressing cross-lingual toxicity, conducting extensive experiments, analyzing the impact of linguistic properties, and investigating the effectiveness with limited data provides novelty and significance. While the core methodology isn't fundamentally new, the empirical analysis contributes valuable insights. The paper systematically investigates many key dimensions of cross-lingual toxicity mitigation, which is a relatively under-explored area.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**.

*   The paper addresses a vital problem with significant real-world implications (toxicity in m-LLMs).
*   The experimental setup is impressive in its scope and comprehensiveness, providing a lot of empirical data.
*   The identified factors (script similarity, language proportion, data limitations) are valuable for future research.
*   However, the limitations in model selection, evaluation metrics, lack of comparison to other relevant techniques, and the incremental nature of the core methodology prevent a higher score. The paper's biggest weakness is the lack of novelty in the approach taken.
*   A more rigorous analysis to further the understanding of what has been observed in the paper, coupled with a greater range of more powerful models, would have increased the overall score.

### Robust LLM Fingerprinting via Domain-Specific Watermarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16723v1)
- **Authors**: Thibaud Gloaguen, Robin Staab, Nikola Jovanović, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: As open-source language models (OSMs) grow more capable and are widely shared and finetuned, ensuring model provenance, i.e., identifying the origin of a given model instance, has become an increasingly important issue. At the same time, existing backdoor-based model fingerprinting techniques often fall short of achieving key requirements of real-world model ownership detection. In this work, we build on the observation that while current open-source model watermarks fail to achieve reliable content traceability, they can be effectively adapted to address the challenge of model provenance. To this end, we introduce the concept of domain-specific watermarking for model fingerprinting. Rather than watermarking all generated content, we train the model to embed watermarks only within specified subdomains (e.g., particular languages or topics). This targeted approach ensures detection reliability, while improving watermark durability and quality under a range of real-world deployment settings. Our evaluations show that domain-specific watermarking enables model fingerprinting with strong statistical guarantees, controllable false positive rates, high detection power, and preserved generation quality. Moreover, we find that our fingerprints are inherently stealthy and naturally robust to real-world variability across deployment scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to model fingerprinting for open-source language models (OSMs) called "domain-specific watermarking."  Instead of embedding a watermark in *all* generated text, as traditional watermarking does, the authors propose training the model to embed watermarks only within specific subdomains (e.g., a certain language like French, or a specific topic like math). This targeted approach aims to improve detection reliability, durability against finetuning, and overall generation quality while maintaining stealthiness. The authors demonstrate that domain-specific watermarking enables model fingerprinting with statistical guarantees, controllable false positive rates, and high detection power. The method proves robust to real-world variability across deployment scenarios, unlike other approaches that lack stealthiness or robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of applying domain-specific constraints to watermarking for *model fingerprinting* is a worthwhile innovation.  Existing model fingerprinting techniques are often brittle (easily removed or circumvented by finetuning), require direct access to model weights (not practical for black-box deployments), or are detectable (lacking stealthiness). The paper addresses these shortcomings in a well-defined way.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the growing importance of model provenance in the GenAI space. As more open-source models become available and are finetuned/deployed, identifying the origin of a model becomes crucial for enforcing licenses and preventing misuse.  The paper offers a practical approach to address this challenge, making it relevant for model providers and the broader AI safety community.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:**  The method focuses on a realistic black-box setting, interacting with the model only through text inputs/outputs.  This makes it suitable for real-world deployments.
    *   **Statistical Guarantees:**  Leveraging the statistical properties of traditional watermarks to fingerprint models is a big plus, providing well-defined false positive rates (reliability).
    *   **Robustness:** The empirical evaluation demonstrates resilience to finetuning and variations in deployment settings (e.g., system prompts). Actively exploiting the monotonicity of watermarks to compensate for signal degradation is a clever technique.
    *   **Harmlessness:** The domain-specific approach minimizes the impact on general generation quality, unlike methods that watermark all outputs.
    *   **Stealthiness:** Because the method uses standard queries (albeit within a specific domain) and the generated text is natural, it's difficult for an adversary to detect or block the fingerprinting attempts.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Domain Dependency:** The method relies on being able to effectively query the model within the target domain. This might be challenging or impractical for certain model use cases or if the adversary actively blocks queries in the target domain. The choice of domain then also influences what can be protected.
    *   **Scalability to Many Domains:** If a provider wants to fingerprint and protect its model across numerous specific domains, it might need to train several individual versions, increasing the complexity and maintenance efforts (though the multiple-key experiments address this partially).
    *   **Potential for Misuse:** The paper mentions that this technology can be misused. However, this is not an explicit part of the discussion.
    *   **Limited Comparisons:** The experiments could have been expanded to other types of model modification.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a significant and practically relevant contribution to the field of model fingerprinting. It effectively addresses the limitations of existing techniques by creatively adapting traditional watermarking methods. The experimental evaluation provides strong evidence of the method's reliability, robustness, and stealthiness. While there are some limitations related to domain dependency and scalability, the overall benefits outweigh the drawbacks. The techniques will be of significant use to model providers.

### Masked Conditioning for Deep Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16725v1)
- **Authors**: Phillip Mueller, Jannik Wiese, Sebastian Mueller, Lars Mikelsons
- **Abstract**: Datasets in engineering domains are often small, sparsely labeled, and contain numerical as well as categorical conditions. Additionally. computational resources are typically limited in practical applications which hinders the adoption of generative models for engineering tasks. We introduce a novel masked-conditioning approach, that enables generative models to work with sparse, mixed-type data. We mask conditions during training to simulate sparse conditions at inference time. For this purpose, we explore the use of various sparsity schedules that show different strengths and weaknesses. In addition, we introduce a flexible embedding that deals with categorical as well as numerical conditions. We integrate our method into an efficient variational autoencoder as well as a latent diffusion model and demonstrate the applicability of our approach on two engineering-related datasets of 2D point clouds and images. Finally, we show that small models trained on limited data can be coupled with large pretrained foundation models to improve generation quality while retaining the controllability induced by our conditioning scheme.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "masked conditioning" approach for deep generative models (DGMs) designed to work with datasets that are common in engineering domains: small, sparsely labeled, and containing mixed data types (numerical and categorical).  The core idea is to mask conditioning variables during training to simulate the conditions that the model will encounter at inference time (i.e., missing data). The authors explore different sparsity schedules (constant, step, linear, exponential) to determine which allows the model to generalize best with incomplete information.  They implement this method in both variational autoencoders (VAEs) and latent diffusion models (LDMs), demonstrating its applicability to point cloud data (bicycle and vehicle geometries) and images (cars and bicycles). Furthermore, they show that smaller, domain-specific models trained with masked conditioning can be combined with larger, pre-trained models (Stable Diffusion XL) for image refinement, boosting the visual quality while maintaining controllability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty is moderate.  Masking inputs is not a new concept *per se* (it's used in areas like NLP and image inpainting). The main contribution here is the *specific application* of masking to *conditioning variables* in generative models for *engineering design*, and the exploration of different sparsity schedules within this context.  The use of both VAEs and LDMs, and the hybrid approach of combining small, domain-specific models with large, pre-trained models adds to the novelty but does not revolutionize the underlying generative modeling techniques. While the integration with pretrained foundation models is interesting, it is more of an application of existing techniques in a new setting rather than a fundamentally new generative modeling approach.

**Significance:**

The significance is more compelling than the novelty. Engineering datasets are, as the paper states, notoriously challenging for standard DGMs.  The ability to train models that can generate useful designs from incomplete or sparse data *is* a practical and important problem. The experiments, while focused on relatively simple datasets (bicycle and vehicle geometries), do demonstrate the potential of the method. The ability to create custom models on minimal datasets and then leveraging the pretrained, large-scale models could be a big win for domains with limited data. The hybrid approach for image refinement is also potentially significant, providing a path to high-quality results with less computational cost. I think this paper presents a practical solution to a specific problem, paving the way for DGMs in design. While the datasets used in the experiments are well-defined, they lack real-world complexity and diversity, which might hinder the generalization to more complex engineering problems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a practical problem:**  Focuses on real-world limitations of DGMs in engineering design.
*   **Clear and well-motivated:**  The paper clearly explains the problem and the proposed solution.
*   **Comprehensive experiments:**  Experiments cover VAEs, LDMs, point clouds, images, and sparsity schedules.
*   **Hybrid approach:** Combines domain-specific models with pre-trained models, providing a practical solution to the data and computational limitations.
*   **Open source** The publication of code/data helps the research community in further development in the space.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited dataset complexity:**  The datasets used are relatively simple, raising questions about generalization to more complex engineering problems.
*   **Incremental improvement:** The core masking technique is not fundamentally new, although the application to conditioning variables with sparsity schedules in engineering is novel.
*   **Lack of theoretical depth:** While the experiments are well-conducted, there's a limited theoretical analysis of *why* certain sparsity schedules work better than others.
*   **Computational cost:** While hybrid approach can provide cost benefits, no benchmarking data provided to validate this
*   **Limited generalizability:** The results are demonstrated only on a few datasets and further analysis on wide variety of datasets could validate this methodology.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper tackles an important practical problem with a reasonable solution that includes experimentation. The paper is novel in its application of masking to conditional data and use of pretrained large models in an engineering context. However, the datasets lack complexity and generalizability to real-world datasets needs more analysis.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Forward-only Diffusion Probabilistic Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16733v1)
- **Authors**: Ziwei Luo, Fredrik K. Gustafsson, Jens Sjölund, Thomas B. Schön
- **Abstract**: This work presents a forward-only diffusion (FoD) approach for generative modelling. In contrast to traditional diffusion models that rely on a coupled forward-backward diffusion scheme, FoD directly learns data generation through a single forward diffusion process, yielding a simple yet efficient generative framework. The core of FoD is a state-dependent linear stochastic differential equation that involves a mean-reverting term in both the drift and diffusion functions. This mean-reversion property guarantees the convergence to clean data, naturally simulating a stochastic interpolation between source and target distributions. More importantly, FoD is analytically tractable and is trained using a simple stochastic flow matching objective, enabling a few-step non-Markov chain sampling during inference. The proposed FoD model, despite its simplicity, achieves competitive performance on various image-conditioned (e.g., image restoration) and unconditional generation tasks, demonstrating its effectiveness in generative modelling. Our code is available at https://github.com/Algolzw/FoD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Forward-only Diffusion (FoD), a novel generative modeling approach that simplifies traditional diffusion models by eliminating the need for a backward diffusion process. FoD relies on a single forward diffusion process governed by a state-dependent linear stochastic differential equation (SDE). The core innovation is a mean-reverting term in both the drift and diffusion functions, ensuring convergence to clean data and simulating data transitions between source and target distributions. FoD is analytically tractable, trained using a stochastic flow matching objective, and enables few-step sampling strategies. Experiments demonstrate competitive performance on image-conditioned (image restoration) and unconditional generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core idea of a forward-only diffusion process is a significant departure from the standard forward-backward diffusion paradigm, indicating some novelty.

    *   The mean-reverting SDE, while inspired by existing work, is adapted in a novel way to enable direct data generation without a reverse process. The particular form of the SDE with mean-reversion in both drift and diffusion, and its analytical tractability, is a key novel aspect.

    *   Connecting FoD to stochastic flow matching provides a new perspective on training generative models and is a valuable theoretical contribution.  Also linking FoD to rectified flow is important.

    *  However,  flow matching methods already exist, and so it's a reinterpretation / extension of these ideas. The devil's in the details, though, and how these pieces come together determines the overall novelty.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The simplification of the diffusion process is potentially significant, as it reduces algorithmic complexity and simplifies training.

    *   The ability to perform few-step sampling without sacrificing sample quality is valuable for improving the efficiency of generative models.

    *   The competitive performance on image restoration tasks suggests that FoD could be a practical alternative to existing diffusion-based methods.

    *   The link to flow matching opens up avenues for further research and development of generative models based on continuous data transformations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** The forward-only approach offers a more straightforward framework compared to traditional diffusion models.
    *   **Analytical Tractability:** This enables efficient training and sampling.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** Demonstrates practical viability across different tasks.
    *   **Theoretical Connections:** The link to stochastic flow matching provides a solid theoretical foundation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvement over existing methods on unconditional image generation (CIFAR-10) is not overwhelming. It trails Rectified Flow by a decent margin. This could suggest that the method may not be as effective for all generative tasks. Or, CIFAR-10 is not the best evaluation setting.
    *  The claims about advantage over flow matching in image conditioned generation needs to be stronger / better justified. It's stated, but more evidence is needed.
    *  The paper could benefit from a more extensive ablation study to isolate the contribution of different components of the FoD model (e.g., the mean-reversion term).
    *  The evaluation could be expanded to include other datasets, metrics, and comparisons with a broader range of state-of-the-art methods.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   FoD could inspire further research into simplified diffusion models and alternative training objectives.
    *   The connections to flow matching and rectified flow could lead to new hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of both frameworks.
    *   The practical efficiency of FoD could make it attractive for applications where computational resources are limited.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to generative modeling with promising initial results. However, the experimental results, while competitive, are not a massive leap forward, and the unconditional generation capabilities lag behind other methods.  The analytical tractability and novel formulation of the SDE are significant technical contributions. The connections drawn to stochastic flow matching and rectified flow are also crucial. The method warrants further investigation and refinement.

### Mitigating Fine-tuning Risks in LLMs via Safety-Aware Probing Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16737v1)
- **Authors**: Chengcan Wu, Zhixin Zhang, Zeming Wei, Yihao Zhang, Meng Sun
- **Abstract**: The significant progress of large language models (LLMs) has led to remarkable achievements across numerous applications. However, their ability to generate harmful content has sparked substantial safety concerns. Despite the implementation of safety alignment techniques during the pre-training phase, recent research indicates that fine-tuning LLMs on adversarial or even benign data can inadvertently compromise their safety. In this paper, we re-examine the fundamental issue of why fine-tuning on non-harmful data still results in safety degradation. We introduce a safety-aware probing (SAP) optimization framework designed to mitigate the safety risks of fine-tuning LLMs. Specifically, SAP incorporates a safety-aware probe into the gradient propagation process, mitigating the model's risk of safety degradation by identifying potential pitfalls in gradient directions, thereby enhancing task-specific performance while successfully preserving model safety. Our extensive experimental results demonstrate that SAP effectively reduces harmfulness below the original fine-tuned model and achieves comparable test loss to standard fine-tuning methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/ChengcanWu/SAP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of safety degradation in large language models (LLMs) that occurs even after fine-tuning on benign data.  It proposes a Safety-Aware Probing (SAP) optimization framework to mitigate these risks.  SAP incorporates a safety-aware probe into the gradient propagation process, which helps to avoid parameter updates that compromise model safety.  The authors hypothesize and provide empirical evidence that useful/critical gradient directions for task performance can be entangled with safety-critical directions, leading to unintended safety compromises during fine-tuning. SAP aims to disentangle these directions.  Experimental results show that SAP effectively reduces harmfulness while maintaining comparable task-specific performance compared to standard fine-tuning methods and outperforms several existing defense strategies. The approach is also shown to improve robustness against adversarial attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to address safety degradation during LLM fine-tuning. The idea of a safety-aware probe is a well-motivated addition to existing methods, and the analysis of the entanglement between useful and safety-critical gradient directions is a significant contribution to the understanding of the problem. The technique does seem like a logical extension of sharpness aware minimization (SAM) and adversarial weight perturbation (AWP) techniques, which reduces the novelty to some degree, but the application to safety is novel.
*   **Significance:**  The problem of unintended safety compromises during fine-tuning is an important and practical concern for the deployment of LLMs.  The proposed SAP method provides a practical solution that addresses this concern without significantly sacrificing task-specific performance. Its compatibility with other safety methods is a strength, and the results regarding adversarial robustness are also noteworthy.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and strong motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with a theoretically grounded safety loss function.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple models, datasets, and attack scenarios.
    *   Demonstrated compatibility with existing defense methods.
    *   Analysis of the training dynamics, specifically characterizing the $L_{su}$ dynamic.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational overhead: The approach increases the processing time compared to standard fine-tuning. The paper mentions roughly a two-fold increase in training time and increased GPU utilization.
    *   The choice of hyperparameters for the safety-aware probe (e.g., layers to probe, update step sizes) may require tuning for different models and datasets, potentially increasing the development effort. The paper does explore the parameters in the ablation study, but does not offer a specific guide on how to efficiently tune the parameters.
    *   The theoretical derivations, although present, are not thoroughly developed and could benefit from a more rigorous mathematical treatment. It appears that the method is empirically driven, and the theory is presented to justify the approach.
    *   The technique is a derivative of SAM and AWP, which diminishes the novelty of the proposed technique.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and safe LLMs, particularly in downstream applications. The safety-aware probing framework provides a useful tool for researchers and practitioners.
    *   The method appears generally applicable, which means it can be integrated into most existing finetuning workflows.
    *   The approach provides robustness against adversarial attacks, and harmful data poisoning attacks.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper addresses a critical problem with a well-motivated and effective solution. The experimental results are convincing and demonstrate the benefits of the proposed SAP framework. The compatibility with other defenses is a significant strength, making the approach practical and readily deployable. While the computational overhead and hyperparameter tuning represent limitations, the benefits of improved safety and robustness outweigh these drawbacks. Also, the approach is derivative of other techniques in optimization, which diminishes the novelty of the technique.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TRIM: Achieving Extreme Sparsity with Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven Pruning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16743v1)
- **Authors**: Florentin Beck, William Rudman, Carsten Eickhoff
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) present significant computational and memory challenges due to their extensive size, making pruning essential for their efficient deployment. Existing one-shot pruning methods often apply uniform sparsity constraints across layers or within each layer, resulting in suboptimal performance, especially at high sparsity ratios. This work introduces TRIM (Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven pruning), a novel approach that applies varying sparsity ratios to individual output dimensions (rows) within each layer. TRIM employs an iterative adjustment process guided by quality metrics to optimize dimension-wise sparsity allocation, focusing on reducing variance in quality retention across outputs to preserve critical information. TRIM can be seamlessly integrated with existing layer-wise pruning strategies. Our evaluations on perplexity and zero-shot tasks across diverse LLM families (Qwen2.5, LLaMA-2, and OPT) and sparsity levels demonstrate that TRIM achieves new state-of-the-art results and enhances stability. For instance, at 80% sparsity, TRIM reduces perplexity by 48% for Qwen2.5-14B and over 90% for OPT-13B compared to baseline methods. We conclude that fine-grained, dimension-wise sparsity adaptation is crucial for pushing the limits of extreme LLM compression. Code available at: https://github.com/flobk/TRIM
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRIM (Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven Pruning), a novel approach to pruning large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing pruning methods that often apply uniform sparsity constraints, TRIM adaptively assigns varying sparsity ratios to individual output dimensions (rows) within each layer. This is achieved through an iterative process guided by quality metrics that focus on reducing variance in quality retention across different outputs.  TRIM can be seamlessly integrated with existing layer-wise pruning strategies and is evaluated across various LLM families (Qwen2.5, LLaMA-2, and OPT) and sparsity levels.  The paper claims state-of-the-art results and enhanced stability, demonstrating significant improvements in perplexity and zero-shot task performance, especially at high sparsity ratios. The authors provide code for reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the concept of dimension-wise sparsity adaptation, a level of granularity beyond existing layer-wise approaches.  This is a meaningful step, as the authors convincingly argue and empirically demonstrate that different output dimensions have varying sensitivities to pruning and importance for overall performance.  The iterative metric-driven approach to adjusting sparsity ratios is also a novel contribution. The integration with existing layer-wise pruning strategies adds to its practical applicability.

* **Significance:** The ability to achieve high sparsity ratios (e.g., 80% or more) while maintaining or even improving performance is highly significant. This directly addresses the critical challenge of deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments. The paper provides compelling evidence that TRIM pushes the limits of LLM compression, achieving substantial reductions in perplexity compared to strong baselines. Demonstrating this across multiple model families and sizes strengthens the claim's generalizability. The impact on downstream zero-shot performance further emphasizes the practical benefits of the approach.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing uniform or layer-wise pruning methods.
    * **Novel Approach:** The dimension-wise sparsity adaptation with iterative adjustment is a significant departure from existing techniques.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results, including perplexity evaluations and zero-shot task performance, across multiple model families and sparsity levels.  The improvements over strong baselines (Wanda, OWL, AlphaPruning) are substantial, especially at high sparsity ratios.
    * **Thorough Analysis:**  The analysis section provides valuable insights into why TRIM works. The authors investigate the varying sensitivities of output dimensions to pruning and the role of outlier features.  This analysis strengthens the theoretical foundation of the approach.
    * **Reproducibility:**  The authors provide code, detailed experimental setups, and hyperparameter settings, which significantly enhances the reproducibility of the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Overhead:** While the paper claims that TRIM adds little computational overhead, a more detailed analysis of the time complexity of the iterative adjustment process would be beneficial. The appendix addresses this, but a more prominent discussion in the main paper would be stronger.
    * **Metric Selection:** The quality metric used to guide the iterative process is crucial.  While the paper evaluates different metrics, a deeper discussion of the rationale for choosing cosine similarity as the default and the conditions under which other metrics might be more suitable would be helpful.
    * **Limited scope of safeguards:** A "no risk" justification is used for not providing safeguards. However, with the broad use of LLMs it's worth considering possible misuse.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM pruning. The concept of dimension-wise sparsity adaptation is likely to be adopted and extended by other researchers. The insights into the varying sensitivities of output dimensions could inform the development of more efficient and effective pruning algorithms. The practical benefits of TRIM for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments are likely to drive its adoption in real-world applications.

* **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:** The score reflects a combination of the paper's novelty, the significance of its results, and the thoroughness of its analysis. While the idea is incremental, the benefits are substantial and clearly demonstrated. Some minor weaknesses are noted (a more thorough runtime analysis and safeguards), but they do not significantly detract from the overall contribution.

### Self-Rewarding Large Vision-Language Models for Optimizing Prompts in Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16763v1)
- **Authors**: Hongji Yang, Yucheng Zhou, Wencheng Han, Jianbing Shen
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image models are powerful for producing high-quality images based on given text prompts, but crafting these prompts often requires specialized vocabulary. To address this, existing methods train rewriting models with supervision from large amounts of manually annotated data and trained aesthetic assessment models. To alleviate the dependence on data scale for model training and the biases introduced by trained models, we propose a novel prompt optimization framework, designed to rephrase a simple user prompt into a sophisticated prompt to a text-to-image model. Specifically, we employ the large vision language models (LVLMs) as the solver to rewrite the user prompt, and concurrently, employ LVLMs as a reward model to score the aesthetics and alignment of the images generated by the optimized prompt. Instead of laborious human feedback, we exploit the prior knowledge of the LVLM to provide rewards, i.e., AI feedback. Simultaneously, the solver and the reward model are unified into one model and iterated in reinforcement learning to achieve self-improvement by giving a solution and judging itself. Results on two popular datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms other strong competitors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Self-Rewarding Large Vision-Language Models for Optimizing Prompts in Text-to-Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel prompt optimization framework for text-to-image generation that leverages Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). Unlike previous methods that rely on manually annotated data or pre-trained aesthetic assessment models, this approach employs LVLMs as both the solver (to rewrite user prompts into more sophisticated ones) and the reward model (to score the aesthetics and alignment of generated images). The key innovation is the "self-rewarding" mechanism where the LVLM provides AI feedback to itself, iteratively improving prompt rewriting and image quality evaluation capabilities through reinforcement learning. This framework aims to alleviate dependence on large-scale datasets, human annotation, and potential biases introduced by pre-trained models.  Experiments on Beautiful-Prompt and DiffusionDB datasets demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms several strong competitors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a clear and innovative approach by using LVLM’s as both prompt rewriters and evaluators. The self-rewarding aspect is a significant step forward, mitigating some of the data dependencies of previous methods. Reinforcement learning is also a key component that makes this paper unique compared to others.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field. The self-rewarding approach addresses a critical bottleneck in prompt optimization: the reliance on expensive and potentially biased human annotation. By reducing this dependency, the framework makes prompt optimization more accessible and scalable. Improved prompt generation capabilities could lead to significant advancements in text-to-image models. The use of vision-language reasoning, and more specifically LLMs, is another advantage that demonstrates clear improvement with respect to other models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem and solution:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing prompt optimization methods and proposes a well-defined and elegant solution.
    *   **Innovative use of LVLMs:** Exploiting the capabilities of LVLMs as both solver and reward model is a clever and efficient strategy.
    *   **Self-rewarding mechanism:** The iterative self-improvement approach is a valuable contribution to reinforcement learning and prompt optimization.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results on multiple datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   **Code Structure and organization:** The framework is organized in key components that are easy to follow throughout the paper.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper includes details that show it is reproducible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** While the self-rewarding mechanism reduces the need for human annotation, it likely increases the computational cost of training, especially with large LVLMs. The paper mentions the use of VILA-40B but then uses the smaller model VILA-v1.5-8b.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Bias:** The paper claims to avoid biases introduced by pre-trained models. It would be important to include potential biases in the LLVM model.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of text-to-image generation, with a clear and novel approach. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the experimental results are convincing.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Safety Detectors Aren't Enough: A Stealthy and Effective Jailbreak Attack on LLMs via Steganographic Techniques
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16765v1)
- **Authors**: Jianing Geng, Biao Yi, Zekun Fei, Tongxi Wu, Lihai Nie, Zheli Liu
- **Abstract**: Jailbreak attacks pose a serious threat to large language models (LLMs) by bypassing built-in safety mechanisms and leading to harmful outputs. Studying these attacks is crucial for identifying vulnerabilities and improving model security. This paper presents a systematic survey of jailbreak methods from the novel perspective of stealth. We find that existing attacks struggle to simultaneously achieve toxic stealth (concealing toxic content) and linguistic stealth (maintaining linguistic naturalness). Motivated by this, we propose StegoAttack, a fully stealthy jailbreak attack that uses steganography to hide the harmful query within benign, semantically coherent text. The attack then prompts the LLM to extract the hidden query and respond in an encrypted manner. This approach effectively hides malicious intent while preserving naturalness, allowing it to evade both built-in and external safety mechanisms. We evaluate StegoAttack on four safety-aligned LLMs from major providers, benchmarking against eight state-of-the-art methods. StegoAttack achieves an average attack success rate (ASR) of 92.00%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 11.0%. Its ASR drops by less than 1% even under external detection (e.g., Llama Guard). Moreover, it attains the optimal comprehensive scores on stealth detection metrics, demonstrating both high efficacy and exceptional stealth capabilities. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/StegoAttack-Jail66
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "When Safety Detectors Aren't Enough: A Stealthy and Effective Jailbreak Attack on LLMs via Steganographic Techniques" introduces StegoAttack, a novel jailbreak attack on Large Language Models (LLMs) that emphasizes stealth. The key idea is to use steganography to hide malicious queries within benign text, thereby bypassing both built-in and external safety mechanisms. StegoAttack involves: (1) hiding the harmful query by using the first letter of each sentence in a paragraph of benign text to spell out the query, (2) designing prompt templates to guide the model in extracting and responding to the hidden query, and (3) implementing a feedback mechanism to refine the prompt and increase attack success. The paper evaluates StegoAttack against several state-of-the-art jailbreak methods and demonstrates that it achieves high attack success rates while maintaining significant stealth, even under external detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in its focus on stealth as a primary objective of jailbreak attacks and the introduction of steganography as a mechanism to achieve it. While previous works have explored toxic and linguistic stealth, the paper argues that they have done so in a less comprehensive way. StegoAttack attempts to achieve both simultaneously at the input and output levels. This focus is a valuable addition to the jailbreaking LLMs literature. However, the novelty could be debated since steganography is a well-established technique, and its application to jailbreaking, while creative, might be seen as a natural extension of existing stealth approaches.
*   **Significance:** The paper’s findings are significant because they highlight the limitations of current safety mechanisms in LLMs and demonstrate the potential for cleverly crafted attacks to bypass these defenses. The high attack success rate and stealth of StegoAttack raise concerns about the vulnerability of deployed LLMs and underscore the need for more robust and adaptive defense strategies. The comprehensive evaluation, including comparisons with state-of-the-art methods and the use of various safety detectors, strengthens the paper's claims.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing jailbreak attacks in terms of stealth and presents a compelling motivation for a new approach.
    *   **Well-Designed Methodology:** StegoAttack is well-designed, with a clear explanation of the steganography-based query embedding, prompt template design, and feedback mechanism.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, covering multiple LLMs, baseline methods, and safety detectors. The use of various metrics (ASR, BPR, PPL, LG Score, etc.) provides a comprehensive assessment of the attack's effectiveness and stealth.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors have provided the code for their attack, which is positive for reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scalability and Complexity:** The use of steganography, particularly the dynamic adaptation of prompts through feedback, may introduce scalability challenges for real-world scenarios where the target model is not readily accessible for iterative probing. While the paper shows successful applications on accessible LLMs, the performance with limited queries might be a problem.
    *   **Evaluation Reliance on LLMs:** The evaluation relies heavily on LLMs (GPT-4o) to judge the "harmfulness" of responses. While common, this can introduce subjectivity and potential biases. Clear guidelines and checks for the judgements would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Defender Perspective:** While the paper focuses on the attacker's perspective (StegoAttack), a deeper analysis from the defender's perspective would strengthen the significance. For example, discussing potential countermeasures, their limitations, or their costs would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** There's limited analysis of the specific cases where the StegoAttack fails. Understanding the reasons for failure would further improve the technique and provide additional insights for defense.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a noteworthy contribution. The focus on stealth and the creative application of steganography are valuable and well-executed. However, the reliance on iterative probing, potential scalability challenges, and the use of LLMs for judging harmfulness slightly temper the impact. On balance, the paper merits a solid score reflecting its novelty and significance.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### IFEval-Audio: Benchmarking Instruction-Following Capability in Audio-based Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16774v1)
- **Authors**: Yiming Gao, Bin Wang, Chengwei Wei, Shuo Sun, AiTi Aw
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong instruction-following capabilities in text-based tasks. However, this ability often deteriorates in multimodal models after alignment with non-text modalities such as images or audio. While several recent efforts have investigated instruction-following performance in text and vision-language models, instruction-following in audio-based large language models remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce IFEval-Audio, a novel evaluation dataset designed to assess the ability to follow instructions in an audio LLM. IFEval-Audio contains 280 audio-instruction-answer triples across six diverse dimensions: Content, Capitalization, Symbol, List Structure, Length, and Format. Each example pairs an audio input with a text instruction, requiring the model to generate an output that follows a specified structure. We benchmark state-of-the-art audio LLMs on their ability to follow audio-involved instructions. The dataset is released publicly to support future research in this emerging area.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IFEval-Audio, a novel evaluation dataset designed to benchmark the instruction-following capabilities of audio-based large language models (LLMs). The dataset comprises 280 audio-instruction-answer triples spanning six dimensions: Content, Capitalization, Symbol, List Structure, Length, and Format. It uses audio from various sources (speech, music, environmental sounds) and includes both rule-based and LLM-based evaluation methods to assess format adherence and semantic correctness. The authors benchmarked several state-of-the-art audio LLMs on the dataset and release it publicly to advance research in this emerging area. The experiments reveal variations in performance across models and dimensions, highlighting the need for improved multimodal alignment and fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the evaluation of multimodal LLMs. Instruction-following in text and image modalities is well-studied, but audio-based instruction-following has been largely unexplored. The creation of IFEval-Audio is a novel contribution. However, the dimensions and evaluation methods build upon prior work like IFEval, diminishing the level of innovation somewhat. While the application to audio is new, the core ideas are inspired by existing frameworks.
*   **Significance:** The paper has moderate significance. The development of a benchmark dataset for audio LLMs is valuable as it enables quantitative comparison of models and identifies areas for improvement. The identified weaknesses of existing models in handling specific instructions (e.g., capitalization, format) is useful for future research. The dataset's availability should stimulate further research in audio-based instruction following. However, the dataset size (280 triples) is relatively small. It is restricted to English audio and instructions which limits its broad applicability. Also, while it assesses semantic correctness, the evaluation methodology relies on LLMs which introduce inherent bias.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses an under-explored area of multimodal learning.
    *   Provides a comprehensive evaluation framework considering both format and content.
    *   Releases a publicly available dataset to facilitate further research.
    *   Uses diverse audio sources.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relatively small dataset size.
    *   English-only, limiting generalizability.
    *   Evaluation methodology relies on LLMs, introducing bias.
    *   The evaluation criteria, while comprehensive, could be considered somewhat derivative of existing instruction-following benchmarks, reducing the overall novelty.
    *   A deeper analysis of error cases in the model outputs, beyond simply reporting the IFR, SCR, and OSR, would provide greater insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** The dataset is likely to be used by researchers working on audio LLMs, leading to improvements in instruction-following capabilities. It can serve as a foundation for more extensive benchmarks in the future.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable, though not exceptionally groundbreaking, contribution to the field. It effectively addresses a gap in the current multimodal LLM research landscape by providing a new dataset and benchmark for audio-based instruction following. While the dataset's size and scope have limitations, it offers a solid starting point for more rigorous evaluation of these models. The evaluation of several existing models is insightful, and the public release of the dataset is a positive step towards fostering further research. While the approach draws from existing instruction-following evaluation methods, the application to audio and the specific design choices tailored to audio challenges are sufficient to warrant a rating above average.

### Reasoning Beyond Language: A Comprehensive Survey on Latent Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16782v1)
- **Authors**: Xinghao Chen, Anhao Zhao, Heming Xia, Xuan Lu, Hanlin Wang, Yanjun Chen, Wei Zhang, Jian Wang, Wenjie Li, Xiaoyu Shen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved impressive performance on complex reasoning tasks with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting. However, conventional CoT relies on reasoning steps explicitly verbalized in natural language, introducing inefficiencies and limiting its applicability to abstract reasoning. To address this, there has been growing research interest in latent CoT reasoning, where inference occurs within latent spaces. By decoupling reasoning from language, latent reasoning promises richer cognitive representations and more flexible, faster inference. Researchers have explored various directions in this promising field, including training methodologies, structural innovations, and internal reasoning mechanisms. This paper presents a comprehensive overview and analysis of this reasoning paradigm. We begin by proposing a unified taxonomy from four perspectives: token-wise strategies, internal mechanisms, analysis, and applications. We then provide in-depth discussions and comparative analyses of representative methods, highlighting their design patterns, strengths, and open challenges. We aim to provide a structured foundation for advancing this emerging direction in LLM reasoning. The relevant papers will be regularly updated at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/Awesome-Latent-CoT.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Reasoning Beyond Language: A Comprehensive Survey on Latent Chain-of-Thought Reasoning." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive survey of Latent Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, a paradigm where Large Language Models (LLMs) perform reasoning in latent spaces rather than explicitly verbalizing each step in natural language. It addresses the limitations of conventional CoT (computational inefficiency and the challenges of representing abstract reasoning linguistically). The authors propose a taxonomy organizing existing research into token-wise strategies, internal mechanisms (structural and representational), analysis, and applications. They provide in-depth discussions and comparative analyses of various methods, highlighting design patterns, strengths, and open challenges, aiming to provide a structured foundation for future research in this emerging area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Scope:** The survey is indeed comprehensive. It covers a wide range of existing work in Latent CoT, systematically categorizing different approaches and providing detailed explanations. The taxonomy helps in understanding the landscape of this relatively new field.
*   **Clear Taxonomy:** The proposed taxonomy is well-defined and useful. Categorizing latent CoT methods into token-wise strategies and internal mechanisms (structural and representational CoT) provides a good framework for understanding the different approaches.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides detailed analyses of various methods, comparing their strengths and weaknesses. This comparative analysis is valuable for researchers looking to understand the trade-offs between different approaches.
*   **Identification of Challenges and Future Directions:** The paper appropriately identifies key challenges facing the field, such as training difficulties, generalization issues, and interpretability concerns. It also outlines promising directions for future research, offering guidance for researchers looking to contribute to the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Novelty of the Survey Itself:** While the compilation and categorization of existing research are valuable, the survey doesn't present fundamentally *new* insights or a groundbreaking theoretical framework. It organizes and synthesizes, but does not significantly advance our theoretical understanding of latent reasoning.
*   **Depth of Analysis:** While the analysis is detailed, it's necessarily limited by the scope of a survey. A deeper, more critical examination of the underlying assumptions and limitations of the surveyed methods would strengthen the paper. For instance, the survey could offer greater critical reflections on what latent reasoning techniques actually "learn" compared to traditional CoT.
*   **Lack of New Benchmarking Considerations**: A stronger contribution could have been made by suggesting new evaluation paradigms or specific benchmark tasks tailored to highlight the unique advantages of latent reasoning versus explicit CoT.
*   **Limited Predictive Power**: While the future directions are well-articulated, the paper could have been more speculative in its predictions about the future of latent reasoning. Predicting or projecting the key areas of growth and which applications are likely to be most successful are also missing.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its ability to organize and synthesize a fragmented field. It provides a valuable resource for researchers looking to understand the current state of Latent CoT reasoning and identify promising areas for future research. It can accelerate progress in the field by providing a common understanding of the key concepts and challenges.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a substantial contribution through its comprehensive review and taxonomy of latent CoT. However, its primary value is in consolidating existing knowledge rather than providing groundbreaking new insights or developing new theoretical concepts. The analysis is well-executed, but could benefit from more critical reflection and speculative future projection. The lack of new benchmarking discussion also impacts the practical implications that could have arisen from the paper. Therefore, based on its novelty in organizing and presenting known research rather than directly advancing it, a score of 7 seems justified.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CoTSRF: Utilize Chain of Thought as Stealthy and Robust Fingerprint of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16785v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenzhen Ren, GuoBiao Li, Sheng Li, Zhenxing Qian, Xinpeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Despite providing superior performance, open-source large language models (LLMs) are vulnerable to abusive usage. To address this issue, recent works propose LLM fingerprinting methods to identify the specific source LLMs behind suspect applications. However, these methods fail to provide stealthy and robust fingerprint verification. In this paper, we propose a novel LLM fingerprinting scheme, namely CoTSRF, which utilizes the Chain of Thought (CoT) as the fingerprint of an LLM. CoTSRF first collects the responses from the source LLM by querying it with crafted CoT queries. Then, it applies contrastive learning to train a CoT extractor that extracts the CoT feature (i.e., fingerprint) from the responses. Finally, CoTSRF conducts fingerprint verification by comparing the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the CoT features of the source and suspect LLMs against an empirical threshold. Various experiments have been conducted to demonstrate the advantage of our proposed CoTSRF for fingerprinting LLMs, particularly in stealthy and robust fingerprint verification.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided paper abstract and the first few pages to provide a comprehensive summary and critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper "CoTSRF: Utilize Chain of Thought as Stealthy and Robust Fingerprint of Large Language Models" proposes a novel LLM fingerprinting scheme.  It aims to address the problem of abusive usage and copyright infringement of open-source LLMs. The proposed method, CoTSRF, leverages the Chain of Thought (CoT) reasoning process as a fingerprint. The process involves: 1) crafting CoT queries and using them to elicit responses from the source LLM; 2) training a CoT extractor using contrastive learning to identify specific logical reasoning patterns within the LLM's responses; and 3) verifying fingerprint infringement by comparing the Kullback-Leibler divergence of CoT features between the source and suspect LLMs. The paper argues that CoTSRF is both stealthy and robust because it uses standard CoT prompting (avoiding detection by malicious users) and operates at the feature level (making it resilient to output perturbations). The paper claims contributions in leveraging CoT for black-box LLM fingerprinting and achieving improved stealth and robustness in the verification process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using the Chain of Thought (CoT) process as a fingerprint is the most novel aspect of the paper.  Existing LLM fingerprinting methods often rely on adversarial prefixes or directly modifying the LLM (watermarking), which are either easily detectable or alter the LLM's performance. Exploiting the intrinsic reasoning pattern, as captured by the CoT, represents a more subtle and potentially less disruptive approach.  The use of contrastive learning to train a CoT extractor is a sensible and appropriate methodology for this task. High-Temperature Data Augmentation to generate diverse CoTs is another minor yet valuable addition.

**Significance:** The problem of LLM infringement is genuinely significant. The widespread availability of open-source LLMs necessitates mechanisms for protecting intellectual property and preventing malicious use. A robust fingerprinting method addresses this directly. The potential advantages of stealthiness and robustness (if validated) are also practically valuable, as these are essential characteristics for a real-world fingerprinting solution.
**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of CoT-based fingerprinting.
*   **Practical Goals:** Focus on stealthiness and robustness addresses key real-world concerns.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The CoT extraction, contrastive learning, and KL divergence comparison are logically sound.
*   **Good Evaluation Design:** the paper introduces unseen LLMs for benchmarking purposes

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Justification for CoT as a Unique Fingerprint:** While the paper *claims* CoT is uniquely related to LLM architecture/training, stronger empirical evidence or theoretical backing for this claim would significantly strengthen the argument. The paper provides a citation, but does not elaborate on this connection.
*   **Dependence on Empirical Thresholds:**  The fingerprint verification relies on comparing the KL divergence against a threshold (τ).  The selection of this threshold is empirical, and the paper needs to discuss the sensitivity of the results to this choice. A small change in τ can have big impact on accuracy
*   **Potential Vulnerabilities to Advanced Adversarial Attacks:**  A determined attacker could potentially manipulate the CoT process itself through targeted fine-tuning to mimic the CoT patterns of the source LLM. The paper needs to acknowledge and discuss the potential for this kind of adversarial manipulation, even if it is beyond the scope of the current work.
*   **Lack of ablations and deeper analysis:** The paper does not provide sufficient ablation experiments to reveal the effect of the different hyper-parameters. The overall writing style is descriptive rather than analytical and it will significantly benefit from a deeper analysis.
*   **Results from TRAP:** Some of the results are duplicated from the original paper and don't directly compare it. The authors should try to obtain better results for a fair comparison.

**Potential Impact:** If the method proves to be genuinely robust and stealthy in a wider range of scenarios, it could become a valuable tool for LLM providers. However, more thorough validation is needed.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a promising and innovative approach to an important problem. However, it is not without limitations. The core concept of CoT-based fingerprinting is novel. The arguments for using CoT as a unique feature of LLMs is not convincingly established. While the experiments provide good initial results, further validation with larger models and against more sophisticated adversarial attacks is required. The dependence on the empirical threshold is also a notable weakness.
Overall, the paper demonstrates good innovation, but has some deficiencies, therefore it is unlikely to have significant impact in the field.

### Accidental Misalignment: Fine-Tuning Language Models Induces Unexpected Vulnerability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16789v1)
- **Authors**: Punya Syon Pandey, Samuel Simko, Kellin Pelrine, Zhijing Jin
- **Abstract**: As large language models gain popularity, their vulnerability to adversarial attacks remains a primary concern. While fine-tuning models on domain-specific datasets is often employed to improve model performance, it can introduce vulnerabilities within the underlying model. In this work, we investigate Accidental Misalignment, unexpected vulnerabilities arising from characteristics of fine-tuning data. We begin by identifying potential correlation factors such as linguistic features, semantic similarity, and toxicity within our experimental datasets. We then evaluate the adversarial performance of these fine-tuned models and assess how dataset factors correlate with attack success rates. Lastly, we explore potential causal links, offering new insights into adversarial defense strategies and highlighting the crucial role of dataset design in preserving model alignment. Our code is available at https://github.com/psyonp/accidental_misalignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Accidental Misalignment: Fine-Tuning Language Models Induces Unexpected Vulnerability" investigates how fine-tuning language models on domain-specific datasets can inadvertently introduce vulnerabilities, even when the datasets themselves are benign. The authors term this phenomenon "Accidental Misalignment." They explore the correlation between dataset characteristics (linguistic features, semantic similarity, toxicity) and the success rate of adversarial attacks on the fine-tuned models. They fine-tune LLaMA 3.1 8B model on several datasets, including benign, legal, cybersecurity, and harmful examples. The authors use jailbreaking techniques from the HarmBench framework to evaluate the model's vulnerability and present feature isolation experiments to identify potential causal links and guide dataset design strategies for increased robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of "Accidental Misalignment" is a valuable contribution, highlighting a nuanced risk beyond simply training on overtly harmful data. The observation that seemingly benign datasets can inadvertently degrade a model's safety measures is not necessarily a brand new discovery, but the systematic investigation into *why* this occurs and *which* dataset features are most impactful is a strength. The focus on *dataset-level* characteristics rather than individual data points is a welcome departure from existing adversarial vulnerability literature and provides novel avenues for research.

*   **Significance:** Understanding how dataset characteristics can compromise LLM safety is highly significant as LLMs are increasingly deployed in real-world applications where robustness is critical. The empirical approach and the identification of correlations offer practical insights for improving dataset curation and adversarial defense strategies. The feature-isolation experiments, while preliminary, point towards potential mitigation strategies and offer guidance for future research. The focus on the *entire* dataset's properties is also a novel and useful viewpoint and will encourage future work to analyze the holistic impacts of specific traits in training data.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of accidental misalignment and frames it within the context of increasing LLM deployment and adversarial threats.
    *   **Empirical Approach:** The systematic experimentation, including fine-tuning on diverse datasets, adversarial evaluations, and correlation analysis, provides a solid foundation for the findings.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The identification of specific dataset features (e.g., token count of responses, prompt toxicity, type-token ratio) that correlate with increased vulnerability offers actionable guidance for dataset curation.
    *   **Feature-Isolation Experiments:** The feature intervention experiments help establish potential causal links between dataset characteristics and model robustness.
    *   **Use of Established Benchmarks:** Leveraging existing toolkits like HarmBench provides a standard framework for evaluation.
    *   **Generalizability:** The inclusion of multiple datasets across different domains adds weight to the overall analysis.
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper makes a notable effort to address a current gap in the literature in that it tries to address *broader* dataset features that are impacting vulnerability. It does a good job of taking the broader properties of these training datasets into account and using that to draw helpful conclusions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Causality:** The correlation analysis establishes associations, but definitive causal relationships are difficult to prove based on the experiments conducted. The feature-isolation experiments are a step in the right direction, but more rigorous causal inference techniques could strengthen the claims.
    *   **Limited Model:** The study relies on a single model architecture (LLaMA 3.1 8B) and primarily a single attack method (PEZ for intervention testing), which limits the generalizability of the findings. The generalizability section is still limited, and future works will have to conduct wider tests to see if the findings hold true.
    *   **Effect Size:** The reported ASR increases, while statistically significant, are sometimes moderate in magnitude. The practical implications of these changes could be further explored.
    *   **Univariate Ablations:** The usage of univariate ablations is also a notable limitation, as more complex datasets often have complex interactions between different variables. Further exploration and examination of relationships between different attributes could have revealed more insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   **Dataset Curation:** Emphasizes the need for careful dataset design and pre-screening beyond simply avoiding overtly harmful content.
    *   **Adversarial Defense:** Guides the development of defense strategies that are tailored to specific dataset characteristics.
    *   **Model Robustness:** Motivates research into training techniques that are less susceptible to accidental misalignment.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The concept of "Accidental Misalignment" is a valuable contribution, the systematic investigation into dataset characteristics and their correlation with adversarial vulnerability is well-executed, and the findings offer practical insights for improving LLM safety. The novelty, while not groundbreaking, is substantial. The authors address a gap in the literature by focusing on broader dataset traits, making the work particularly significant. Furthermore, the paper offers plenty of paths for future work and discussion, highlighting just how important the overall data curation process can be.
