### Learning Flexible Forward Trajectories for Masked Molecular Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16790v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunjin Seo, Taewon Kim, Sihyun Yu, SungSoo Ahn
- **Abstract**: Masked diffusion models (MDMs) have achieved notable progress in modeling discrete data, while their potential in molecular generation remains underexplored. In this work, we explore their potential and introduce the surprising result that naively applying standards MDMs severely degrades the performance. We identify the critical cause of this issue as a state-clashing problem-where the forward diffusion of distinct molecules collapse into a common state, resulting in a mixture of reconstruction targets that cannot be learned using typical reverse diffusion process with unimodal predictions. To mitigate this, we propose Masked Element-wise Learnable Diffusion (MELD) that orchestrates per-element corruption trajectories to avoid collision between distinct molecular graphs. This is achieved through a parameterized noise scheduling network that assigns distinct corruption rates to individual graph elements, i.e., atoms and bonds. Extensive experiments on diverse molecular benchmarks reveal that MELD markedly enhances overall generation quality compared to element-agnostic noise scheduling, increasing the chemical validity of vanilla MDMs on ZINC250K from 15% to 93%, Furthermore, it achieves state-of-the-art property alignment in conditional generation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating molecules using masked diffusion models (MDMs). It identifies a "state-clashing" problem, where distinct molecules collapse into the same intermediate state during the forward diffusion process, leading to poor generation quality. To mitigate this, the authors propose Masked Element-wise Learnable Diffusion (MELD). MELD introduces a parameterized noise scheduling network that assigns distinct corruption rates to individual atoms and bonds, avoiding the state-clashing issue.  Experiments on various molecular benchmarks demonstrate that MELD significantly improves generation quality, particularly in chemical validity and property alignment, achieving state-of-the-art results in conditional generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in:

1.  **Identifying and Articulating the State-Clashing Problem:**  While diffusion models have been applied to molecule generation before, the paper clearly defines and explains the "state-clashing" issue in MDMs for molecular graphs. This is a valuable contribution as it explains the limitations of directly applying generic MDMs to molecular data.
2.  **MELD's Element-Wise Learnable Diffusion:**  The idea of learning element-specific (atom and bond) noise schedules is novel within the context of MDMs for molecule generation.  It moves away from the uniform noise schedules typically used in MDMs and offers a more nuanced approach to controlling the forward diffusion process.
3.  **Joint Learning of Forward and Reverse Processes:**  The architecture jointly learns both the forward (noise scheduling) and the reverse (denoising) processes. This end-to-end approach differentiates MELD from methods that use fixed noise schedules.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Molecular Generation:** The empirical results clearly demonstrate that MELD significantly improves the validity of generated molecules, addressing a major hurdle in using MDMs for molecule generation. The state-of-the-art results for conditional generation suggest practical benefits for drug discovery and material design.
*   **Generalizability:** By addressing a fundamental problem with MDMs on graph structured data, the method, or at least the insights, may generalize beyond molecules to other graph generation tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper presents the state-clashing problem clearly, making it easy to understand why existing MDMs fail in this domain.
*   **Well-Designed Solution:**  MELD directly addresses the state-clashing problem with a plausible mechanism: element-wise noise scheduling.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experimental section includes a range of benchmarks (QM9, ZINC250K, Polymers) and ablation studies, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of MELD.
*   **Qualitative Analysis:** The paper includes insightful qualitative analysis, such as visualizing prediction entropy, to support the claimed benefits of MELD.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** The addition of a noise scheduling network increases the model's complexity. While the performance gains justify this, it would be valuable to understand the computational overhead and scalability of MELD compared to simpler MDMs.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper offers an intuitive explanation for why MELD works but lacks a rigorous theoretical analysis of the state-clashing problem or the optimality of the learned noise schedules.
*   **Practical considerations:** The paper lacks discussions related to the implications for downstream tasks such as computational time or cost.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of molecular generation in the following ways:

*   **Spurring Further Research on Noise Scheduling:**  MELD could inspire other researchers to explore more sophisticated noise scheduling techniques for MDMs and other generative models.
*   **Broader Adoption of MDMs for Molecules:** By addressing a key limitation of MDMs, the paper could encourage wider adoption of this approach for molecular generation.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7/10.

*   **Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying and addressing the state-clashing problem in MDMs for molecular graphs. MELD offers a novel solution (element-wise learnable diffusion) and achieves significant improvements in generation quality. While there is a lack of a theoretical analysis and discussion on practical considerations, the empirical results are strong and convincingly demonstrate the benefits of the proposed approach. The work offers a valuable advancement of diffusion models for molecular graphs, contributing to the fields of generative models and molecule design. While the technical novelty might not be revolutionary, its impact in making MDMs practical for molecular generation is substantial.
*   The clear problem articulation and strong experimental validation weigh in favor of a strong positive evaluation.
*  Future investigation of practical performance considerations would be necessary to justify a higher score.

### REPA Works Until It Doesn't: Early-Stopped, Holistic Alignment Supercharges Diffusion Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16792v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqiao Wang, Wangbo Zhao, Yuhao Zhou, Zekai Li, Zhiyuan Liang, Mingjia Shi, Xuanlei Zhao, Pengfei Zhou, Kaipeng Zhang, Zhangyang Wang, Kai Wang, Yang You
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) deliver state-of-the-art image quality, yet their training remains notoriously slow. A recent remedy -- representation alignment (REPA) that matches DiT hidden features to those of a non-generative teacher (e.g. DINO) -- dramatically accelerates the early epochs but plateaus or even degrades performance later. We trace this failure to a capacity mismatch: once the generative student begins modelling the joint data distribution, the teacher's lower-dimensional embeddings and attention patterns become a straitjacket rather than a guide. We then introduce HASTE (Holistic Alignment with Stage-wise Termination for Efficient training), a two-phase schedule that keeps the help and drops the hindrance. Phase I applies a holistic alignment loss that simultaneously distills attention maps (relational priors) and feature projections (semantic anchors) from the teacher into mid-level layers of the DiT, yielding rapid convergence. Phase II then performs one-shot termination that deactivates the alignment loss, once a simple trigger such as a fixed iteration is hit, freeing the DiT to focus on denoising and exploit its generative capacity. HASTE speeds up training of diverse DiTs without architecture changes. On ImageNet 256X256, it reaches the vanilla SiT-XL/2 baseline FID in 50 epochs and matches REPA's best FID in 500 epochs, amounting to a 28X reduction in optimization steps. HASTE also improves text-to-image DiTs on MS-COCO, demonstrating to be a simple yet principled recipe for efficient diffusion training across various tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/NUS-HPC-AI-Lab/HASTE .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "REPA Works Until It Doesn't: Early-Stopped, Holistic Alignment Supercharges Diffusion Training" addresses the slow training of Diffusion Transformers (DiTs).  It identifies a limitation of Representation Alignment (REPA), a technique that accelerates early training epochs by aligning DiT features with those of a non-generative teacher (e.g., DINO).  The paper argues that REPA's effectiveness diminishes later in training due to a "capacity mismatch," where the teacher's representations become a constraint. To overcome this, the authors propose HASTE (Holistic Alignment with Stage-wise Termination for Efficient training), a two-phase schedule: Phase I uses a holistic alignment loss (attention maps and features) for rapid convergence, and Phase II deactivates the alignment loss after a trigger, allowing the DiT to focus on denoising and generative capacity.  HASTE is shown to accelerate DiT training on ImageNet and MS-COCO without architecture changes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in identifying the capacity mismatch issue that arises when using REPA for extended training and proposing the HASTE schedule to address it. The holistic alignment by integrating attention maps alongside features is a worthwhile addition, though not revolutionary. Stage-wise termination based on gradient similarity as a signal for switching from teacher-guided learning to self-improvement also appears to be new. However, the core idea of early stopping or decaying the influence of a teacher is well-established in other areas of machine learning.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical impact of accelerated DiT training. Diffusion models are computationally expensive, and the presented approach offers a relatively simple way to improve efficiency without requiring specialized kernels or complex hyperparameter tuning. Achieving a 28x speed-up while maintaining performance is substantial. The fact that HASTE requires minimal code changes makes it easily adoptable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of diminishing returns with REPA.
    *   **Well-Supported Claims:** The claims are supported by empirical results on standard datasets (ImageNet, MS-COCO).
    *   **Gradient-Angle Analysis:** Using gradient angles to diagnose the problem is insightful and provides a quantitative basis for the termination strategy.
    *   **Simplicity:** HASTE's simplicity (a few lines of code) increases its potential for adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the "capacity mismatch" explanation is intuitive, a more rigorous theoretical analysis would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Fixed Termination Trigger:** The termination trigger (fixed iteration) might not be optimal across all datasets and model architectures. While the gradient-angle trigger is mentioned, the results primarily focus on the fixed-iteration strategy.
    *   **Incremental Advance:** While practically significant, HASTE builds heavily on existing work (REPA). The fundamental ideas of representation alignment and knowledge distillation are not new. The contribution is primarily in the schedule and identifying when to stop the alignment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's potential influence is reasonably high.  The speed-up achieved and the ease of implementation make HASTE an attractive option for researchers and practitioners working with DiTs. It is likely to become a standard technique or, at least, a point of comparison for future work on efficient diffusion model training. The analysis of the REPA limitation will likely guide future research in this area.

*   **Critiques:**
    *   The paper presents HASTE as architecture-agnostic. Although the paper provides solid results, there is a need to validate these findings on other DiT architectures besides the SiT and DiT family.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying and addressing a practical limitation of an existing technique (REPA) for accelerating DiT training.  The proposed HASTE method is effective, simple to implement, and yields significant performance improvements. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing work in representation alignment and early stopping. A deeper theoretical analysis and a more adaptive termination strategy (beyond a fixed iteration) would have increased the paper's impact. Nonetheless, the empirical results and clear problem definition make this a solid contribution that will likely be adopted and cited by other researchers in the field.

### SEED: Speaker Embedding Enhancement Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16798v1)
- **Authors**: KiHyun Nam, Jungwoo Heo, Jee-weon Jung, Gangin Park, Chaeyoung Jung, Ha-Jin Yu, Joon Son Chung
- **Abstract**: A primary challenge when deploying speaker recognition systems in real-world applications is performance degradation caused by environmental mismatch. We propose a diffusion-based method that takes speaker embeddings extracted from a pre-trained speaker recognition model and generates refined embeddings. For training, our approach progressively adds Gaussian noise to both clean and noisy speaker embeddings extracted from clean and noisy speech, respectively, via forward process of a diffusion model, and then reconstructs them to clean embeddings in the reverse process. While inferencing, all embeddings are regenerated via diffusion process. Our method needs neither speaker label nor any modification to the existing speaker recognition pipeline. Experiments on evaluation sets simulating environment mismatch scenarios show that our method can improve recognition accuracy by up to 19.6% over baseline models while retaining performance on conventional scenarios. We publish our code here https://github.com/kaistmm/seed-pytorch
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEED (Speaker Embedding Enhancement Diffusion), a novel diffusion-based model designed to improve the robustness of speaker recognition systems in real-world environments. SEED addresses the challenge of performance degradation caused by environmental mismatches by refining speaker embeddings directly. The method involves training a diffusion model to progressively add Gaussian noise to both clean and noisy speaker embeddings (obtained from a pre-trained speaker recognition model) and then reconstruct the embeddings to a clean state. During inference, all embeddings are regenerated via the diffusion process.  SEED doesn't require speaker labels during training or modifications to the existing speaker recognition pipeline.  Experiments demonstrate that SEED improves recognition accuracy, especially under mismatched conditions, while maintaining performance in conventional scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of a diffusion model directly at the *embedding level* for speaker recognition. While diffusion models have been used for audio enhancement (waveform or spectrogram level), this paper explores their application within the already-extracted speaker embedding space. The core idea of using the forward and reverse diffusion process to bridge the gap between noisy and clean embeddings is innovative and provides a direct mechanism for improving speaker embeddings. Another key point is the approach does not require speaker labels, so the model can utilize clean speech dataset for training. However, similar denoising processes have been applied to other domains, which reduces the uniqueness.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in speaker recognition: the sensitivity to environmental noise and variations. By operating at the embedding level, SEED offers a computationally efficient approach compared to raw waveform or spectrogram enhancements, which are often more complex. The fact that it doesn't require speaker labels and can work with existing pre-trained models enhances its practical appeal and reduces the barrier to adoption. The results are promising, showing considerable accuracy improvements in mismatched conditions. This could have a practical effect in the performance of speaker ID in the real world.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of environmental mismatch and its impact on speaker recognition performance.
    * **Novel Approach:**  The use of diffusion models at the embedding level is a novel contribution.
    * **Practical Benefits:**  SEED is computationally lightweight, doesn't require speaker labels for training, and can be applied on top of existing models.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of SEED in improving performance, especially in mismatched environments. The improvements are statistically significant (reported the accuracy is improved by up to 19.6%) over the baseline.
    * **Good Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the methodology and experimental setup. The figures are helpful in understanding the core concepts.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper provides a good overview of the method but could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of why SEED works so effectively. It would be nice to have some discussion of the distribution of the embedding space that justify the improvement of the proposed method.
    * **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** SEED relies on the quality of the pre-trained speaker embedding network. While this simplifies deployment, the performance is still limited by the quality of the initial embedding.
    * **Modest architectural modification.** The architectural changes are modest.
    * **Lack of ablation study.** It would be good to have an ablation study that shows the effect of each component of the proposed SEED.

* **Potential Influence:**  SEED has the potential to become a valuable tool for improving the robustness of speaker recognition systems in real-world applications. It offers a practical and effective solution to the challenging problem of environmental mismatch. Future research may focus on improving robustness to other noises.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**.  While the core idea of applying diffusion models at the embedding level is novel and the results are significant, the lack of theoretical analysis, the dependency on pre-trained models (while also a benefit), and modest architectural modifications reduce its overall impact. The improvements in mismatched environments are substantial, making SEED a useful approach. Overall, SEED is a solid contribution with strong potential, but can be improved to have stronger potential.

### Learning Beyond Limits: Multitask Learning and Synthetic Data for Low-Resource Canonical Morpheme Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16800v1)
- **Authors**: Changbing Yang, Garrett Nicolai
- **Abstract**: We introduce a transformer-based morpheme segmentation system that augments a low-resource training signal through multitask learning and LLM-generated synthetic data. Our framework jointly predicts morphological segments and glosses from orthographic input, leveraging shared linguistic representations obtained through a common documentary process to enhance model generalization. To further address data scarcity, we integrate synthetic training data generated by large language models (LLMs) using in-context learning. Experimental results on the SIGMORPHON 2023 dataset show that our approach significantly improves word-level segmentation accuracy and morpheme-level F1-score across multiple low-resource languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a transformer-based approach to canonical morpheme segmentation for low-resource languages. It addresses the challenge of limited training data by employing two strategies: multitask learning (jointly predicting segmentation and glosses) and augmenting the training data with synthetic examples generated by large language models (LLMs) using in-context learning. The authors evaluate their approach on the SIGMORPHON 2023 dataset, demonstrating significant improvements in word-level segmentation accuracy and morpheme-level F1-score compared to baseline methods, especially in languages with complex morphology and limited resources.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining multitask learning and LLM-generated synthetic data for canonical morpheme segmentation is relatively novel, particularly in the context of low-resource languages. While both multitask learning and data augmentation are established techniques, their synergistic application in this specific domain, especially using LLMs for generating synthetic data reflecting morphological alternations, adds a layer of innovation. However, the use of LLMs for synthetic data generation is increasingly common, so this aspect alone is less groundbreaking. The joint prediction of segmentations and glosses is a good idea to leverage the information present in Interlinear Glossed Texts, which are common in linguistic documentation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the automation of linguistic documentation, particularly for endangered and under-resourced languages. Accurate morpheme segmentation is crucial for linguistic analysis and language preservation. By reducing the reliance on manual annotation and linguistic expertise, the proposed approach can democratize the documentation process and accelerate language revitalization efforts. The results reported are solid and show real improvement across multiple low-resource languages. The qualitative analysis showing that multitask learning reduces "unnecessary" modifications is insightful.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    *   The proposed approach is technically sound and well-explained.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over baseline methods across multiple languages.
    *   The authors provide insightful qualitative analysis of the model's behavior.
    *   The combination of methods provides complementary benefit that is validated.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on LLM-generated synthetic data raises concerns about data quality, linguistic validity, and potential biases. The paper acknowledges this limitation but lacks a detailed qualitative error analysis to assess the impact of synthetic data on segmentation quality. It would also be beneficial to evaluate the linguistic plausibility of generated data. The prompts used by the LLMs should have been described and justified in greater detail.
    *   The paper does not explore the impact of different LLMs or prompting strategies on the quality of synthetic data. The use of GPT-4 is justified, but the sensitivity of results to the specific LLM is not investigated. The prompt examples shown seem rather simplistic.
    *   The study is limited to a subset of languages in the SIGMORPHON 2023 dataset. Expanding the experiments to include a broader range of languages with diverse morphological characteristics would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The paper doesn't compare directly with all possible alternative approaches. For example, using a language model to augment only one of the tasks (segmentation or gloss prediction) or using a simple back-translation approach would be interesting ablations.
    *   The ethical considerations section, while present, is relatively brief. A more in-depth discussion of the potential risks associated with misusing LLM-generated linguistic data would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in low-resource NLP, morphological segmentation, and language documentation. The proposed approach could be extended to other morphological tasks, such as morphological generation and paradigm completion. It could also serve as a starting point for developing more sophisticated data augmentation techniques using LLMs. The paper's findings could also inform the development of best practices for collecting and stewarding language data from underserved communities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to a significant problem, demonstrates solid experimental results, and offers insightful qualitative analysis. However, the reliance on LLM-generated synthetic data and the limited exploration of its potential drawbacks and alternative strategies prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. It's a solid piece of work that moves the field forward, but it's not a paradigm shift.
