### UNCLE: Uncertainty Expressions in Long-Form Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16922v1)
- **Authors**: Ruihan Yang, Caiqi Zhang, Zhisong Zhang, Xinting Huang, Dong Yu, Nigel Collier, Deqing Yang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are prone to hallucination, particularly in long-form generations. A promising direction to mitigate hallucination is to teach LLMs to express uncertainty explicitly when they lack sufficient knowledge. However, existing work lacks direct and fair evaluation of LLMs' ability to express uncertainty effectively in long-form generation. To address this gap, we first introduce UNCLE, a benchmark designed to evaluate uncertainty expression in both long- and short-form question answering (QA). UNCLE spans five domains and comprises 4k long-form QA instances and over 20k short-form QA pairs. Our dataset is the first to directly bridge short- and long-form QA with paired questions and gold-standard answers. Along with the benchmark, we propose a suite of new metrics to assess the models' capabilities to selectively express uncertainty. Using UNCLE, we then demonstrate that current models fail to convey uncertainty appropriately in long-form generation. We further explore both prompt-based and training-based methods to improve models' performance, with the training-based methods yielding greater gains. Further analysis of alignment gaps between short- and long-form uncertainty expression highlights promising directions for future research using UNCLE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UNCLE, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to express uncertainty explicitly in both long-form and short-form question answering (QA).  UNCLE consists of approximately 4k long-form QA instances and over 20k short-form QA pairs, spanning five domains.  The dataset is unique in that it bridges short and long-form QA with paired questions and gold-standard answers.  The paper also proposes new metrics to evaluate models' capacity to selectively express uncertainty. The authors evaluate several LLMs on UNCLE, finding that current models struggle to convey uncertainty effectively in long-form generation. Finally, they explore both prompt-based and training-based methods to improve model performance, with training-based methods showing greater gains. They also highlight alignment gaps between short- and long-form uncertainty expression.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the creation of the UNCLE benchmark itself. Existing work largely focused on evaluating factuality without specifically assessing uncertainty expression or comparing uncertainty between short and long form responses. The paired question format linking short and long-form QA is a valuable contribution. The new metrics are a straightforward but necessary component. However, the prompt and training-based approaches are not particularly novel, employing standard techniques in the field. The investigation into alignment gaps is a good starting point, but is limited in scope.

**Significance:**  The benchmark addresses an important gap in the evaluation of LLMs, specifically their ability to communicate limitations in their knowledge. Explicitly expressing uncertainty is crucial for building trustworthy AI systems, especially in high-stakes applications. The findings that current LLMs struggle with this task highlight a significant area for future research and development. While the interventions are not revolutionary, they serve as a baseline and demonstration of the benchmark's utility. The dataset's size (4k long-form instances) is reasonable, but not overwhelmingly large, and therefore could benefit from further expansion.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of lacking direct and fair evaluation for LLMs' ability to express uncertainty in long-form generation.
*   **Novel Dataset:** UNCLE directly addresses this gap by providing a dedicated benchmark with paired short- and long-form QA instances.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Metrics:** The proposed metrics offer a more nuanced assessment of uncertainty expression than existing approaches.
*   **Practical Contributions:** The dataset provides a testbed for the community to evaluate existing LLMs and develop novel methods.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors have provided a link to the project page which promotes reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Interventions:** The prompt-based and training-based methods are standard techniques and lack significant innovation. While they show potential, they are not game-changing.
*   **Dataset Size:** While reasonable, the dataset could be larger to further improve the reliability of evaluations, particularly for less common types of queries or model behaviors.
*   **Metric Simplicity:** While the proposed metrics serve their purpose, they could be further refined to capture more nuanced aspects of uncertainty expression. For example, considering the type of uncertainty expression (e.g., epistemic vs. aleatoric) or the severity of the potential error.
*   **Alignment Gap Analysis:** The analysis of the alignment gap between short- and long-form uncertainty expression is a good starting point, but more in-depth analysis could reveal further insights.

**Potential Influence:**

UNCLE has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating uncertainty expression in LLMs.  This, in turn, could spur further research in this critical area, leading to more trustworthy and reliable AI systems.  However, its impact will depend on its adoption by the research community and the development of new methods that leverage UNCLE to achieve significant improvements in uncertainty expression. The paper will likely encourage further work on improving alignment and fine-grained uncertainty expression in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the UNCLE benchmark and the importance of the problem addressed, the paper makes a solid contribution. However, the limited scope of the interventions and the potential for further improvements in the dataset and metrics hold back the score. UNCLE provides a valuable tool for the community, but the paper itself doesn't represent a transformative breakthrough.

### LLaDA-V: Large Language Diffusion Models with Visual Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16933v1)
- **Authors**: Zebin You, Shen Nie, Xiaolu Zhang, Jun Hu, Jun Zhou, Zhiwu Lu, Ji-Rong Wen, Chongxuan Li
- **Abstract**: In this work, we introduce LLaDA-V, a purely diffusion-based Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) that integrates visual instruction tuning with masked diffusion models, representing a departure from the autoregressive paradigms dominant in current multimodal approaches. Built upon LLaDA, a representative large language diffusion model, LLaDA-V incorporates a vision encoder and MLP connector that projects visual features into the language embedding space, enabling effective multimodal alignment. Our empirical investigation reveals several intriguing results: First, LLaDA-V demonstrates promising multimodal performance despite its language model being weaker on purely textual tasks than counterparts like LLaMA3-8B and Qwen2-7B. When trained on the same instruction data, LLaDA-V is highly competitive to LLaMA3-V across multimodal tasks with better data scalability. It also narrows the performance gap to Qwen2-VL, suggesting the effectiveness of its architecture for multimodal tasks. Second, LLaDA-V achieves state-of-the-art performance in multimodal understanding compared to existing hybrid autoregressive-diffusion and purely diffusion-based MLLMs. Our findings suggest that large language diffusion models show promise in multimodal contexts and warrant further investigation in future research. Project page and codes: https://ml-gsai.github.io/LLaDA-V-demo/.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces LLaDA-V, a novel approach to multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that uses a purely diffusion-based model rather than the more common autoregressive models. LLaDA-V integrates visual instruction tuning with masked diffusion models. The authors demonstrate that LLaDA-V achieves competitive, and in some cases, state-of-the-art performance on various multimodal understanding tasks, showcasing the promise of large language diffusion models in multimodal contexts. The paper also investigates the data scalability of LLaDA-V compared to an autoregressive baseline (LLaMA3-V) and explores the impact of different attention mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in building a *purely* diffusion-based MLLM and demonstrating its competitive performance, particularly given the dominance of autoregressive models in this space. Previous diffusion-based MLLMs have often relied on autoregressive models for language modeling or used discrete diffusion approaches with limited language capabilities. The authors directly tackle this limitation by building on their existing LLaDA model.
*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. It challenges the prevailing paradigm in MLLMs and shows that diffusion models can be a viable alternative. The data scalability results are interesting, suggesting a potential advantage of diffusion models in leveraging large datasets. The performance compared to hybrid autoregressive-diffusion models is noteworthy.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the approach and experimental setup.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The authors evaluate LLaDA-V on a diverse set of benchmarks, providing a comprehensive assessment of its capabilities.
    *   **Interesting findings:** The data scalability and attention mechanism ablation studies reveal interesting insights into the behavior of diffusion-based MLLMs.
    *   **Open-source:** Providing the code and project page helps ensure reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Language tower limitations:** The language tower (LLaDA) is acknowledged to be weaker than those used in state-of-the-art autoregressive models (e.g., Qwen2-7B), which potentially limits the overall performance of LLaDA-V. The comparable results on some tasks suggest that, with a stronger language model backbone, LLaDA-V could potentially achieve even greater performance gains.
    *   **Incremental improvement:** While showing superior data scalability, it might be arguable that LLaDA-V is a direct evolution of LLaDA.
    *   **Narrower scope:** While challenging autoregressive models directly, it could have presented or discussed broader perspectives, such as the cost of computational complexity and stability of diffusion-based MLLMs.

* **Potential Influence:** The work can encourage exploration of diffusion models for multimodal tasks, inspiring improvements in diffusion-based language models. It may also provide insights into the architectural design and training strategies for effective diffusion-based MLLMs.

Considering these points, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field, but it is not groundbreaking. It is well-executed and demonstrates promising results, but the weaker language model backbone and potential limitations of scope restrain its impact.

- **Score**: 6/10

### In-Context Watermarks for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16934v1)
- **Authors**: Yepeng Liu, Xuandong Zhao, Christopher Kruegel, Dawn Song, Yuheng Bu
- **Abstract**: The growing use of large language models (LLMs) for sensitive applications has highlighted the need for effective watermarking techniques to ensure the provenance and accountability of AI-generated text. However, most existing watermarking methods require access to the decoding process, limiting their applicability in real-world settings. One illustrative example is the use of LLMs by dishonest reviewers in the context of academic peer review, where conference organizers have no access to the model used but still need to detect AI-generated reviews. Motivated by this gap, we introduce In-Context Watermarking (ICW), which embeds watermarks into generated text solely through prompt engineering, leveraging LLMs' in-context learning and instruction-following abilities. We investigate four ICW strategies at different levels of granularity, each paired with a tailored detection method. We further examine the Indirect Prompt Injection (IPI) setting as a specific case study, in which watermarking is covertly triggered by modifying input documents such as academic manuscripts. Our experiments validate the feasibility of ICW as a model-agnostic, practical watermarking approach. Moreover, our findings suggest that as LLMs become more capable, ICW offers a promising direction for scalable and accessible content attribution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "In-Context Watermarks for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces In-Context Watermarking (ICW), a novel method for watermarking text generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) that doesn't require access to the LLM's decoding process. Instead, it leverages the LLM's in-context learning and instruction-following abilities by embedding watermarks into the generated text through prompt engineering. The authors explore four ICW strategies at varying granularities (Unicode, Initials, Lexical, Acrostics) and pair each with a tailored detection method. They also investigate the application of ICW in an Indirect Prompt Injection (IPI) setting, where watermarking is covertly triggered by modifying input documents. The paper presents experimental results validating the feasibility of ICW as a practical, model-agnostic watermarking approach and suggests its increasing potential as LLMs become more capable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to watermarking LLM-generated text. The key innovation lies in the use of in-context learning to embed watermarks without direct access to the LLM's internals, which is a significant departure from existing methods that typically require modifying the decoding process. The exploration of different ICW strategies and their application in the IPI setting also adds to the novelty of the work.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in the field of LLM security and accountability. By offering a model-agnostic watermarking approach, ICW has the potential to be applied in a wider range of scenarios where access to the LLM's internals is restricted. The demonstration of ICW's applicability in the IPI setting, particularly in the context of detecting AI-generated reviews in academic peer review, highlights its practical relevance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Model-agnostic:** ICW doesn't require access to the LLM's decoding process, making it applicable in scenarios where this access is restricted.
    *   **Practical and accessible:** ICW leverages prompt engineering, making it relatively easy to implement and deploy.
    *   **Versatile:** The paper explores different ICW strategies, each with its own trade-offs, offering flexibility in terms of LLM requirements, detectability, robustness, and text quality.
    *   **Relevant application:** The IPI setting demonstrates the practical utility of ICW in a real-world scenario, such as detecting AI-generated reviews.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM dependency:** The effectiveness of ICW heavily relies on the LLM's in-context learning and instruction-following abilities. The paper acknowledges that the performance of some ICW strategies is limited by the capabilities of certain LLMs.
    *   **Simplicity of instructions:** The paper notes that existing watermarking instructions are relatively simple. Further exploration of advanced prompt engineering techniques could improve the performance of ICW.
    *   **Vulnerability to attack:** The paper acknowledges the potential vulnerability of ICW to adaptive attacks where adversaries attempt to identify and remove watermarks. Further investigation of attack and defense methods is warranted.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings suggest that ICW offers a promising direction for scalable and accessible content attribution. As LLMs continue to advance and in-context learning becomes more sophisticated, ICW is likely to gain increasing attention as a practical and effective watermarking approach. The paper could stimulate further research in areas such as advanced prompt engineering for ICW, robust detection methods, and defense strategies against adaptive attacks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant approach to watermarking LLM-generated text that is both practical and versatile. The demonstration of ICW's feasibility and applicability in the IPI setting is particularly compelling. However, the paper's limitations, including its dependency on LLM capabilities, simplicity of instructions, and vulnerability to attack, prevent it from receiving a higher score. While ICW shows promise as a model-agnostic watermarking solution, further research is needed to address these limitations and fully realize its potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AGENTIF: Benchmarking Instruction Following of Large Language Models in Agentic Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16944v1)
- **Authors**: Yunjia Qi, Hao Peng, Xiaozhi Wang, Amy Xin, Youfeng Liu, Bin Xu, Lei Hou, Juanzi Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated advanced capabilities in real-world agentic applications. Growing research efforts aim to develop LLM-based agents to address practical demands, introducing a new challenge: agentic scenarios often involve lengthy instructions with complex constraints, such as extended system prompts and detailed tool specifications. While adherence to such instructions is crucial for agentic applications, whether LLMs can reliably follow them remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce AgentIF, the first benchmark for systematically evaluating LLM instruction following ability in agentic scenarios. AgentIF features three key characteristics: (1) Realistic, constructed from 50 real-world agentic applications. (2) Long, averaging 1,723 words with a maximum of 15,630 words. (3) Complex, averaging 11.9 constraints per instruction, covering diverse constraint types, such as tool specifications and condition constraints. To construct AgentIF, we collect 707 human-annotated instructions across 50 agentic tasks from industrial application agents and open-source agentic systems. For each instruction, we annotate the associated constraints and corresponding evaluation metrics, including code-based evaluation, LLM-based evaluation, and hybrid code-LLM evaluation. We use AgentIF to systematically evaluate existing advanced LLMs. We observe that current models generally perform poorly, especially in handling complex constraint structures and tool specifications. We further conduct error analysis and analytical experiments on instruction length and meta constraints, providing some findings about the failure modes of existing LLMs. We have released the code and data to facilitate future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AGENTIF: Benchmarking Instruction Following of Large Language Models in Agentic Scenarios":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AGENTIF, a new benchmark for evaluating the instruction-following capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) specifically within agentic scenarios. The key characteristics of AGENTIF are: (1) it's realistic, based on 50 real-world agentic applications; (2) instructions are long (average 1723 words); and (3) the instructions are complex, averaging nearly 12 constraints per instruction, covering diverse types like tool specifications and condition constraints. The dataset was constructed using human-annotated instructions collected from industrial and open-source agentic systems. The paper also includes annotations for associated constraints and evaluation metrics (code-based, LLM-based, hybrid). The authors use AGENTIF to systematically evaluate various existing LLMs and conduct error analysis to identify failure modes related to instruction length and meta-constraints. The paper releases the code and data for future research. The experiments demonstrate that current LLMs struggle with complex constraints, especially with tool specifications, indicating room for further improvement in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in addressing a critical gap in evaluating LLMs' instruction-following capabilities within the context of *agentic* scenarios.  While existing benchmarks exist, they often fall short in capturing the length, complexity, and real-world relevance of instructions found in agentic applications.  The focus on tool specifications and conditional constraints is particularly valuable. The classification of 'meta constraints' is a new contribution that highlights the priority-related issues when following complex instructions.
*   **Significance:** The significance of AGENTIF is considerable. Agentic applications are a growing area of LLM deployment, and reliable instruction following is *essential* for their success. The benchmark provides a much-needed tool for:
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** Assessing the suitability of different LLMs for agentic tasks.
    *   **Identifying weaknesses:** Pinpointing specific areas where LLMs struggle (e.g., tool usage, condition constraints).
    *   **Guiding future research:** Highlighting the need for improved architectures, training methods, or prompting strategies to enhance instruction following in complex, real-world settings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Realistic Dataset:** The use of instructions from actual agentic applications significantly increases the benchmark's practical value.
    *   **Comprehensive Annotation:** The meticulous annotation of constraints, types, and evaluation metrics adds considerable value and facilitates in-depth analysis.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides valuable insights into the specific failure modes of LLMs, which is crucial for guiding future improvements.
    *   **Benchmark Coverage**:  AgentIF provides a more holistic constraint set for LLMs to follow as compared to previous research, and includes real-world complexities involved in LLM applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope (Languages):** As the authors acknowledge, the focus on Chinese and English might limit the benchmark's widespread use and generalizability, and might limit its widespread use.
    *   **Scale and Automation:** The data collection and annotation process, while rigorous, requires significant manual effort. The authors suggest expanding this effort in future work.
    *   **Limited Model Diversity (Tested)**:  The evaluation could be more extensive and cover a wider range of models and instruction-tuning approaches.
    *   **Static Dataset**: The agentic landscape changes rapidly. The AGENTIF dataset will need to be periodically updated and re-evaluated to maintain its relevance and representativeness.
*   **Impact:** The benchmark will likely have a significant impact by providing a more challenging and realistic evaluation setting for LLMs intended for agentic use.  It will help researchers focus their efforts on addressing the specific weaknesses of LLMs in this domain, potentially leading to more reliable and effective agents.

**Overall:**

AGENTIF is a valuable and significant contribution to the field of LLM evaluation, with its novel focus on agentic scenarios, realistic data, and comprehensive annotation. While there are some areas for improvement, the benchmark is poised to become a standard tool for researchers working on developing LLMs for real-world agentic applications. The rigorous analysis and clear identification of failure modes will serve as a crucial guide for future research directions.

### MixAT: Combining Continuous and Discrete Adversarial Training for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16947v1)
- **Authors**: Csaba Dékány, Stefan Balauca, Robin Staab, Dimitar I. Dimitrov, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: Despite recent efforts in Large Language Models (LLMs) safety and alignment, current adversarial attacks on frontier LLMs are still able to force harmful generations consistently. Although adversarial training has been widely studied and shown to significantly improve the robustness of traditional machine learning models, its strengths and weaknesses in the context of LLMs are less understood. Specifically, while existing discrete adversarial attacks are effective at producing harmful content, training LLMs with concrete adversarial prompts is often computationally expensive, leading to reliance on continuous relaxations. As these relaxations do not correspond to discrete input tokens, such latent training methods often leave models vulnerable to a diverse set of discrete attacks. In this work, we aim to bridge this gap by introducing MixAT, a novel method that combines stronger discrete and faster continuous attacks during training. We rigorously evaluate MixAT across a wide spectrum of state-of-the-art attacks, proposing the At Least One Attack Success Rate (ALO-ASR) metric to capture the worst-case vulnerability of models. We show MixAT achieves substantially better robustness (ALO-ASR < 20%) compared to prior defenses (ALO-ASR > 50%), while maintaining a runtime comparable to methods based on continuous relaxations. We further analyze MixAT in realistic deployment settings, exploring how chat templates, quantization, low-rank adapters, and temperature affect both adversarial training and evaluation, revealing additional blind spots in current methodologies. Our results demonstrate that MixAT's discrete-continuous defense offers a principled and superior robustness-accuracy tradeoff with minimal computational overhead, highlighting its promise for building safer LLMs. We provide our code and models at https://github.com/insait-institute/MixAT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MIXAT, a novel adversarial training method for Large Language Models (LLMs) that combines continuous and discrete adversarial attacks.  It addresses the limitations of existing adversarial training approaches, which either rely on computationally expensive discrete attacks or cheaper continuous relaxations that leave models vulnerable to diverse discrete attacks. MIXAT leverages the efficiency of continuous attacks applied on top of discrete adversarial inputs. The authors evaluate MIXAT across various attacks, using a new metric, "At Least One Attack Success Rate" (ALO-ASR), to measure worst-case vulnerability. Experiments show that MIXAT achieves better robustness compared to previous defenses while maintaining comparable runtime. The paper also investigates the impact of chat templates, quantization, LoRA, and temperature on adversarial training and evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining continuous and discrete adversarial training is a reasonable and incremental step. Continuous relaxations for adversarial training are computationally effective but may not capture all possible discrete adversarial examples. Performing continuous perturbations on top of discrete adversarial inputs is sensible. The introduction of the ALO-ASR metric is useful for capturing worst-case vulnerabilities, acknowledging the limitations of relying on individual attack success rates.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that MIXAT achieves significantly better robustness than previous methods (e.g., lower ALO-ASR). The exploration of factors like chat templates and quantization is practical and addresses a gap in the existing literature, contributing to a more realistic evaluation of adversarial robustness in LLMs. Showing generalizability across various model sizes and architectures further increases the significance. The release of code and models facilitates reproducibility and further research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies a weakness in current adversarial training methods.
    *   The proposed MIXAT method provides a practical solution and a compelling trade-off between robustness and computational cost.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough and uses a comprehensive set of attacks and datasets.
    *   The ALO-ASR metric is a valuable addition.
    *   The analysis of practical factors like chat templates and quantization strengthens the paper's relevance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the core idea is logical, it isn't a radical breakthrough. The improvement is incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   The gains against GCG attacks are not as substantial as with other attack types, showing that it might not be universally effective. While the authors experiment with incorporating GCG samples, the increase in training time is a significant drawback.
    *   The claims about the Llama3 LAT results are confusing since they couldn't reproduce it.
    *   The limitations section mentions randomness, dataset inadequacy, but lacks a deeper theoretical analysis.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a stronger baseline for adversarial training of LLMs. The insights regarding practical deployment factors are valuable, and the code release will encourage adoption and further research.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a solid, incremental improvement that makes a relevant contribution to adversarial training of LLMs. It is well-motivated, thoroughly evaluated, and practically relevant. While the central idea is not ground-breaking, it represents a worthwhile advancement.

### Bottlenecked Transformers: Periodic KV Cache Abstraction for Generalised Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16950v1)
- **Authors**: Adnan Oomerjee, Zafeirios Fountas, Zhongwei Yu, Haitham Bou-Ammar, Jun Wang
- **Abstract**: Despite their impressive capabilities, Large Language Models struggle with generalisation beyond their training distribution, often exhibiting sophisticated pattern interpolation rather than true abstract reasoning (extrapolation). In this work, we approach this limitation through the lens of Information Bottleneck (IB) theory, which posits that model generalisation emerges from an optimal balance between input compression and retention of predictive information in latent representations. We prove using IB theory that decoder-only Transformers are inherently constrained in their ability to form task-optimal sequence representations. We then use this result to demonstrate that periodic global transformation of the internal sequence-level representations (KV cache) is a necessary computational step for improving Transformer generalisation in reasoning tasks. Based on these theoretical insights, we propose a modification to the Transformer architecture, in the form of an additional module that globally rewrites the KV cache at periodic intervals, shifting its capacity away from memorising input prefixes and toward encoding features most useful for predicting future tokens. Our model delivers substantial gains on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, outperforming both vanilla Transformers with up to 3.5x more parameters, as well as heuristic-driven pruning mechanisms for cache compression. Our approach can be seen as a principled generalisation of existing KV-cache compression methods; whereas such methods focus solely on compressing input representations, they often do so at the expense of retaining predictive information, and thus their capabilities are inherently bounded by those of an unconstrained model. This establishes a principled framework to manipulate Transformer memory using information theory, addressing fundamental reasoning limitations that scaling alone cannot overcome.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Bottlenecked Transformer" architecture designed to improve the generalization capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in reasoning tasks. The authors address the tendency of standard Transformers to memorize patterns rather than perform abstract reasoning by applying information bottleneck (IB) principles.  They theoretically demonstrate that decoder-only Transformers have inherent limitations in forming task-optimal representations due to the need to reconstruct input prefixes in addition to predicting future tokens. The core contribution is a Cache Processor module that periodically rewrites the KV cache, forcing the model to compress input representations and retain only the information most relevant for predicting future tokens. This approach improves performance on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, outperforming vanilla Transformers with significantly more parameters and heuristic cache compression methods. The paper frames this modification as a principled generalization of existing KV-cache compression techniques and positions it as a way to address fundamental reasoning limits beyond what scaling alone can achieve.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of applying information bottleneck principles to Transformer architectures to improve generalization is not entirely new. Existing work has explored IB-inspired token merging, variational IB, and downsampling techniques. However, the paper's **novelty lies in its theoretical analysis focusing on the KV-cache** as a critical bottleneck in decoder-only Transformers and its proposed **Cache Processor module** that directly rewrites the entire cache at periodic intervals. The architecture provides a mechanism to globally transform internal representations, as opposed to local token-wise compression methods.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of current LLMs: their struggles with extrapolation and genuine reasoning. While scaling models and increasing training data have led to impressive capabilities, they haven't fully solved the problem of abstract reasoning. The Bottlenecked Transformer offers a **principled architectural modification** that potentially tackles this limitation by forcing the model to focus on task-relevant information. The **empirical results are compelling**, demonstrating substantial performance gains over strong baselines on challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks, including improvements in out-of-distribution performance. This suggests a more robust generalization capability. Also, the theoretical justification using IB provides a good frame for future exploration of Transformer architectures.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a solid theoretical foundation based on information bottleneck principles, offering a formal argument for why standard training objectives hinder generalization in decoder-only Transformers. The proofs connecting the KV-cache to sequence-to-sequence bottlenecks are valuable.
    *   **Architectural Innovation:** The Cache Processor represents an interesting architectural design choice. Rewriting the entire KV cache at periodic intervals provides a simple yet effective mechanism for information filtering and compression. It establishes an architectural equivalent for advantages of RNNs while preserving the advantages of Transformers.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks are convincing.  The Bottlenecked Transformer consistently outperforms vanilla Transformers and heuristic cache compression methods. The increased OOD performance is especially significant.
    *   **Clear Framing:** The paper effectively positions the Bottlenecked Transformer as a principled generalization of existing KV-cache compression techniques.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experimental evaluation is limited to three synthetic mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are useful for controlled analysis, it's unclear how well the approach will generalize to more complex, real-world reasoning problems.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The choice of cache rewrite frequency (B) and other hyperparameters related to the Cache Processor might be critical to performance, and the paper does not extensively explore this sensitivity.  The authors suggest additional parameters for the Cache Processor might lead to better outcomes.
    *   **Computational Overhead:**  The Cache Processor adds computational overhead to the Transformer architecture, which may become a significant bottleneck for long sequences or large-scale models, as suggested by the authors.
    *   **Limited Comparison:**  Although the model compares to H2O, comparisons to the more recent approaches to sparsity and efficient attention (e.g. sparse transformers or attention variants) would strengthen the contribution.
*   **Potential Impact:** If the Bottlenecked Transformer approach proves effective in a broader range of reasoning tasks and can be efficiently scaled, it has the potential to significantly impact the development of more robust and generalizable LLMs. This is particularly relevant for tasks that require abstract reasoning and extrapolation beyond the training distribution.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and theoretically grounded approach to address a fundamental limitation of LLMs. The empirical results are strong and suggest a significant improvement in generalization capabilities on mathematical reasoning tasks. The work offers a valuable contribution to the field by providing both a theoretical framework and a practical architecture that goes beyond mere scaling. While further validation on more complex tasks and exploration of the computational aspects are needed, the paper's innovative approach and promising results justify a strong rating.

### Invisible Prompts, Visible Threats: Malicious Font Injection in External Resources for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16957v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Xiong, Changjia Zhu, Shuhang Lin, Chong Zhang, Yongfeng Zhang, Yao Liu, Lingyao Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly equipped with capabilities of real-time web search and integrated with protocols like Model Context Protocol (MCP). This extension could introduce new security vulnerabilities. We present a systematic investigation of LLM vulnerabilities to hidden adversarial prompts through malicious font injection in external resources like webpages, where attackers manipulate code-to-glyph mapping to inject deceptive content which are invisible to users. We evaluate two critical attack scenarios: (1) "malicious content relay" and (2) "sensitive data leakage" through MCP-enabled tools. Our experiments reveal that indirect prompts with injected malicious font can bypass LLM safety mechanisms through external resources, achieving varying success rates based on data sensitivity and prompt design. Our research underscores the urgent need for enhanced security measures in LLM deployments when processing external content.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Invisible Prompts, Visible Threats: Malicious Font Injection in External Resources for Large Language Models" investigates a new vulnerability in LLMs related to processing external resources such as webpages and documents. The authors demonstrate that attackers can inject malicious prompts into these resources by manipulating font code, making the prompts invisible to humans but still readable by LLMs. The paper explores two attack scenarios: malicious content relay (spreading harmful content) and sensitive data leakage (exfiltrating user data via MCP-enabled tools). They find that these attacks can be successful, depending on factors like prompt design, document format, injection frequency, and prompt placement. Newer LLMs, despite their advancements, show more vulnerability to this type of attack.  Indirect prompts are more effective at bypassing safety mechanisms. The research highlights the need for better security measures to handle visually deceptive content.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its exploration of malicious font injection as a specific attack vector against LLMs interacting with external resources, particularly in the context of MCP. While prompt injection is a known problem, the use of font manipulation to create visually hidden prompts is a unique and practical approach. The attack's stealthiness adds a new dimension to the existing prompt injection landscape.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they expose a previously under-appreciated vulnerability that can be easily exploited and is difficult to detect with existing security measures. The demonstrated scenarios of malicious content relay and data leakage are concerning and have real-world implications. The paper contributes to the understanding of how LLMs process and interpret external content, and highlights the limitations of current safety mechanisms. The paper's focus on the impact with the MCP protocol in particular is very relevant given the growing interest in LLM agents and autonomous tool use.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined attack scenarios and a systematic experimental setup.
    *   Clear methodology and thorough analysis of influencing factors.
    *   Demonstration of successful attacks across multiple LLM models.
    *   Identification of gaps in current LLM security measures and suggestions for improvement.
    *   Emphasizes the importance of visual deceptive content and how it can be exploited.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study is limited primarily to English language content.
    *   The experimental setup relies on synthetic data, which might not fully reflect real-world scenarios.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on attack success rates, while the stealthiness and detectability aspects of the attacks could be further explored.
    *   The study might benefit from a more in-depth discussion of potential defense mechanisms beyond generic "enhanced security measures."
    *   Some of the vulnerabilities may be addressed in later iterations of the examined LLMs.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by raising awareness about this specific vulnerability and motivating the development of more robust security frameworks for LLMs.  It could lead to research on new detection and mitigation techniques that focus on visually deceptive content and content integrity.  Given the increasing reliance on LLMs in various applications, this research is timely and relevant.

* **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The paper combines novelty (font injection as a specific prompt injection vector), practical attack scenarios, and a systematic methodology. The vulnerability discovered is relatively easy to exploit, as demonstrated in the paper, and bypasses the existing safety checks of LLMs. However, the study's reliance on synthetic data and the limitations in evaluating the stealthiness aspects slightly reduce the overall impact. More importantly, while font injection is technically interesting, it is a somewhat limited attack vector, especially when compared to other more sophisticated methods of prompt injection. Also, some findings might become less relevant as LLM providers roll out security updates.

### Bigger Isn't Always Memorizing: Early Stopping Overparameterized Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16959v1)
- **Authors**: Alessandro Favero, Antonio Sclocchi, Matthieu Wyart
- **Abstract**: Diffusion probabilistic models have become a cornerstone of modern generative AI, yet the mechanisms underlying their generalization remain poorly understood. In fact, if these models were perfectly minimizing their training loss, they would just generate data belonging to their training set, i.e., memorize, as empirically found in the overparameterized regime. We revisit this view by showing that, in highly overparameterized diffusion models, generalization in natural data domains is progressively achieved during training before the onset of memorization. Our results, ranging from image to language diffusion models, systematically support the empirical law that memorization time is proportional to the dataset size. Generalization vs. memorization is then best understood as a competition between time scales. We show that this phenomenology is recovered in diffusion models learning a simple probabilistic context-free grammar with random rules, where generalization corresponds to the hierarchical acquisition of deeper grammar rules as training time grows, and the generalization cost of early stopping can be characterized. We summarize these results in a phase diagram. Overall, our results support that a principled early-stopping criterion - scaling with dataset size - can effectively optimize generalization while avoiding memorization, with direct implications for hyperparameter transfer and privacy-sensitive applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Bigger Isn't Always Memorizing: Early Stopping Overparameterized Diffusion Models" challenges the conventional view that generalization in diffusion models is solely dependent on underparameterization.  The authors demonstrate empirically that highly overparameterized diffusion models exhibit a phase of generalization during training *before* the onset of memorization. They find that the time at which memorization begins scales linearly with the dataset size. This relationship is observed across diverse datasets (images and text) and diffusion model architectures (iDDPM, Stable Diffusion, MD4, D3PM). The paper further investigates this phenomenon using a controlled context-free grammar model, showing that generalization corresponds to learning deeper grammar rules before memorizing the training data.  The authors propose that early stopping, guided by dataset size, can effectively optimize generalization while avoiding memorization, offering implications for hyperparameter transfer and privacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core finding—that generalization precedes memorization in overparameterized diffusion models and that memorization time scales with dataset size—is a significant contribution. It moves beyond the established notion that underparameterization is the sole driver of generalization. The use of a synthetic grammar model to dissect generalization dynamics provides valuable insights. The findings, while grounded in the behavior of diffusion models, have potential implications for other deep learning architectures as well, where generalization and memorization trade-offs are a central concern.
*   **Significance:** The implications of this work are substantial. The identification of an early generalization phase and its scaling with dataset size opens avenues for better training strategies, early stopping criteria, and hyperparameter transfer. This directly addresses practical issues such as privacy concerns and the reduction of computational costs associated with training diffusion models to full convergence. This is especially important given the increasing size of datasets used to train generative models. It also proposes a better understanding of the dynamics within these models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Support:** The findings are supported by experiments across multiple datasets (CIFAR-10, CelebA, text8, LAION) and diffusion model architectures.
    *   **Analytical Model:** The context-free grammar model provides a useful framework for understanding the mechanisms of generalization and memorization, offering more control and interpretability.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper explicitly addresses the practical relevance of the findings by discussing early stopping and dataset-size-aware training.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Linear Scaling Assumption:** While the experiments show a linear scaling of memorization time with dataset size, further exploration is needed to determine the limits and generalizability of this observation.  For instance, it's plausible that this relationship might break down at much larger dataset sizes or with drastically different data distributions.
    *   **Simplification of the Synthetic Data Model:** While the Random Hierarchy Model (RHM) is a valuable tool for analysis, its simplistic nature compared to the complexities of real-world data may limit the direct applicability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The theoretical insights are largely interpretive and descriptive. A more rigorous theoretical analysis explaining why this generalization-before-memorization phenomenon occurs and how to predict the onset of memorization more accurately would strengthen the paper.
*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence the design and training of diffusion models, specifically around early stopping techniques and dataset-aware training protocols. The insights into the generalization-memorization trade-off are also valuable for understanding the behavior of other generative models and deep learning architectures.
    *   **Potential Follow-Up Research:** It opens avenues for research into more sophisticated early stopping algorithms and techniques for better hyperparameter transfer that are cognizant of dataset size and structure. Exploration of the generalization-memorization dynamics in other generative architectures is also possible.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the findings, the strong empirical support, and the practical implications for training diffusion models and related generative architectures, while acknowledging the limitations in theoretical depth and the idealization of the synthetic model, I believe a score of **8** is appropriate.
